3
Abstract
This thesis presents novel algebraic and geometric approaches to the classification problem of coboundary
Lie bialgebras up to Lie algebra automorphisms. This entails the analysis of modified and classical Yang-
Baxter equations and other related mathematical structures. More specifically, we develop Grassmann,
graded algebra, and algebraic invariant techniques for the classification problem of coboundary Lie bial-
gebras and the so-called r-matrices. The devised techniques are mainly focused on the study of r-matrices
for three-dimensional and indecomposable four-dimensional Lie algebras. An example of a decomposable
four-dimensional Lie algebra, namely gl2, is also considered. Other particular higher-dimensional Lie
bialgebras, e.g. so(2, 2) and so(3, 2), are partially studied. Special relevance has the development of a
new notion: the Darboux families, which provide a powerful method for the classification and determina-
tion of classes of r-matrices. In fact, the classification of r-matrices for four-dimensional indecomposable
Lie bialgebras is a significative advance relative to previous results in the literature as the classification
problem of Lie bialgebras has been predominantly treated algebraically in the literature. Meanwhile, we
address the problem in a more geometric manner.
Moreover, the theory of Lie bialgebras and related notions are employed in the study of Lie systems,
their generalisations, and Hamiltonian systems. More specifically, we study the use of r-matrices for the
description of interesting Hamiltonian systems relative to symplectic and Poisson structures. Additionally,
we extend the construction of integrable deformations of Lie–Hamilton systems on symplectic manifolds
to Jacobi manifolds.
The outlook of this doctoral thesis suggests some further research directions, both in the abstract classi-
fication problem and the mathematical-physics applications. Appendices present a new practical method
to obtain matrix representation for Lie algebras with nontrivial centre used throughout the work and the
code written in Mathematica that has been used to facilitate some of the computations for coboundary
Lie bialgebras classifications.
Keywords:
Classical Yang–Baxter equation, Darboux family, deformation of Lie–Hamilton system, foliated Lie
system, invariant form, Jacobi manifold, Lie bialgebra, modified Yang–Baxter equation, r-matrix

Streszczenie
Niniejsza rozprawa prezentuje nowe algebraiczne i geometryczne podejścia do problemu klasyfikacji ko-
brzegowych bialgebr Liego z dokładnością do automorfizmów algebr Liego.
Analizowane są również
zmodyfikowane i klasyczne równania Yanga–Baxtera oraz powiązane struktury matematyczne. W szczegól-
ności, rozwijamy techniki oparte na zastosowaniu algebr Grassmanna, algebr gradowanych oraz alge-
braicznych niezmienników w celu klasyfikacji kobrzegowych bialgebr Liego oraz tak zwanych r-macierzy.
Skupiamy się przede wszystkim na badaniu r-macierzy dla trójwymiarowych oraz nierozkładalnych cztero-
wymiarowych algebr Liego. Rozważamy też czterowymiarowy przykład rozkładalnej algebry Liego, gl2.
Inne wyżejwymiarowe przypadki, m.in.
so(2, 2) oraz so(3, 2), są częściowo analizowane.
Szczególne
znaczenie ma nowe pojęcie wprowadzone i rozwijane w pracy, rodziny Darboux, które dostarcza wyjątkowo
użytecznej metody wyznaczania i klasyfikacji klas r-macierzy. Analiza r-macierzy przeprowadzona dla
czterowymiarowych nierozkładalnych algebr Liego jest jednym z niewielu geometrycznych ujęć problemu
klasyfikacji bialgebr Liego i stanowi istotny postęp względem wcześniejszych prac, które oferowały głównie
algebraiczne podejścia.
Ponadto, stosujemy pojęcia znane z teorii bialgebr Liego do badania układów Liego, ich uogólnień oraz
układów hamiltonowskich.
Przyglądamy się szczególnie zastosowaniom r-macierzy w opisie układów
hamiltonowskich wględem struktur symplektycznych i poissonowskich.
Dodatkowo, rozszerzamy kon-
strukcję całkowalnych deformacji układów Liego–Hamiltona na rozmaitościach symplektycznych na roz-
maitości Jacobiego.
W Podsumowaniu pracy nakreślamy możliwe kierunki dalszych badań, zarówno w abstrakcyjnym prob-
lemie klasyfikacyjnym, jak i zastosowaniach w fizyce matematycznej. W Dodatkach opisujemy praktyczną
metodę otrzymywania macierzowej reprezentacji algebr Liego posiadających nietrywialne centrum, uży-
waną w całej pracy, oraz prezentujemy kod napisany w programie Mathematica, który był stosowany do
wykonania rachunków niezbędnych podczas klasyfikowania kobrzegowych bialgebr Liego.
Słowa kluczowe
Bialgebra Liego, deformacja układu Liego–Hamiltona, foliacja układów Liego, forma niezmiennicza,
klasyczne równanie Yanga–Baxtera, zmodyfikowane równanie Yanga–Baxtera, r-macierz, rodzina
Darboux, rozmaitość Jacobiego
Tytuł pracy w języku polskim
Geometryczne podejścia do bialgebr Liego, ich klasyfikacji oraz zastosowań

ration (2023).

Introduction
It has been a decade-long journey for the notion of Lie bialgebra to appear on the mathematical landscape
and become a topic of separate studies. As for many modern concepts, its beginnings are tightly related
with the intense development of physical theories in the previous century and can be traced back to the
research on exactly solvable models in quantum mechanics by Yang [185, 186] and statistical mechanics
by Baxter [31, 32]. In these independent work, the same type of equation, called later the quantum Yang–
Baxter equation (QYBE), has been analysed. Further contributions in the study of QYBE has come from
the researchers of the Leningrad Mathematical School. L. Faddeev and Sklyanin generalised the so-called
inverse scattering method [39, 81], an effective tool for analysing nonlinear partial differential equations,
in order to address the integrability problems of Yang and Baxter [77, 78, 79, 80, 163]. The relation
between these methods and their underlying geometric structures have been discussed extensively by
Drinfeld. These endeavours are considered the origin of the theory of Hopf algebras and quantum groups
(see [58, 67, 127]). It provided mathematical foundations for prior findings in the field. In particular,
the description of r-matrices and the classical Yang–Baxter equation, which are crucial objects in the
inverse scattering method, has been given a simple mathematical formulation. In this framework, an
r-matrix r is just an element of the tensor product g⊗2 for a Lie algebra (g, [·, ·]g) satisfying the classical
Yang–Baxter equation of the form
[ai, aj] ⊗bi ⊗bj + ai ⊗[bi, aj] ⊗bj + ai ⊗aj ⊗[bi, bj] = 0
where one uses the decomposition r = P
i ai ⊗bi, ai, bi ∈g. Moreover, it could be understood as a
special case of a new structure, a Lie bialgebra, that is a pair of Lie algebra structures on g and g∗with
a suitable compatibility condition [58].
Since the report [67] presented by Drinfeld in 1986 at the International Mathematical Symposium, the
research on the topic has experienced noticeable growth. Particularly influential work has been done
on the quantisation problem of Lie bialgebras [71, 74, 153]. Alongside these mathematical inquiries on
quantisation, physical applications of Lie bialgebras have been developed, especially the study of quantum
spacetimes [16, 17, 18, 20, 21, 23, 24, 131, 132], field-theoretical models [43, 45] and deformations of
classical systems [11, 13, 15, 28].
A separate line of research concerned the study of solutions and
geometry of CYBE [7, 65, 90, 128, 162, 181] and QYBE [61, 70, 91]. Both notions also admitted interesting
generalisations [8, 9, 64, 75, 76].
The systematic analysis of solutions to CYBE remains until now the most basic problem. In [35, 36, 66],
Drinfeld classified the solutions to the classical Yang–Baxter equation in case of the complex semisimple
Lie algebra and has made many relevant observations on the structure of the solutions to CYBE for the
Lie algebra g[u] associated with the complex semisimple g. It was the first result that addressed the
classification problem for r-matrices. Since then, further research in this direction was relatively scarce,
as the problem is commonly considered hard. However, several noticeable advancements have been made
over last forty years on this issue.
9

10
Chapter 0. Introduction
By direct generalisation of the method presented in [66], Andruskiewitsch obtained the classification of
r-matrices for real absolutely simple Lie algebras [4]. Another insightful approach is due to A. Stolin. In
[168, 169], he continued the initial study of Drinfeld on solutions over g[u] and obtained the classification
of so-called constant and rational solutions for sln, n > 1. He also proved that this problem is equivalent
to the analysis of Frobenius subalgebras of g. In [147], he extended the previous analysis and by cohomo-
logical means, classified all bialgebra structures on sl(n, K), where K is an arbitrary field of characteristic
zero. Recently, S. Maximov et al. obtained the classification of Lie bialgebra structures over g[u], where
g is any simple Lie algebra over a field of characteristic zero [2].
Apart from already mentioned structural considerations on the classification problem, there has been
several more direct algebraic approaches to the problem. Full classification of three-dimensional real Lie
bialgebras has been obtained by Gomez [95] and Farinati [84], whereas the four-dimensional case has only
been partially studied [1, 42]. A few cases of particular structure have also been analysed [83, 82]. The
problem has also been discussed in equivalent formulations (see [62] on the classification of Manin pairs
or [101] on the classification of Drinfeld doubles). However, Lie bialgebra structures on Lie algebras of
physical importance has mostly been considered [12, 18, 19, 21, 22, 26, 42, 98, 126, 139, 141, 142, 179].
Outline of the thesis
This thesis presents a few novel algebraic and geometric approaches to the classification problem of
coboundary Lie bialgebras. In particular, we focus on the study of r-matrices for three-dimensional and
indecomposable four-dimensional Lie algebras. An additional example of a four-dimensional decompos-
able Lie algebra gl2 is also considered.
Chapter 1 is a survey of prerequisites that are necessary to understand the findings of this thesis. Basic
notions and fundamental results in differential geometry, Hopf algebra theory, Lie algebras and Lie systems
have been presented.
Chapter 2 is based on [123] and it presents the algebraic approach to the classification problem. Its
exposition notably clarifies and extends the content of the master thesis of the author [184].
Let g
hereafter stand for a Lie algebra and let End(V ) stand for the space of endomorphisms on a vector space
V . Section 2.1 discusses invariant maps on vector spaces. In particular, the construction of k-multilinear
g-invariant maps on ΛV and ΛmV , for the exterior algebra ΛV and the subspaces ΛmV of m-vectors, using
k-multilinear g-invariant map on V is detailed (Theorem 2.1.3). Moreover, we show that the induced map
on ΛmV is invariant relative to the Lie group GL(Λmρ) of the g-module ΛmV , namely the natural lifts to
ΛmV of the automorphisms on V induced by the exponentiation of the endomorphisms ρ(g). In Section
2.2, we apply these results to the maps induced on Λmg by the Killing form, called Killing-type forms. We
prove Killing-type forms are also invariant relative to the induced automorphism group GL(Λmf
ad). Some
efficient methods to determine such forms are presented in Section 2.3. In the next part, we focus on the
modified classical Yang-Baxter equation (mCYBE). Section 2.4 introduces a gradation on the Grassmann
algebra that simplifies the analysis of mCYBE solutions and provides an approximate geometric picture
of the mCYBE. In Section 2.5, several tools based on this gradation are presented, which allow us to
easily obtain g-invariant elements in Λg. We discuss how the study of mCYBE might be facilitated by
such elements. In Section 2.8, previous techniques are employed to compute inequivalent r-matrices for
all three-dimensional Lie algebras. As an illustration of further possibilities of our approach, r-matrices
for the four-dimensional Lie algebra gl2 are obtained in Section 2.9.
Chapter 3 is based on [124] and it presents an approach from the geometric standpoint. The main tool
is the so-called Darboux family, an analogue of a Darboux polynomial, which is carefully explained in

Chapter 0. Introduction
11
Section 3.1. Its relation to mCYBE and other geometric properties are discussed in Section 3.2. Finally,
the usefulness of this notion in the classification problem is illustrated in Sections 3.3 and 3.4 by computing
inequivalent r-matrices for all indecomposable four-dimensional Lie algebras and the decomposable Lie
algebra gl2.
Chapters 4 and 5 focus on using r-matrices to study differential equations and develop new applications.
Based on [54], Chapter 4 introduces the notion of stratified Lie systems and shows how Poisson structures
induced by r-matrices might be applied to obtain and to study Hamiltonian systems of this type. Chapter
5 presents some unpublished results [125]. We extend the construction of integrable deformations by
Ballesteros et al. [13, 15] to Jacobi and contact settings. Moreover, the properties and applications of
such generalisations are briefly discussed.
The outlook chapter suggests some further research directions, both in the abstract classification problem
and the applications in mathematical physics.
Appendix A presents the code written in Mathematica that has been used in order to facilitate the
computations necessary for the classification results presented in Chapter 2 and 3.
Appendix B discusses an effective method to obtain matrix representation for Lie algebras with nontrivial
centre. Such representations are necessary to carry out the computations in Chapter 3.

12
Chapter 0. Introduction

Chapter 1
Fundamentals
As already stressed in the Introduction, this thesis addresses the classification problem of Lie bialgebras
in a geometric manner. In consequence, presented methods require basic familiarity with certain well-
known results in differential geometry, Poisson geometry etc. This chapter is intended as a brief survey
of such prerequisites. We introduce the necessary geometric tools and algebraic notions to be used in
further chapters.
Unless explicitly stated otherwise, several general assumptions hold throughout this work. The set of
natural numbers N contains the zero element. All geometric objects are smooth and real. Manifolds
are finite-dimensional and connected. Moreover, all geometric structures are globally defined. Of course,
a more detailed treatment without previous assumptions is possible (especially it is believed that most
results could be extended to the complex case without much difficulties). However, we decided to focus
on presenting the main aspects of the theory in the simplest scenario and thus, we omit such minor
technical considerations.
Finally, for the tangent bundle TM of a manifold M, the space of its sections will be denoted by X(M).
Each element of X(M) is called a vector field on M.
1.1
Poisson geometry and Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket
A Poisson algebra over R is a real vector space V equipped with two bilinear maps • and {·, ·}, such that
(V, •) is a real algebra and (V, {·, ·}) forms a Lie algebra. Moreover, the Lie bracket {·, ·} satisfies the
Leibniz rule relative to •, i.e. {a, b • c} = {a, b} • c + b • {a, c} for any a, b, c ∈V .
The most relevant example of a Poisson algebra is the tensor algebra T(g) of a Lie algebra (g, [·, ·]g).
Recall that T(g) := L
p=0 T p(g), where T 0(g) := R and T k(g) := g⊗k for any k ≥0. it is an associative
algebra relative to the tensor product. The Lie bracket [·, ·]T (g) on T(g) is defined as follows. First,
[m, g]T (g) := 0 for m ∈T 0(g) and g ∈T(g). Next, we put [p, q]T (g) := [p, q]g for p, q ∈T 1(g). Then
recursively, we get the Lie bracket on the whole T(g) by imposing [a ⊗b, c]T (g) = [a, c]T (g) + a ⊗[b, c]T (g)
for a, b, c ∈T(g).
The Poisson algebra structure is inherited by the universal enveloping algebra, that is the quotient
U(g) := T(g)/I, where I is the ideal generated by the elements a ⊗b −b ⊗a −[a, b]g, where a, b ∈g.
Another crucial example is the algebra F(M) of real smooth functions on a symplectic manifold M. In
short, a symplectic manifold is a manifold M equipped with a nondegenerate, closed 2-form ω ∈Ω2(M).
Given any function f ∈C∞(M), there exists an associated unique vector field Xf ∈X(M), called
13

14
Chapter 1. Fundamentals
Hamiltonian vector field, satisfying ιXF ω = −df (see [3] for details). Then for any f, g ∈C∞(M), the
map {f, g} := ω(Xf, Xg) together with the pointwise multiplication of functions gives the sought Poisson
algebra structure on C∞(M).
The above case motivates a slightly general notion of a Poisson manifold. A Poisson manifold is a manifold
M such that its algebra C∞(M) of smooth functions is equipped with a Poisson algebra structure. In
other words, there exists an bilinear antisymmetric map {·, ·} : C∞(M) × C∞(M) →C∞(M) that
satisfies Jacobi identity and Leibniz rule. This map is called a Poisson bracket. There is, however, an
equivalent definition that we discuss next.
Since X(M) is a vector space, we can construct the k-th tensor product of X(M) with itself. Its anti-
symmetric part is usually denoted by VkM and the elements of VkM are called k-vector fields. Let
V0M := C∞(M) and define VM := L
k∈N VkM, the space of so-called multivector fields.
From the fact that a Poisson bracket on M is antisymmetric and satisfies the Leibniz rule, we conclude
that {·, ·} gives rise to a 2-vector field π ∈Λ2M. The remaining question is how to encode the Jacobi
identity in terms of such a bivector field. This task can be accomplished using the notion introduced
next.
Definition 1.1.1. The Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket on VM is the unique bilinear map [·, ·]S : VM×VM →
VM satisfying that: a) [f, g]S = 0 for every f, g ∈C∞(M); b) Xf = [X, f]S = −[f, X]S, for every
X ∈X(M) and f ∈C∞(M); c) We define
[X1 ∧. . . ∧Xk, Y1 ∧. . . ∧Yl]S :=
k
X
i=1
l
X
j=1
(−1)i+j[Xi, Yj] ∧X1 ∧. . . ∧b
Xi ∧. . . Xk ∧Y1 ∧. . . ∧bYj ∧. . . ∧Yl,
where X1, . . . Xk, Y1, . . . , Yl are vector fields on M with k, l ∈N, hatted vector fields are omitted in the
exterior product and [·, ·] stands for the Lie bracket of vector fields.
The Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket restricted to V1M = X(M) matches the Lie bracket of vector fields. In
this sense, the Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket can be considered as a generalisation of the Lie derivative of
vector fields to multivector fields [73, 138, 173]. Since the Lie bracket of two vector fields is local, namely
the value of [X, Y ] for two vector fields X, Y on M at p ∈M depends only on the value of X and Y on a
local open neighbourhood of p. Due to this and Definition 1.1.1, the Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket is also
local.
Finally, it is a matter of straightforward computation to verify that the Jacobi identity for the Poisson
bracket is equivalent to the condition [π, π]SN = 0 for the associated 2-vector field π.
Proposition 1.1.2. Let X ∈VkM, Y ∈VlM, and Z ∈VmM. The Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket satisfies
the following properties:
1. [X, Y ]S ∈Vk+l−1M,
2. [X, Y ]S = −(−1)(k−1)(l−1)[Y, X]S,
3. [X, Y ∧Z]S = [X, Y ]S ∧Z + (−1)(k+1)lY ∧[X, Z]S,
4. [X, [Y, Z]S]S = [[X, Y ]S, Z]S + (−1)(k−1)(l−1)[Y, [X, Z]S]S.
The last property in Proposition 1.1.2 can be rewritten as
(−1)(k−1)(m−1)[X, [Y, Z]S]S + (−1)(l−1)(k−1)[Y, [Z, X]S]S + (−1)(m−1)(l−1)[Z, [X, Y ]S]S = 0
and, in this form, it is called a graded Jacobi identity [130]. The space VM equipped with the Schouten-
Nijenhuis bracket is an example of a Gerstenhaber algebra.

15
In this thesis, we are mostly concerned with a special case of the Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket, defined on
the exterior algebra Λg of a Lie algebra g. Let us see how its construction follows from the above definition.
Let G be a Lie group with an associated Lie algebra g. Since the Lie bracket of two left-invariant vector
fields on G is left-invariant, it stems from Definition 1.1.1that the Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket of two left-
invariant multivector fields on G is again a left-invariant multivector field on G. Moreover, left-invariant
0-vector fields V0(G)L on a Lie group G are given by the constant functions on G. Indeed, the space of
0-vector fields on G consists of functions on G. Given f ∈V0(G), the left-invariance condition implies
that f(h) = f(gh) for any g, h ∈G. Thus, f is constant on G and (V0(G))L is isomorphic, as a vector
space, to R. Therefore, the restriction of the Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket on VG to the space VLG of
left-invariant multivector fields on G gives rise to an algebra, which is again a Gerstenhaber algebra.
Since TeG is isomorphic to the left-invariant vector fields on G, a k-antisymmetric tensor product Λkg
of elements of g can be identified with Vk(G)L for any k ∈N. In consequence, the whole VLG can be
written as L
k∈N Λkg. For convenience, we denote Λg := L
k∈N Λkg. By identifying VLG with Λg, we
obtain the Schouten bracket on Λg. This bracket is known in the literature as the algebraic Schouten
bracket on the exterior algebra Λg [173, pg. 172].
Example 1. Consider the Lie algebra sl2 = ⟨e1, e2, e3⟩with commutation relations
[e1, e2] = e1,
[e1, e3] = 2e2,
[e2, e3] = e3.
Then, Λ2sl2 = ⟨e1 ∧e2, e1, ∧e3, e2 ∧e3⟩, Λ3sl2 = ⟨e1 ∧e2 ∧e3⟩. Every Lie algebra can be understood as
the Lie algebra of left-invariant vector fields of a Lie group [97]. In particular, sl2 is isomorphic to the
Lie algebra of vector fields of the Lie group, SL2, of unimodular real 2 × 2 matrices. Then, the elements
of each Λksl2 can be identified with left-invariant k-vector fields on SL2. In particular, e1, e2, e3 can be
understood as left-invariant vector fields on SL2 and, in view of the formula for the Schouten bracket, it
reduces to the Lie bracket of elements of sl2, namely
[e1, e2]S = e1,
[e1, e3]S = 2e2,
[e2, e3]S = e3.
Meanwhile, applying the property 3. of Proposition 1.1.2, we get
[e1, e2 ∧e3]S = [e1, e2]S ∧e3 + e2 ∧[e1, e3]S = e1 ∧e3 + e2 ∧2e2 = e1 ∧e3.
Similarly, we obtain
[e1, e1 ∧e2 ∧e3]S = [e1, e1] ∧e2 ∧e3 + e1 ∧[e1, e2 ∧e3]S = e1 ∧e1 ∧e3 = 0
One can proceed analogously with the remaining multivectors.
1.2
Generalised distributions
A generalised distribution (also called a Stefan–Sussmann distribution) on M is a correspondence D
attaching to each x ∈M a subspace Dx ⊂TxM. We say that D is differentiable if for every point x ∈M
there exist smooth vector fields X1, . . . , Xp defined on some open neighbourhood U of x so that they take
values in D at points of U, i.e. X1(x′), . . . , Xp(x′) ∈Dx′ for every x′ ∈U, and they span the distribution
at p, namely Dx = ⟨X1(x), . . . , Xp(x)⟩. We stress that, from now on, we assume generalised distributions
to be differentiables.
We call rank of D at x the dimension of Dx. As a function ρ : x ∈M →dim Dx ∈N, the rank of a
differentiable distribution is lower-semicontinuous (for a given topological space X, a function f : X →R
is called lower-semicontinuous at x if for every ϵ > 0 there exists a neighbourhood U of x such that any

16
Chapter 1. Fundamentals
point y ∈U satisfies f(y) > f(x) −ϵ.). Indeed, since D is differentiable, it is spanned by a finite family
of vector fields being linearly independent at x and taking values in D. This ensures that, for every
x ∈M, there exists an open neighbourhood U of x such that ρ(y) ≥ρ(x) for every y ∈U. A generalised
distribution need not have the same rank at every point of M. If D has the same rank at every point of
M, then D is said to be regular or D is simply called a distribution. Otherwise, D is said to be singular.
A generalised distribution D on M is involutive if every two vector fields taking values in D satisfy that
their Lie bracket takes values in D as well.
Example 2. Let us consider the generalised distribution D on R2 of the form

with y ≤0. Therefore, D is singular.

A stratification F on a manifold M is a partition of M into connected disjoint immersed submanifolds
{Fk}k∈I, where I is a certain set of indices, i.e. M = S
k∈I Fk and the submanifolds {Fk}k∈I satisfy
Fk ∩Fk′ = ∅for k ̸= k′ and k, k′ ∈I. The connected immersed submanifolds Fk, with k ∈I, are called
the strata of the stratification. A stratification is regular if its strata are immersed submanifolds of the
same dimension, whereas it is singular otherwise. Regular stratifications are called foliations and their
strata are called leaves. The tangent space to a stratum, Fk, of a stratification passing through a point
x ∈M is a subspace Dx ⊂TxM. All the subspaces Dx ⊂TxM for every point x ∈M give rise to a
generalised distribution D := S
x∈M Dx on M. All the leaves of a foliation have the same dimension and,
therefore, the generalised distribution formed by the tangent spaces at every point to its leaves is regular.
Meanwhile, a singular stratification gives rise to a singular generalised distribution.
In this work, we are specially interested in generalised distributions generated by finite-dimensional Lie
algebras of vector fields, the so-called Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebras [120]. More specifically, let V be a
Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra, the vector fields of V span a generalised distribution DV given by
DV
x := {Xx : X ∈V } ⊂TxM,
∀x ∈M.
Since the space of vector fields tangent to the strata of a stratification are closed under Lie brackets, the
Lie bracket of vector fields on M taking values in a distribution D can be restricted to each one of its
strata. A generalised distribution D on M is integrable if there exists a stratification F on M such that
each stratum Fk thereof satisfies TxFk = Dx for every x ∈Fk.
A relevant question is whether a generalised distribution on M is integrable or not. For regular distribu-
tions, the Fröbenius theorem holds [87, 108].
Theorem 1.2.1. If D is a regular generalised distribution on a manifold M, then D is integrable if and
only if it is involutive.
In case of a generalised distribution, involutivity is a natural necessary condition for its integrability
because the space of vector fields tangent to any stratum of a stratification is involutive. Nevertheless, if
a generalised distribution is not regular, its involutiveness is not sufficient.

Chapter 1. Fundamentals
17
Example 4. We have already verified that the distribution in Example 2 is singular. Let us see now
whether it is integrable. To do so, it is enough to give a stratification whose tangent spaces to its strata.
In the region y ≤0, the strata are the straight lines given by y = const. These are indeed connected
disjoint immersed submanifolds. There is an additional stratum given by the whole half plane for which
y > 0. It is simple to see that the tangent space to these strata give the generalised distribution D.
The distribution in Example 3 is not integrable. As before, the lower half-plane (with y ≤0) is stratified
by the straight lines y = const. Now, let us look for the stratification of the upper half-plane. For the
quadrants x > 0, y > 0 and x < 0, y > 0, we can take the quadrants themselves as the strata. The
remaining y-axis, however, poses a problem. The distribution at these points is spanned by ∂x. Since
the tangent space to a point is zero, we cannot stratify it by separate points. If on the other hand, we
consider an open interval, the tangent space would have the ∂y-component. Therefore, any available
option contradicts TxFx = Dx.
Remarkably, integrability of generalised distributions can be asserted under additional conditions. Let
us note two relevant results on this issue (for a detailed exposition of integrability theorems we refer to
[108]). If a generalised distribution D on M is analytical, i.e. for every x ∈M there exists a family of
analytical vector fields taking values in D and spanning Dx′ for every x′ in an open neighbourhood of x,
one has the following proposition.
Theorem 1.2.2. (Nagano [136]) Let M be a real analytic manifold, and let V be a sub-Lie algebra of
analytic vector fields on M. Then, the induced analytic distribution DV is integrable.
In Example 2, the distribution is spanned by the vector fields
X = ∂
∂x,
Y = exp(−1/y2) ∂
∂y
which generate a two-dimensional abelian Lie algebra. However, since Y is not an analytical vector field,
Theorem 1.2.2 cannot ensure its integrability.
If a generalised distribution is not analytical, sufficient conditions for integrability are established by the
following Stefan-Sussmann theorem. This result will be of great relevance in this thesis.
Theorem 1.2.3. (Hermann [99]) Let M be a smooth manifold.
If V is a finite-dimensional Lie
subalgebra of X(M), then the distribution DV is integrable.
As already noticed, vector fields X, Y in Example 2 generate a two-dimensional Lie algebra. Thus in
view of Theorem 1.2.3, the induced distribution is integrable.
Once we assure the integrability of the generalised distribution, the next relevant problem concerns the
determination of its strata.
In case of a distribution DV on a manifold M spanned by the Vessiot–
Guldberg Lie algebra V , it is known (see [165] for details) that the stratum Fk of DV passing through
x ∈M is given by the set of points
Fk = {y ∈M : ∃t1, . . . , ts ∈R, y = exp(t1X1) ◦exp(t2X2) ◦. . . ◦exp(tsXs)x},
(1.2.1)
where s is any natural number, X1, . . . , Xs are any vector fields of V , and each exp(X) stands for the
local diffeomorphism on M induced by the vector field X on M, i.e. it maps x onto the point γ(1) of the
integral curve γ :] −ϵ, ϵ[⊂R 7→M of X passing through x.
1.3
Lie algebras, g-modules, and Grassmann algebras
For a vector space V , let GL(V ) and gl(V ) stand for the Lie group of automorphisms and the Lie algebra
of endomorphisms on V , respectively. A g-module is a pair (V, ρ), where ρ : v ∈g 7→ρv ∈gl(V ) is a Lie

18
Chapter 1. Fundamentals
algebra homomorphism. A g-module (V, ρ) will be represented just by V , while ρv(x), for any v ∈g and
x ∈V , will be written simply as vx if ρ is understood from context. If Im(ρ) leaves invariant a certain
subspace W ⊂V , then one can consider the restriction ρW : g →gl(W). A pair (W, ρW ) is called a
g-submodule.
Example 5. Let ad : v ∈g 7→[v, ·]g ∈gl(g) be the adjoint representation of g. Then, (g, ad) is a
g-module [88]. Since each [v, ·]g, with v ∈g, is a derivation of the Lie algebra g [88], the map ad can be
considered as a mapping ad : g →der(g), where der(g) is the Lie algebra of derivations on g.
Example 6. The group Aut(g) of Lie algebra automorphisms of g admits a Lie group structure [155] and
its Lie algebra is denoted by aut(g). The tangent map at idg ∈Aut(g) to the injection ι : Aut(g) ,→GL(g)
induces a Lie algebra morphism c
ad : aut(g) ≃TidgAut(g) →gl(g) ≃TidgGL(g) and (g, c
ad) becomes an
aut(g)-module.
Given a g-module (V, ρ), a Grassmann algebra Λg and its specific subspaces can also be equipped with
the g-module structure.

A Lie algebra homomorphism ρ : g →gl(V ) gives rise to a Lie group homomorphism Φ : eG →GL(V ),
where eG is connected and simply connected, so that the associated diagram of the form (1.3.1) is commu-
tative [68]. Thus, Lemma 1.3.2 asserts Φ( eG) is generated by the elements of exp(ρ(g)). In other words,
Φ( eG) is the smallest group containing exp(ρ(g)). Although Φ( eG) may not be an embedded submanifold
in GL(V ), it is always a Lie group [112]. Previous facts motivate the following definition.
Definition 1.3.3. The Lie group of a g-module (V, ρ) is the immersed Lie subgroup GL(ρ) of GL(V )
generated by exp(ρ(g)).

Finally, let us shortly mention about a special kind of maps associated with g-modules.
Definition 1.3.10. Let us consider a g-module (V, ρ). A trace form associated with g is the bilinear
symmetric mapping trρ on V given by trρ(v1, v2) := tr(ρ(v1) ◦ρ(v2)) for any v1, v2 ∈V .
Lemma 1.3.11. Given a g-module (V, ρ), its associated trace form is ad-invariant, i.e.
trρ([v1, v2], v3) + trρ(v2, [v1, v3]) = 0,
∀v, v′, v′′ ∈g.
Moreover, ker trρ is an ideal of g.
Proof. Since ρ : g →gl(V ) is an algebra homomoprhism and by the cyclicity of the trace, we get
trρ([v1, v2], v3) + trρ(v2, [v1, v3]) = tr(ρ([v1, v2]) ◦ρ(v3)) + tr(ρ(v2) ◦ρ([v1, v3]))
= tr((ρ(v1) ◦ρ(v2) −ρ(v2) ◦ρ(v1)) ◦ρ(v3)) + tr(ρ(v2) ◦(ρ(v1) ◦ρ(v3) −ρ(v3) ◦ρ(v1)))
= tr(ρ(v1) ◦ρ(v2) ◦ρ(v3)) −tr(ρ(v2) ◦ρ(v1) ◦ρ(v3)) + tr(ρ(v2) ◦ρ(v1) ◦ρ(v3)) −tr(ρ(v2) ◦ρ(v3) ◦ρ(v1))
= 0

Chapter 1. Fundamentals
21
Recall that the kernel of a bilinear form b on V is a subspace ker b ⊂V such that b(v, w) = 0 for any
v ∈V and w ∈ker b [107]. Proving the second part of our lemma amounts to showing that if v ∈ker trρ
and w ∈g, then [w, v] ∈ker trρ. From the definition of the trace form, for any g ∈g we obtain
trρ([w, v], g) = tr(ρ([w, v]), ρ(g)) = tr(ρ(w) ◦ρ(v) ◦ρ(g) −ρ(v) ◦ρ(w) ◦ρ(g))
= tr(ρ(w) ◦ρ(v) ◦ρ(g)) −tr(ρ(v) ◦ρ(w) ◦ρ(g)) = tr(ρ(v) ◦ρ(g) ◦ρ(w)) −tr(ρ(v) ◦ρ(w) ◦ρ(g))
= tr(ρ(v) ◦(ρ(g) ◦ρ(w) −ρ(w) ◦ρ(w))) = tr(ρ(v) ◦ρ([g, w])) = trρ(v, [g, w]) = 0
Thus, [w, v] ∈ker trρ.
1.4
The Chevalley-Eilenberg cohomology of Lie algebras
Following the standard exposition in [104], we present the construction of Lie algebra cohomology. For
a complex Lie algebra g, let (M, ρ) be a g-module. For any n ∈N, denote the space of n-cochains as
Cn(g, M) := HomC(Λng, M), consisting of skew-symmetric n-multilinear functions on g×n with values in
M. We understand C0(g, M) as the set of constant functions from g to a fixed element of M. For n ≥1,
the coboundary map dn : Cn(g, M) →Cn+1(g, M) is defined as
dnf(x1, . . . , xn, xn+1) :=
n+1
X
p=1
(−1)pρxpf(x1, . . . , ˆxi, . . . , xn+1)
+
X
1≤i<j≤n+1
(−1)i+jf([xi, xj], x1, . . . , ˆxi, ˆxj, . . . , xn+1)
(1.4.1)
where the hatted elements are omitted and by the symbol ρx we denote the action of x ∈g on M. In case
of n = 0, we set d0f(x1) := ρx1xf for the constant map f : g ∈g 7→xf ∈M. By a tedious computation,
one shows that dn+1 ◦dn = 0. This property implies that Bn ⊆Zn+1, where Zn := ker(dn) is the set of
n-cocycles and Bn := Im(dn−1) is the set of n-coboundaries. By default, we put B0 = ∅. In consequence,
we can define the cohomology space in the usual way, as the quotient space Hn(g, M) := Zn+1/Bn.
It is remarkable that for a semi-simple complex Lie algebra g, its cohomology spaces H1(g, M) and
H2(g, M) are trivial. These two facts are commonly known as, respectively, the first and the second
Whitehead lemma.
Let us briefly focus on the first lemma. By definition, the space of 1-cocycles consists of linear functions
Ψ : g →M such that d1Ψ(x, y) = 0, or equivalently ρx · Ψ(y) −ρy · Ψ(x) −Ψ([x, y]) = 0. On the other
hand, the space of 1-coboundaries is formed by the functions f : g ∋v 7→ρvm ∈M for a fixed m ∈M.
Thus, vanishing of the cohomology space H1(g, M) is equivalent to the following statement: any linear
function Ψ : g →M such that ρx · Ψ(y) −ρy · Ψ(x) −Ψ([x, y]) = 0 can be written as Ψ(g) = ρgm for a
certain m ∈M. This result follows immediately from the next theorem.
Theorem 1.4.1. Let g be a semi-simple Lie algebra over a field k of characteristic zero and let (V, ρ) be
a g-module. If ϕ : g →V is a linear map such that
ϕ([v1, v2]) = ρv1ϕ(v2) −ρv2ϕ(v1),
(1.4.2)
then there exists x ∈V such that ϕ(v) = ρvx for every v ∈g.

Now, let g denote a Lie algebra satisfying the assumptions of this Theorem. Denote by R the kernel of

Recall the Fitting’s lemma [104]: any linear endomorphism A of a finite-dimensional vector space V yields
a decomposition V ≃V0⊕V1 into A-invariant subspaces, such that A restricted to V0 is nilpotent, whereas
its restriction to V1 gives an isomorphism. Using that fact, we obtain the decomposition V ≃V0 ⊕V1
relative to Γ. Since Γρg = ρgΓ for any g ∈g, the subspaces V0, V1 are g-submodules. Indeed, if v ∈V0,
then Γρgv = ρgΓv = 0, which means that ρgv ∈V0. If v ∈V1, then Γρgv = ρgΓv = ρgv, whence ρgv ∈V1.
Similarly, the map ϕ admits a decomposition ϕ(v) = ϕ0(v) + ϕ1(v) for any v ∈g, where ϕ0(v) ∈V0 and
ϕ1(v) ∈V1. Since ϕ satisfies (1.4.2), we get
ϕ0([v, w]) + ϕ1([v, w]) = (vϕ0(w) −wϕ0(v)) + (vϕ1(w) −wϕ1(v)) ∈V0 ⊕V1
In consequence, both maps ϕ0, ϕ1 satisfy the condition (1.4.2) as well.

ϕ0, ϕ1 satisfying the assumptions of this Theorem, we can repeat the whole argument for these smaller
submodules.

1.5
Lie bialgebras
This section describes the most important facts on Lie bialgebras (see [58, 65, 67, 106, 111] for details).
Definition 1.5.1. A Lie bialgebra is a pair (g, δ), where g is a Lie algebra with a Lie bracket [·, ·] and
δ : g →T 2g is a linear map satisfying that:
1. the (restricted) dual map δ∗: T 2g∗→g∗is a Lie bracket on g∗,
2. the map δ satisfies the condition
δ([x, y]) = ⊗2adxδ(y) −⊗2adyδ(x)
∀x, y ∈g.
(1.5.1)
Definition 1.5.2. A homomorphism of Lie bialgebras (g, δg) and (h, δh) is a homomorphism ϕ : g →h
of Lie algebras such that
(ϕ ⊗ϕ) ◦δg = δh ◦ϕ.
The map δ is usually called a cocommutator to emphasize the fact that its transpose is indeed a Lie
bracket (also called commutator at times) on g∗.
The first condition in Definition 1.5.1 implies that Imδ ⊆Λ2g ⊂T 2g as δ∗is required to be a Lie bracket
on g∗. In consequence, (1.5.1) can be written using Schouten bracket as
δ([x, y]) = [x, δ(y)]S −[y, δ(x)]S,
∀x, y ∈g.
(1.5.2)
Note that δ∗is well-defined regardless of the Lie algebra dimension. Indeed, for every finite-dimensional
Lie algebra g, one has that (T 2g)∗≃T 2g∗, whereas in the infinite-dimensional case, we have g∗⊗g∗⊂
(g ⊗g)∗. The latter situation requires only the restriction of the dual map.

24
Chapter 1. Fundamentals
The second condition in Definition 1.5.1 has a concise cohomological interpretation. For δ : g →T 2g,
one can understand (1.5.1) as d1δ = 0, where d1 is the coboundary map given by (1.4.1) for M = T 2g.
In other words, a cocommutator δ is a 1-cocycle in the Chevalley-Eilenberg cohomology of g with values
in T 2g.
Naturally, 1-coboundaries represent a particular example of such 1-cocycles. Thus, 1-coboundaries give
rise to the following special case of the Lie bialgebra structure.
Definition 1.5.3. A coboundary Lie bialgebra is a Lie bialgebra (g, δr) whose cocommutator δr takes
the form δr(x) = T 2adxr for certain r ∈T 2g and all x ∈g. The element r is called a quasitriangular
r-matrix of δr. If r ∈Λ2g, then it is called a triangular r-matrix.
Once again, the condition δr(x) = T 2adxr imposed on the cocommutator δr follows immediately from the
definition of the coboundary map (1.4.1). In the particular case of r ∈Λ2g, it can be written concisely
as δr(x) = [x, r]S.
It is obvious that for any Lie algebra g, there is a trivial Lie bialgebra structure δ(v) = 0 ∈Λ2g for
any v ∈g. The natural question is whether there are any nontrivial bialgebra structures as well. For a
large class of finite-dimensional Lie algebras, the positive answer was given. For abelian Lie algebras, any
Lie bracket on g∗induces a Lie bialgebra structure on g. In case of nonabelian Lie algebras, de Smedt
[164] asserts the existence of a triangular structure over fields of characteristic zero. Lie algebras over
fields of arbitrary characteristic were considered in [86, 133]. However, very little is known for general
infinite-dimensional Lie algebras - only specific examples were studied in detail (e.g. [98, 113, 133, 179]).
Example 7. Consider the Lie algebra sl2 with the basis and commutation relations as in Example 1.
Let us define a mapping δ : v ∈sl2 7→[v, e2 ∧e3]S ∈Λ2sl2. Then by the third property in Proposition
1.1.2, one has that
δ([v1, v2]) = [[v1, v2], e2 ∧e3]S = −[e2 ∧e3, [v1, v2]S]S = −[[e2 ∧e3, v1]S, v2]S + [[e2 ∧e3, v2]S, v1]S.
Thus,
δ([v1, v2]) = −[v2, δ(v1)]S + [v1, δ(v2)]S.
In particular, one has
δ(e1) = [e1, e2 ∧e3] = [e1, e2] ∧e3 + e2 ∧[e1, e3] = e1 ∧e3,
δ(e2) = e2 ∧e3,
δ(e3) = 0.
Let us work out δ∗: Λ2sl∗
2 →sl∗
2. Denote by {e1, e2, e3} the basis dual to {e1, e2, e3}. Then, {e1 ∧e2, e2 ∧
e3, e1 ∧e3} is the dual basis to {e1 ∧e2, e2 ∧e3, e1 ∧e3}. For each element of the dual basis, let us compute
its image by δ∗. For example,
[δ∗(e1 ∧e2)](e1) = ⟨e1 ∧e2, δ(e1)⟩= ⟨e1 ∧e2, e1 ∧e3⟩= 0
[δ∗(e1 ∧e2)](e2) = ⟨e1 ∧e2, δ(e2)⟩= ⟨e1 ∧e2, e2 ∧e3⟩= 0
[δ∗(e1 ∧e2)](e3) = ⟨e1 ∧e2, δ(e3)⟩= ⟨e1 ∧e2, 0⟩= 0
Thus, δ∗(e1 ∧e2) = 0. Similarly, we get
δ∗(e1 ∧e3) = e1,
δ∗(e2 ∧e3) == e2.

[e1, [e2, e3]sl∗]sl∗+ [e2, [e3, e1]sl∗]sl∗+ [e3, [e1, e2]sl∗]sl∗= 0,

Interestingly, semi-simple Lie algebras admit only coboundary Lie bialgebra structures. Let g be a semi-
simple Lie algebra and let M = T 2g. Then, Theorem 1.4.1 implies that for any cocommutator δ : g →M,
there exists r ∈M such that δ(v) = ⊗2advr for any v ∈g.
Let us now address an important question what conditions an element r ∈T 2g should satisfy so that
the map δr : v ∈g 7→⊗2advr is a cocommutator. First, write r = s + a, where s ∈S2g and a ∈Λ2g.
Since the dual of δr = d0r must yield a Lie bracket, which is antisymmetric by definition, it follows that
d0s = 0 or equivalently, ⊗2advs = 0 for any v ∈g.
Definition 1.5.4. An element of w ∈T kg is called g-invariant if ⊗2advw = 0 for every v ∈g. We
write (T kg)g for all g-invariant elements in T kg. Similarly, an element of w ∈Λkg is called g-invariant if
[v, w]S = 0 for every v ∈g. We write (Λkg)g for all g-invariant elements in Λkg.
In other words, for the element r ∈T 2g to be a quasitriangular r-matrix, its symmetric part has to be
g-invariant.
Additionally, in order for (d0r)∗to define a Lie bracket, the dual (d0a)∗must satisfy the Jacobi identity.
In turn, this puts an additional condition on the element r ∈T 2g, which we discuss next.
First, let us introduce a comfortable notation. For r = P
i ai ⊗bi ∈T 2g, we define
[[r, r]] := [r12, r13] + [r12, r23] + [r13, r23]
where
[r12, r13] := [ai, aj] ⊗bi ⊗bj,
[r12, r23] := ai ⊗[bi, aj] ⊗bj,
[r13, r23] := ai ⊗aj ⊗[bi, bj].
Obviously, [[r, r]] gives an element of T 3g. Let us note that for r ∈Λ2g, the element [[r, r]] reduces to the
Schouten bracket [r, r]S of r with itself.
Definition 1.5.5. The map Alt : g⊗(n+1) →g⊗(n+1) is defined by
Alt(a1 ⊗. . . ⊗an) :=
n
X
k=0
aσk(1) ⊗. . . aσk(n),
∀a1, . . . , an ∈g,
where σk is a cyclic permutation of {1, . . . , n} such that σk(i) := (i + k) (mod n) for i = 1, 2, . . . , n.
Lemma 1.5.6. Let the map δr : g →Λ2g be defined by δr(x) := [x, r]S for r ∈T 2g. Then
Jδr(x) + ⊗3adx[[r, r]] = 0,
where Jδr(x) := Alt[((δr ⊗id) ◦δr)(x)].
The proof is a direct computation (see [58] for details).
Theorem 1.5.7. The map δr : g ∋v 7→[v, r]S ∈Λ2g defined by r ∈Λ2g is a cocommutator if and only
if
[[r, r]] ∈(T 3g)g.
(1.5.3)

26
Chapter 1. Fundamentals
Proof. Let us take any linear map δ : g →Λ2g and define a bilinear map on g∗by [ξ, η]g∗:= δ∗(ξ ∧η).
Since [[ξ, η]g∗, ζ]g∗= δ∗(δ∗(ξ ⊗η) ⊗ζ) = δ∗(δ∗⊗id)(ξ ⊗η ⊗ζ), we see that [·, ·]g∗satisfies Jacobi identity
if and only if Jδ vanishes. Thus, by virtue of Lemma 1.5.6, an element [[r, r]] has to be g-invariant.
The condition (1.5.3) is known as the modified classical Yang-Baxter equation (mCYBE). Its special case
[[r, r]] = 0 is called the classical Yang-Baxter equation (CYBE). Following the previously introduced
nomenclature, the solutions to the mCBYE are called quasitriangular r-matrices (or simply, r-matrices)
while solutions to the CYBE are called triangular r-matrices.
This thesis is solely devoted to study the triangular r-matrices. We will hereafter refer to such elements
shortly as r-matrices, since it will not lead to any confusion later.
Example 8. Let us illustrate previous notions by analysing a two-dimensional non-abelian real Lie
algebra g = ⟨x, y⟩with [x, y] = x. Since Λ2g = ⟨x ∧y⟩, every cocommutator is of the form δ(x) = ax ∧y
and δ(y) = bx ∧y for certain a, b ∈R. The cocycle condition (1.5.1) is satisfied since
δ([x, y]) = [x, δ(y)]S −[y, δ(x)]S = [x, bx ∧y] −[y, ax ∧y]] = ax ∧y = δ(x).
Since g∗is a two-dimensional vector space, δT is an antisymmetric map that becomes automatically a
Lie bracket on g∗. Indeed, by multilinearity of the Jacobi identity, its value on any elements θ∗
1, θ∗
2, θ∗
3
reduces to obtaining its value on elements of a dual basis {x∗, y∗} to {x, y}. Among four cases, we get
two expressions that require further calculations, namely
[x∗, [x∗, y∗]] + [x∗, [y∗, x∗]] + [y∗, [x∗, x∗]] = [x∗, [x∗, y∗]] + [x∗, [y∗, x∗]] = [x∗, [x∗, y∗]] −[x∗, [x∗, y∗]] = 0
and
[y∗, [y∗, x∗]] + [y∗, [x∗, y∗]] + [x∗, [y∗, y∗]] = 0
Thus, the Jacobi identity holds and it follows that δ gives a cocommutator with no restrictions on the
coefficients a, b.
Let us now assume that (g, δ) is a coboundary Lie bialgebra. Since dim g = 2, any r-matrix r ∈Λ2g
satisfies (1.5.3) and thus, defines a cocommutator. Denote r = cx ∧y for any c ∈R. From Definition
1.5.3, it follows that
δ(x) = [x, r]S = 0 = ax ∧y
⇐⇒
a = 0
and
δ(y) = [y, r]S = −cx ∧y = bc ∧y
⇐⇒
b = −c.
It is important to establish when two r-matrices induce the same cocommutator. This is answered by
the following proposition.
Proposition 1.5.8. Two r-matrices r1, r2 ∈Λ2g satisfy that δr1 = δr2 if and only if r1 −r2 ∈(Λ2g)g.
Proof. Let δr1 and δr2 be the cocommutators respectively induced by elements r1, r2 ∈λ2g.
Since
δr1 = δr2, one obtains that [r1, v]S = [r2, v]S for every v ∈g. Hence, [r1 −r2, v]S = 0 for any v ∈g, which
implies r1 −r2 ∈(Λ2g)g. The converse is immediate.
In other words, two r-matrices give rise to the same cocommutator if and only if their difference is a
g-invariant bivector. Thus in order to determine coboundary Lie bialgebras, one should focus not on
r-matrices themselves, but on their equivalence classes in Λ2g/(Λ2g)g.
It follows from Definition 1.5.1 that given a Lie bialgebra (g, δg), one can construct a Lie bialgebra
structure on g∗and vice versa. This observation motivates another characterisation of the Lie bialgebra
notion, which makes such a symmetry more apparent.

Chapter 1. Fundamentals
27
Definition 1.5.9. A Manin triple consists of a triple of Lie algebras (p, p1, p2) and a nondegenerate
symmetric bilinear form (·, ·) on p such that
• p1 and p2 are Lie subalgebras of p,
• there is a decomposition p = p1 ⊕p2 as vector spaces,
• p1 and p2 are isotropic relative to the bilinear form (·, ·), that is (v, w) = 0 for all w ∈p and any
v ∈p1 (and analogously, for v ∈p2).
For finite-dimensional Lie algebras, the notions of Manin triples and Lie bialgebras are equivalent, as
proved by the next proposition.
Proposition 1.5.10. Let g be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra. There is a one-to-one correspondence
between Lie bialgebra structures on g and Manin triples (p, p1, p2) with p1 = g.
It is known that Lie algebras are infinitesimal counterparts of Lie groups [97]. Similarly for Lie bialgebras,
a cocommutator induces an additional structure at the Lie group level.
Definition 1.5.11. A Poisson–Lie group is a Lie group G equipped with a Poisson bracket {·, ·} such
that the multiplication in G gives a Poisson map, that is
{f, g}(p · q) = {f ◦Lp, g ◦Lp}(q) + {f ◦Rq, g ◦Rq}(p)
for any f, g ∈C∞(G) and p, q ∈G. The maps Lg, Rg correspond to the left and right translation maps
on G, respectively.
In terms of the Poisson bivector w ∈Λ2TG, the above condition reads
wp·q = (TqLp ⊗TqLp)(wq) + (TpRq ⊗TpRq)(wp),
∀p, q ∈G.
(1.5.4)
Let us briefly investigate the correspondence between Lie bialgebras and Poisson–Lie groups. Introduce
the map wR : G →g ⊗g given by wR(g) := (TgRg−1 ⊗TgRg−1)(wg), the right translation of the Poisson
bivector w to the identity element e of G. Its derivative at e yields a map δ : g →g ⊗g. Using wR, one
can rewrite condition (1.5.4) as
wR(p · q) = (Adp ⊗Adp)(wR(q)) + wR(p)
Differentiating the above equality at e gives
δ([v, w]) = ⊗2advδ(w) −⊗2adwδ(v)
This shows that the Lie bialgebra structure on a Lie algebra g can be derived from the Poisson–Lie
groups structure on the corresponding Lie group G. Conversely, any Lie bialgebra on g gives rise to the
Poisson–Lie group structure on the connected and simply-connected Lie group G associated with g. We
shall not discuss the converse result (see [58] and references therein for details).
1.6
Lie systems
Among first-order ordinary differential equations, Lie systems constitute a particular class, as their general
solutions can be expressed in a special way. In this section, we summarise necessary results on such
systems.
If not otherwise stated, every system of differential equations considered in this Section is
non-autonomous and moreover, N is an n-dimensional manifold.
First, let us recall the correspondence between first-order ODEs and t-dependent vector fields. Define
π2 : (t, x) ∈R × N 7→x ∈N and let τN : TN →N be the tangent bundle projection. A t-dependent

28
Chapter 1. Fundamentals
vector field on N is a mapping X : (t, x) ∈R × N 7→X(t, x) ∈TN such that τN ◦X = π2. An integral
curve of X is a mapping γ : R →N such that
dγ
dt (t) = X(t, γ(t)),
∀t ∈R.
(1.6.1)
Given a t-dependent vector field X, one can construct [3, 53] a vector field ˜X on R × N, called an
autonomisation of X, of the form e

Conversely, if eγ : R →R × N is an integral curve of e

t ∈R, then π2 ◦eγ is a solution to (1.6.1). In consequence, we can identify a system (1.6.1) and its
associated t-dependent vector field X.
A superposition rule [51, 53, 183] for a system X on a manifold N is a map Ψ : Nm × N →N satisfying
that the general solution, x(t), to X can be written as
x(t) = Ψ(x(1)(t), . . . , x(m)(t), k),
for a generic family of particular solutions x(1)(t), . . . , x(m)(t) of X and a parameter k ∈N to be related
to the initial condition of x(t). We call Lie system a system of first-order ODEs admitting a superposition
rule.
Given a system of first-order ODEs, one may ask whether it forms a Lie system. The following theorem
provides a suitable geometric condition.
Theorem 1.6.1. (The Lie–Scheffers theorem [50, 51, 116, 183]) A system X on N admits a
superposition rule if and only if X =

spanning an r-dimensional Lie algebra of vector fields, a so-called Vessiot-Guldberg Lie algebra of X, and
a family b1(t), . . . , br(t) of t-dependent functions.

1.7
Jacobi structures
This section briefly introduces the basic theory on Jacobi structures, independently introduced by Kirillov
and Lichnerowicz(see [100, 105, 115, 173] for further details). If not otherwise stated, it is assumed that
all results and structures are real and globally defined. Moreover, manifolds are assumed to be connected.

30
Chapter 1. Fundamentals
Definition 1.7.1. A Jacobi manifold is a triple (M, Λ, R), where M is a manifold, Λ is a bivector field
on M, and R is a vector field, the so-called Reeb vector field, satisfying
[Λ, Λ]SN = −2R ∧Λ,
[R, Λ]SN = 0,
where [·, ·]SN is the Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket on M (cf. [173]).
To understand the relation of our results with the formalism in other works, e.g. [114], it is convenient to
clarify that it is commonly assumed that [Λ, Λ]′ = 2R ∧Λ and [Λ, R]′ = 0 by using the original Schouten
bracket [·, ·]′ given in [159, 138, 173].
Example 9. Since a Poisson manifold is a pair (M, Λ) where Λ is a bivector field on M such that
[Λ, Λ]SN = 0 [173], every Poisson manifold can be understood as a Jacobi manifold of the form (M, Λ, R =
0).
Example 10. The continuous Heisenberg group [180] is the matrix Lie group
H =

[RH, ΛH]SN = 0.
Hence, (H, ΛH, RH) is a Jacobi manifold.

Every bivector field Λ on a manifold N leads to a mapping Λ♯: T ∗M →TM of the form Λ♯(θp) =

Definition 1.7.2. Let (M, Λ, R) be a Jacobi manifold. A vector field on M is called Hamiltonian if there
exists a function f ∈C∞(M) so that
X = [Λ, f]SN + fR = Λ♯(df) + fR,
where f is called a Hamiltonian function of f. If f is additionally a first-integral of R, then f is called a
good Hamiltonian function and X is called a good Hamiltonian vector field [100].
We hereafter write Ham(M, Λ, R) for the space of Hamiltonian vector fields relative to the Jacobi manifold
(M, Λ, R). Note that the Reeb vector field of (M, Λ, R) is the Hamiltonian vector field relative to the
constant function f = 1.
The notion of a Hamiltonian vector field relative to a Poisson manifold (M, Λ) is retrieved from the
above definition as, in such a case, the Poisson manifold is a Jacobi manifold (M, Λ, R = 0) and then the
Hamiltonian vector field related to a function f ∈C∞(M) is of the form Xf = Λ♯(df).
A Hamiltonian vector field relative to a Jacobi manifold (M, Λ, R) may have different Hamiltonian func-
tions. Finally, it is worth noting that a vector field X on M is Hamiltonian relative to a Jacobian manifold
(M, Λ, R) if and only if LXΛ = −(Rf)Λ for some function f ∈C∞(M).
Example 12. Given the Jacobi manifold (H, ΛH, RH) and the vector field XL
1 = ∂/∂x, one has that
XL
1 = [ΛH, −y]SN −yRH = Λ♯
H(−dy) −yRH.
Hence, XL
1 is a Hamiltonian vector field with Hamiltonian function hL
1 = −y relative to (H, ΛH, RH).
Example 13. Consider again the Jacobi manifold (SL2, ΛSL, RSL) on SL2 given in Example 11. Then,
the function on SL2 given by f(a, b, c) = a on U and vanishing off U has a Hamiltonian vector field
Xf = [ΛSL, a]SN + aRSL = a2∂a + ac∂c.
It stems from Definition 1.7.2 that every f ∈C∞(M) admits a unique Hamiltonian vector field relative to
(M, Λ, R). Meanwhile, a Hamiltonian vector field may have several Hamiltonian functions. A condition
ensuring the uniqueness of the associated Hamiltonian function is given in the following proposition.
Proposition 1.7.3. Let (M, Λ, R) be a Jacobi manifold such that the distribution DR spanned by R
satisfies DR ∩Im Λ♯= 0 for Im Λ♯:= {Λ♯(df) : f ∈C∞(M)}. Then, every Hamiltonian vector field
admits a unique Hamiltonian function.
Proof. If f1 and f2 are two different Hamiltonian functions of a vector field X on M, then
X = Λ♯(df1) + f1R = Λ♯(df2) + f2R =⇒0 = Λ♯(d(f1 −f2)) + (f1 −f2)R.
By assumption, R /∈Im Λ♯. Thus, the above equality implies that (f1 −f2)R = 0. Since R does not take
values in Im Λ♯, it does not vanish and f1 = f2.
Definition 1.7.4. Every Jacobi manifold (M, Λ, R) gives rise to a bracket on {·, ·}Λ,R : C∞(M) ×
C∞(M) →C∞(M) of the form
{f, g}Λ,R := Λ♯(df, dg) + fRg −gRf,
∀f, g ∈C∞(M).
The following proposition, whose proof can be found in [172] or derived from the results in [173], will be
of interest.

32
Chapter 1. Fundamentals
Proposition 1.7.5. Let (M, Λ, R) be a Jacobi manifold and let f ∈C∞(M). Then,
• X{f,g} = [Xf, Xg],
• [Xf, R] = −(Rf)R −Λ♯(d(Rf)),
• LXf Λ = −(Rf)Λ.
It stems from Proposition 1.7.5 that Ham(M, Λ, R) is a Lie algebra with respect to the standard Lie
bracket of vector fields [173]. Additionally, C∞(M) can be endowed with a Lie algebra structure as
shown below.
Proposition 1.7.6. Let (M, Λ, R) be a Jacobi manifold. The map {·, ·}Λ,R is a Lie bracket on C∞(M).
This Lie bracket becomes a Poisson bracket if and only if R = 0. Moreover, the morphism ϕΛ,R : f ∈
C∞(M) 7→Xf ∈Ham(Λ, R) is a Lie algebra morphism.
While Proposition 1.7.6 ensures that every Jacobi manifold gives rise to a Lie bracket that acts as a
derivation of up to order one in each entry, the following proposition, whose proof can be found in [173],
ensures the converse of this result.
Proposition 1.7.7. A Jacobi manifold on M amounts to a Lie bracket {·, ·} : C∞(M) ⊗C∞(M) →
C∞(M) which is a derivation of up to order one in each entry.
Definition 1.7.8. Let (M, Λ, R) be a Jacobi manifold. The characteristic distribution, DΛ,R, of (M, Λ, R)
is the distribution on M spanned by the elements of Ham(M, Λ, R).
It stems form Proposition 1.7.5 that the characteristic distribution DΛ,R of a Jacobi manifold (M, Λ, R)
is invariant under the flows of Hamiltonian vector fields. Therefore, one obtains the following result (see
[173, Theorem 2.9”] for details).
Theorem 1.7.9. The characteristic distribution of a Jacobi manifold (M, Λ, R) is integrable.
The integral leaves of the characteristic distribution of a Jacobi manifold can be divided into two types:
even- and odd-dimensional. Since Im Λ♯is an even-dimensional distribution, even-dimensional charac-
teristic distributions are those in which R ∈Im Λ♯.
Meanwhile, odd-dimensional ones are those one
where R /∈Im Λ♯. A characteristic distribution DΛ,R on M is said to be transitive when DΛ,R = TM.
In the case of a Poisson manifold (M, Λ), the characteristic distribution in the Jacobi sense induced by
(M, Λ, R = 0) retrieves the characteristic distribution of the Poisson manifold, which is given by Im Λ♯.
1.7.1
Jacobi manifolds and contact manifolds
This section recalls that every transitive Jacobi manifold on an odd-dimensional manifold amounts to a
so-called co-orientable contact manifold. In particular, this applies to the odd-dimensional leaves of the
characteristic distribution of a Jacobi manifold. Let us recall the definition of a co-orientable contact
manifold. For simplicity, and according to the standard convention in the literature, co-orientable contact
manifolds will be simply called contact manifolds [41].
Definition 1.7.10. A contact manifold is a pair (M, θ), where M is a (2m + 1)-dimensional manifold
and θ is a differential one-form on M satisfying that θ ∧(dθ)m ̸= 0. We call Reeb vector field of (M, θ)
the unique vector field R on M such that ιRθ = 1 and ιRdθ = 0.
A Hamiltonian vector field on a contact manifold (M, θ) is a vector field on M such that there exists a
function f ∈C∞(M) so that f = −ιXθ and
df = ιXdθ + (Rf)θ.

Chapter 1. Fundamentals
33
It can proved that a vector field X on M is Hamiltonian relative to the contact form θ if and only if
LXθ = −hθ for some h ∈C∞(M). In such a case, h = Rf.
Let us show that every odd-dimensional leaf F of DΛ,R, the characteristic distribution of a Jacobi manifold
(M, Λ, R), amounts to a contact manifold. In this case, Rx /∈(Im Λ♯)x at every point x ∈F and thus,
DΛ,R
x
= (Im Λ♯)x ⊕⟨Rx⟩. Consequently, there exists a unique differential one-form θ on F, such that
ιRθ = −1,
ιΛ♯(ξ)θ = 0,
∀ξ ∈Ω1(F).
It stems from Proposition 1.7.5 that ιRdθ = 0. Finally, one has to show that θ ∧(dθ)m ̸= 0. To prove it,
one can use that
ιΛ♯(η)dθ = −(ιRη)θ + η,
∀η ∈Ω1(F).
(1.7.5)
The above formula yields that θ does not belong to the image of the mapping dθ♭: vp ∈TM 7→dθ♭
p(vp, ·) ∈
T ∗M. In fact, if θ = ιΥdθ, for Υ ∈Ω1(F), then (1.7.5) shows that every η ∈Ω1(F) belongs to the image
of dθ♭. This is impossible as the image of every two-form has even rank while F is odd-dimensional.
Moreover, (1.7.5) shows that dθ♭has rank 2m. Therefore, θ ∧(dθ)m ̸= 0.
Conversely, a contact manifold gives rise to a Jacobi manifold as follows. The Reeb vector field of the
Jacobi manifold is assumed to be minus the Reeb vector field, R, of the contact one. Next, Λ is defined
to be the mapping Λ♯: T ∗M →TM determined by the conditions
ιΛ♯(η)θ = 0,
ιΛ♯(η)dθ = η −(ιRη)θ.
In fact, since dθ♭: TM →T ∗M is a 2m-dimensional rank map, the second condition determines the value
of Λ♯(η) up to an element of ker dθ. Since ιΛ♯(η)θ = 0, this fixes exactly the value of Λ♯and, therefore, Λ.
From these conditions, it follows that [Λ, Λ]SN = −R ∧Λ and [R, Λ]SN = 0. A simple way to prove the
latter facts is to write θ = dz −Pn
i=1 pidxi using local canonical coordinates {z, xi, pi} via the Darboux
theorem for contact forms. Then, Λ = −Pn
i=1(∂xi + pi∂z) ∧∂pi and R = −∂z. It is immediate that
[R, Λ]SN = 0, whilst
[Λ, Λ]SN = −2∂z ∧
n
X
i=1
[(∂xi + pi∂z) ∧∂pi] = −2R ∧Λ.
1.7.2
Jacobi manifolds and locally conformal symplectic manifolds
This section shows that every transitive Jacobi manifold (M, Λ, R) on an even-dimensional M amounts to
a so-called locally conformal symplectic manifold (see [174, 171] for details). In particular, this applies to
the even-dimensional leaves of the characteristic distribution of a Jacobi manifold (M, Λ, R). There exists
a plethora of works in the literature dealing with locally conformally symplectic manifolds, in particular,
they appear in the study of Hamilton-Jacobi theories for mechanical systems [29, 72].
Definition 1.7.11. A locally conformal symplectic (l.c.s) form on M is a non-degenerate differential
two-form Θ on M such that
dΘ = η ∧Θ
for a closed-one form η on M, the so-called Lee form [29]. The triple (M, Θ, η) is called a l.c.s. manifold.
It is worth noting that η is unique. A l.c.s. structure (M, Θ, η) allows us to define a new cohomology by
means of the operator dη : Υ ∈Ω(M) 7→dΥ −η ∧Υ ∈Ω(M), i.e. d2
η = 0.
Definition 1.7.12. Given a l.c.s. manifold (M, Θ, η), its Reeb vector field is the unique vector field R
on M such that
ιRΘ = η.

34
Chapter 1. Fundamentals
Definition 1.7.13. A Hamiltonian vector field relative to a l.c.s. manifold (M, Θ, η) is a vector field X
on M satisfying that there exists f ∈C∞(M) such that dηf = ιXΘ.
Since Θ is non-degenerate, every f ∈C∞(M) gives rise to an associated Hamiltonian vector field Xf on
M relative to (M, Θ, η). Moreover, one can endow C∞(M) with a Poisson structure {·, ·}Θ : C∞(N) ×
C∞(N) →C∞(N) by defining
{f, g}Θ := Θ(Xg, Xf) = ιXf dηg,
∀f, g ∈C∞(M).
It is worth noting that Hamiltonian vector fields do not need to be Lie symmetries of Θ. Instead,
LXf Θ = (ιXf η)Θ,
∀f ∈C∞(M).
If ιXf η = 0, we say that Xf is horizontal. Moreover, ιXf η = 0 if and only if Rf = 0. Indeed, since
ιRη = 0, it follows that
Rf = ιRdf = ιRdηf = ιRιXf Θ = −ιXf ιRΘ = −ιXf η.
Assume that the bivector field Λ of a Jacobi manifold (M, Λ, R) such that Λ♯: T ∗M →TM is invertible
and it gives rise to a non-degenerate two-form Θ on M such that Θ♭= (Λ♯)−1. The following proposition
shows how Θ gives rise to a locally conformal symplectic manifold on M (see [172]).
Proposition 1.7.14. Let Θ♭= Λ♯−1, where Λ is the bivector field of a Jacobi manifold (M, Λ, R) and
ι−RΘ = η. Then,
dΘ = [(Λ♯)−1 ⊗(Λ♯)−1]([Λ, Λ]SN) = η ∧Θ,
LRΘ = 0.
Conversely, a l.c.s. manifold (M, Θ, η) gives rise to a transitive Jacobi manifold on M. In fact, the Reeb
vector field R of the Jacobi manifold is the only vector field on M satisfying that ιRΘ = η, while Λ is
the only bivector field on M whose induced Λ♯: T ∗M →TM satisfies that
ιΛ♯(β)Θ = β,
∀β ∈Ω1(M).
Definition 1.7.15. Let (M, Θ, η) be a locally conformal symplectic manifold. A good Hamiltonian vector
field relative to (M, Θ, η) is a vector field on M such that
ιXΘ = dηf,
LXΘ = 0.

Chapter 2
Algebraic methods in the classification
of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
In this Chapter, based on [123], we present a novel algebraic approach to the classification of coboundary
Lie biaglebras. It relies on the so-called g-invariant maps, which allow to identify equivalent r-matrices.
This notion and associated results are discussed at the beginning of the chapter. A separate part of this
chapter is devoted to the analysis of a modified Yang–Baxter equation (mCYBE, for short). We show
that it is sufficient to study this equation in the reduced Grassmann algebra ΛRg := L
n∈N Λng/(Λng)g.
We also demonstrate how other structures can be reduced to this space. Since g-invariant elements play
a notable role in this procedure, we discuss several methods to obtain them. Among others, we focus on
a technique which employs a Lie algebra gradation. Finally, all these tools are tested in the classification
of equivalent r-matrices for three-dimensional Lie algebras. Our results are summarised in Table. We
retrieve the classification presented in [84, 95].
2.1
The g-invariant maps on Grassmann algebras Λg
Many notions introduced for Lie algebras can be extended to Grassmann algebras and even for general
g-modules. Let us focus particularly on the ad-invariance.
Definition 2.1.1. A k-linear map b : V ⊗k →R is GL(ρ)-invariant relative to a g-module (V, ρ) if
T ∗b = b for every T ∈GL(ρ), i.e. b(Tx1, . . . , Txk) = b(x1, . . . , xk) for all x1, . . . , xk ∈V . Moreover, b is
g-invariant relative to (V, ρ) if
b(ρv(x1), . . . , xk) + . . . + b(x1, . . . , ρv(xk)) = 0,
∀v ∈g,
∀x1, . . . , xk ∈V.
(2.1.1)
As explained next, g-invariance can be interpreted as an extension of ad-invariance to arbitrary g-modules.
Example 14. Let us consider the bilinear form κρ on g induced by a g-module (V, ρ) of the form
κρ(v1, v2) := tr(ρv1 ◦ρv2) for every v1, v2 ∈g. It satisfies that κρ(adv(v1), v2) + κρ(v1, adv(v2)) = 0 for all
v, v1, v2 ∈g (cf. [155]). Note that the above notion reduces to the usual Killing form for the g-module
(g, ad). Thus, the Killing form κg is g-invariant relative to (g, ad).
Given a g-module (V, ρ), both introduced notions of invariance for k-linear maps, the invariance relative
to GL(ρ) and g-invariance, are related, as shown in Proposition 2.1.2. The proof, based on the use of the
exponential map, is quite immediate.
35

Chapter 2. Algebraic methods in the classification of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
Proposition 2.1.2. A k-linear map b : V ⊗k →R is GL(ρ)-invariant relative to a g-module (V, ρ) if and
only if b is g-invariant relative to (V, ρ).
Given g-invariant map on a vector space V , one can obtain an associated g-invariant map on a Grassmann
algebra ΛV .

and sg(σ) stands for the sign of a permutation σ ∈Sm.

Corollary 2.1.6. If b is a symmetric g-invariant k-linear mapping on an r-dimensional g-module V , then
bΛV is symmetric. If b is bilinear, then: a) it diagonalises in a basis {e1, . . . , er} and bΛmV diagonalises
in the basis {eJ}|J|=m; b) b is non-degenerate if and only if bΛV is non-degenerate as well.
Proof. If b is a symmetric g-invariant k-linear mapping on V , then the symmetry of bΛmV on decomposable
elements of ΛmV , m ∈N, is guaranteed by condition 3 of Theorem 2.1.3, whereas condition 2 ensures
the same for m = 0. Since bΛV is multilinear, it is symmetric on ΛV .
If b is bilinear and symmetric, it can always be put into diagonal form in a certain basis {e1, . . . , er} for
V . This gives rise to the bases {eJ}m≥|J|≥0 of ΛmV , for m ∈{1, . . . r}, and {eJ}r≥|J|≥0 of ΛV . The
expression (2.1.2) for bΛmV implies that bΛmV is also diagonal. In consequence, bΛV is diagonal as well.
The element bΛV (eJ, eJ) lying in its diagonal, for |J| ≥1, reads Q|J|
j=1 b(eJ(j), eJ(j)). Thus, we conclude
that b is non-degenerate if and only if the induced symmetric bΛmV on each ΛmV is non-degenerate as
well.

2.2
Killing-type forms
The Killing form [88] gives the most crucial example of a symmetric bilinear form, playing an indisputable
role in the structure theory of Lie algebras. In this section we focus on certain multilinear symmetric
maps on the spaces Λmg induced by Killing forms and study their invariance properties. These maps will
be of interest in the description of coboundary cocommutators in further sections.
As shown by Theorem 2.1.3, any g-invariant k-linear symmetric map on g gives rise to the g-invariant
k-linear symmetric map on the Grassmann algebra Λg. For brevity, extended maps obtained via the
method described in Theorem 2.1.3 from the Killing form will be called Killing-type forms. Such forms
share similar properties as the Killing form. The most important one is the invariance relative to the
automorphism group Aut(g), as illustrated by the following proposition.
Proposition 2.2.1. The Killing-type form κΛg is invariant relative to the action of Aut(g) on Λg. In
particular, κΛg is aut(g)-invariant relative to the aut(g)-module (Λg, Λc
ad).

Let us note that the expression (2.2.1) is satisfied for decomposable elements of Λmg, which span Λmg.

If g is semi-simple, then the converse of Theorem 2.2.3 holds and any g-invariant k-linear symmetric
form on g amounts to a Casimir element. Indeed, it is known citeFH91 that the map eκg is a vector
space isomorphism for a semi-simple Lie algebra g over a field of characteristic zero. Thus, any map
b : g⊗k →R can be understood as a mapping C : (g∗)⊗k →R. Then, the computation presented in the
proof of Theorem 2.2.3 ensures that C is a Casimir element.
If g is not semi-simple, eκg is not invertible and the set of g-invariant multilinear symmetric maps is
usually bigger than the set of Casimir elements. In consequence, such maps allow a more detailed study
of r-matrix equivalence in the future.
2.3
Obtaining g-invariant bilinear maps
Although the calculation of g-invariant maps seems to be an easy linear algebra exercise, it often happens
to be computationally time-consuming, especially when dim g is large. In this section, we present several
methods to simplify this task.
Proposition 2.3.1. Let b be a g-invariant bilinear map on a g-module V . Then, b(Im ρv, ker ρv) = 0 for
every v ∈g and x ∈V . Moreover, b(adv(w), w) = 0 for every v, w ∈g. If b is additionally symmetric,
then b(ρv(x), x) = 0 for any v ∈g and x ∈V .
Proof. Every x1 ∈Imρv can be written as x1 := ρv(x3) for some x3 ∈V . Assume that x2 ∈ker ρv. As b
is g-invariant, b(x1, x2) = b(ρv(x3), x2) = −b(x3, ρv(x2)) = 0 and b(Im ρv, ker ρv) = 0. For any v1, v2 ∈g,
we get b(adv1(v2), v2) = −b(adv2(v1), v2) = b(v1, adv2(v2)) = 0. Finally by the symmetricity of b, we
obtain b(ρv(x), x) = −b(x, ρv(x)) = −b(ρv(x), x) for all v ∈g and x ∈V . Therefore, b(ρv(x), x) = 0 for
every x ∈V and v ∈g.
Proposition 2.3.2. Let b : V ⊗2 →R be a g-invariant bilinear map relative to the g-module (V, ρ). If W
is a two-dimensional linear subspace of V satisfying that ρvW ⊂W for any v ∈g, then for any linearly
independent fs, ft ∈W one has
Tr(ρv|W )b(fs, ft)ft ∧fs = b(fs, fs)(ρvft) ∧ft + b(ft, ft)fs ∧(ρvfs).
(2.3.1)
Proof. Since ρvW ⊂W, there exists constants α1, α2, β1, β2 ∈R such that ρvfs = α1fs + α2ft and
ρvft = β1fs + β2ft. The g-invariance of b ensures that
α1b(fs, ft) + α2b(ft, ft) = b(vfs, ft) = −b(fs, vft) = −β1b(fs, fs) −β2b(fs, ft).

Chapter 2. Algebraic methods in the classification of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
41
The above expression gives (2.3.1) after rearranging.

2.4
G-Gradations, Grassmann algebras, and CYBEs
In this section, we discuss another method for an efficient study of mCYBE solutions. It is based on a
particular type of gradation admitted by a Lie algebra g. We show that it induces a decomposition of Λg
which is compatible, in a way to be stated, with the algebraic Schouten bracket.
Definition 2.4.1. We say that g admits a G-gradation if g = L
α∈G⊂Rn g(α), where (G ⊂Rn, ⋆) is a
commutative group, the g(α) are subspaces of g, and [g(α), g(β)] ⊂g(α⋆β) for all α, β ∈G. The spaces
g(α), for α ∈G, are called the homogeneous spaces of the gradation and α is the degree of g(α).
A G-gradation is a type of graded Lie algebra [173]. Obviously, every g admits a trivial Z-gradation
with g(0) = g and g(α) = ⟨0⟩with α ̸= 0. If g admits a G-gradation and G is known from context or of
minor importance, we will simply say that g admits a gradation. It is worth noticing that several results
presented in this section can be extended for G being a semigroup. However, such a generalisation will
not be necessary for our purposes.
Example 19. It stems from the commutation relations in Table 2.1 that su2 is isomorphic to the Lie
algebra on R3 with the Lie bracket given by the vector product in R3, denoted by ×. Consider a basis
{ea, eb, ec} of R3, where ea ∈R3\{0}, the vector eb is perpendicular to ea, and ec := ea × eb. Then,
su2 admits a Z2-gradation g(0) = ⟨ea⟩and g(1) = ⟨eb, ec⟩. Since ea is not unique, su2 admits several
Z2-gradations.

42
Chapter 2. Algebraic methods in the classification of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
Example 20. Consider the Lie algebra sl2 = span(e1, e2, e3) with commutation relations
[e1, e2] = e2,
[e1, e3] = −e3,
[e2, e3] = e1
One can immediately verify that sl2 admits a Z-gradation with g1 = ⟨e2⟩, g−1 = ⟨e3⟩, g0 = ⟨e1⟩and
gα = {0} for any α ∈Z\{−1, 0, 1}.
Example 21. Any complex semisimple Lie algebra g over an algebraically closed field of characteristic
zero admits the so-called root space decomposition [104], namely
g = h ⊕
M
α∈Φ
gα
for a Cartan subalgebra h and a root system Φ.
Among other properties, the subspaces gα satisfy
[h, gα] ⊆gα and [gα, gβ] ⊆gα+β, whenever α + β ∈Φ. It can be shown that the root system gives
rise to the lattice Π in Rn (see [88] for details). In consequence, we can extend the root system to the
whole lattice Π and construct a generalised root space decomposition by associating previously introduces
subspaces gα for α ∈Φ and gβ = {0} for β ∈Π\Φ. Thus, a root space decomposition gives rise to a
Zk-gradation, where k = dim(h).
Further examples of gradations are presented for all three-dimensional Lie algebras in Table 2.1. Addi-
tionally, Figures 2.1 and 2.2 give Z2-gradations for the special pseudo-orthogonal Lie algebras so(3, 2)
and so(2, 2) [57, 102].
Example 21 details a type of gradation that stems from a specific structure of semisimple Lie algebras given
by the root decomposition. Although more general Lie algebras do not admit such a decomposition, it is
sometimes possible to introduce a G-gradation whose properties are close to standard root decompositions.
This kind of gradation will be useful for deriving g-invariant subspaces in Λg.
Definition 2.4.2. A Zk-gradation on a Lie algebra g is called a root Zk-gradation if there exist an
abelian Lie subalgebra g(0) ⊆g and an injective group homomorphism Ξ : α ∈Zk 7→eα ∈g(0)∗such that
dim g(0) = k and [e, e(α)] = eα(e)e(α) for every e ∈g(0) and e(α) ∈g(α).
Since g(0) is abelian, every root decomposition gives rise to a root Zk-gradation.

{f−, f0, f+} are bases of each copy of sl2 within so(2, 2).

As stated in Definition 2.4.1, homogeneous spaces gα, α ∈G, of a G-gradation on a Lie algebra g satisfy
[gα, gβ] ⊆gα⋆β.
Thus, we say that the Lie bracket on g is compatible with the decomposition into
homogeneous spaces. A G-gradation on g induces a decomposition of the Grassmann algebra Λg and its
associated subspaces also enjoy the similar compatibility property relative to the Schouten bracket. The
details of this construction are presented in the following theorem.
Theorem 2.4.3. Let g admit a G-gradation g = L
α∈G⊂Rn g(α). Then, each subspace Λmg of a Grass-
mann algebra Λg := L
m∈N Λmg admits a decomposition Λmg = L
α∈G(Λmg)(α) into so-called homoge-
neous spaces of the form
(Λmg)(α) :=
M
{α1,...,αm}⊂G
α1⋆...⋆αm=α
g(α1) ∧. . . ∧g(αm), m > 0,
(Λ0g)(0) = R.
(2.4.1)
Moreover, the homogeneous spaces are compatible with the algebraic Schouten bracket on Λg, namely
[(Λpg)(α), (Λqg)(β)]S ⊂Λp+q−1g(α⋆β), for p, q ∈Z, α, β ∈G.
Proof. First, let us show that the homogeneous subspaces are well defined. Given any m ≥1 and α ∈G,
let us consider the exterior product e(α1) ∧. . . ∧e(αm) of elements e(αi) ∈g(αi) for every i = 1, . . . , m,
such that α = α1 ⋆. . . ⋆αm. Since G is commutative, such elements belong to (Λmg)(α) regardless of the
order of e(αi) in the exterior product.
Let us assume w ∈(Λmg)(β1) and w ∈(Λmg)(β2) for β1 ̸= β2. For simplicity, we can take w to be
indecomposable. Recall that any indecomposable w ∈(Λmg)(α) is of the form eα1 ∧. . . ∧eαm, where
α1 ⋆. . . ⋆αm = α. Since w ∈(Λmg)(α) ∩(Λmg)(β), we get that α1 ⋆. . . αm = α and simultaneously
α1 ⋆. . . αm = β. This contradicts the assumption α ̸= β.
Moreover, any element of Λmg is a finite sum of indecomposable elements of Λmg, each of which were
belong to a given (Λmg)(α). Hence, Λmg ⊆L
α∈G(Λmg)(α). Conversely, any element of (Λmg)(α) for
arbitrary α ∈G belong to Λmg and thus, any finite sum of such elements belong to Λmg as well. Thus,
L
α∈G(Λmg)(α) ⊆Λmg and in consequence, Λmg = L
α∈G(Λmg)(α).
The above argument justifies that there exists a decomposition of Λmg into homogeneous spaces that is
indeed a direct sum of (Λmg)α.
Finally, let us take the indecomposable elements v ∈(Λpg)(α) and w ∈(Λqg)(β) and consider their
Schouten bracket. Since both elements can be written as v = eα1 ∧. . . ∧eαp for α1 ⋆. . . ⋆αp = α and
w = eβ1 ∧. . . ∧eβq for β1 ⋆. . . βq = β, the Schouten bracket [v, w]S reads
[v, w]S =
p
X
i=1
q
X
j=1
(−1)i+j[eαi, eβj] ∧eα1 ∧. . . ∧c
eαi ∧. . . eαp ∧eβ1 ∧. . . ∧c
eβj ∧. . . ∧eβq
Obviously, each term of the above sum belongs to Λp+q−1. Let us analyse their degrees. Since G is
commutative and [eαi, eβj] ⊆g(αi⋆βj), it follows that the degree of each summand equals
(αi ⋆βj) ⋆α1 ⋆. . . ⋆αi−1 ⋆αi+1 ⋆. . . ⋆αp ⋆β1 ⋆. . . ⋆βj−1 ⋆βj+1 ⋆. . . ⋆βq
= (α1 ⋆. . . ⋆αp) ⋆(β1 ⋆. . . ⋆βq) = α ⋆β.
Therefore, the Schouten bracket [(Λpg)(α), (Λqg)(β)]S ⊆(Λp+qg)(α⋆β).
