The decomposition on a Grassmann algebra Λg, detailed in Theorem 2.4.3, allow us to obtain certain
solutions to CYBE in the following manner.
Given a G-gradation of g, consider an indecomposable
element r ∈(Λ2g)(α) for a certain α ∈G. Since in that case [r, r]S ∈(Λ3g)(α⋆α), it follows that r gives
an r-matrix if (Λ3g)(α⋆α) = 0. The following definition distinguishes the homogeneous spaces of Λ2g that
satisfy this property.
Definition 2.4.5. A limit homogeneous space of a graded decomposition of Λ2g is a homogeneous sub-
space (Λ2g)(α) ⊂Λ2g such that (Λ3g)(α⋆α) = 0.
Thus, elements of limit homogeneous spaces are solutions of the CYBE.
Example 25. Consider the diagram of Λ2so(2, 2) in Figure 2.1 showing its limit homogeneous subspaces.
By direct computation, one can verify that all the elements of these subspaces are solutions of the CYBE.
For instance, consider the middle-right point corresponding to the homogeneous space (Λ2so(2, 2))(0,1) =
⟨e0 ∧e+, f0 ∧e+⟩. Indeed,
[e0 ∧e+, e0 ∧e+]S = [e0, e0] ∧e+ ∧e+ −[e0, e+] ∧e+ ∧e0 −[e+, e0] ∧e0 ∧e+ + [e+, e+] ∧e0 ∧e0 = 0
and similarly for the second element of that space.
Let us consider r1 ∈(Λ2g)(α) and r2 ∈(Λ2g)(β).
If (Λ3g)(α⋆β) = {0}, then any linear combination
of elements r1, r2 is an r-matrix. One can verify this condition easily by examining the decomposition
diagram of Λ2g.
Example 26. Let us consider the homogeneous spaces of Λ2so(2, 2) induced by the root Z2-gradation
on so(2, 2) that we mentioned in previous examples, namely (Λ2so(2, 2))(0,1) = ⟨e0 ∧e+, f0 ∧e+⟩and
(Λ2so(2, 2))(1,1) = ⟨e+ ∧f+⟩. Since (1, 1) + (0, 1) = (1, 2) and the element (1, 2) ∈Z2 corresponds to
an empty homogeneous space of Λ2so(2, 2), any element of (Λ2so(2, 2))(0,1) ⊕(Λ2so(2, 2))(1,1) yields an
r-matrix.
A short calculation shows that f0 ∧e0 is an r-matrix for so(2, 2). This is a particular case of the following
elementary result.
Proposition 2.4.6. If g admits a root Zk-gradation, then Λ2(g(0)) yields a CYBE solution.
Proof. Let us write g(0) := ⟨v1, . . . , vk⟩for any linearly independent elements v1, . . . , vk ∈g(0). As g(0) is
abelian, [w, w′] = 0 for any two elements w, w′ ∈g(0). Thus, [w, vi ∧vj]S = [w, vi] ∧vj + vi ∧[w, vj] = 0
for any i, j ∈{1, . . . , k} and w ∈g(0). Consequently, elements of Λ2g(0) are CYBE solutions.
2.5
Geometry of g-invariant elements
As indicated previously, the determination of g-invariant elements (Λg)g for a Lie algebra g is necessary
for further analysis of CYBE solutions. In this section, we focus our attention on the spaces (Λmg)g of
g-invariant m-vectors. It is mainly devoted to study in more detail the structure of (Λg)g for general Lie
algebras. However, we present some useful observations in two special cases: Lie algebras with a root
gradation and nilpotent Lie algebras. Although these results do not characterise (Λmg)g completely, they
help obtain many of its elements and might simplify noticeably the determination the whole (Λmg)g.
Let us begin with a simple fact concerning the structure of (Λg)g.

46
Chapter 2. Algebraic methods in the classification of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
Proposition 2.5.1. The space (Λg)g is an R-algebra relative to the exterior product and each space
(Λmg)g is aut(g)-invariant.
Proof. It is immediate that (Λg)g is a subalgebra of Λg. By Proposition 2.1.2, the second part of the
proposition amounts to the fact that (Λmg)g is invariant relative to ΛmT for every T ∈Autc(g). Take
w ∈(Λmg)g and T ∈Autc(g). Then for every v ∈g,
[v, ΛmTw]S = [TT −1v, ΛmTw]S = ΛmT[T −1v, w]S = 0
and in consequence, ΛmTw ∈(Λmg)g. Hence, (Λmg)g is Autc(g)-invariant.
The next notion gives further possibilities to study g-invariant elements.
Definition 2.5.2. A traceless ideal of g is a nonzero ideal h ⊂g such that the restriction adv|h to h
of each adv, for v ∈g, is traceless. If the elements ad(v) are traceless for all v ∈g, then g is called
unimodular.
The Lie algebras of abelian, compact, semi-simple, or nilpotent groups are unimodular [5, 30, 97]. In the
next proposition, we observe that traceless ideals give rise to some g-invariant elements.
Proposition 2.5.3. Every traceless ideal h ⊂g is such that Λdim hh ⊂(Λdim hg)g.
Proof. The ideal h gives a one-dimensional space Λdim hh. Let h = ⟨v1, . . . , vm⟩for some linearly inde-
pendent v1, . . . vm ∈g. Then, r ∈Λdim hh is of the form r = v1 ∧. . .∧vm. As h is an ideal, each [v, vj] ∈h
for any v ∈g. Let us write [v, vj] = Pm
k=1 ajkvk for certain coefficients ajk ∈R. It is immediate to notice
that v1 ∧. . . [v, vj] ∧. . . vm = ajjv1 ∧. . . ∧vm and hence,
[v, r]S =
m
X
j=1
v1 ∧. . . ∧[v, vj] ∧. . . ∧vm = Tr(adv|h)r = 0
for every v ∈g. Thus, r ∈(Λdim hg)g.
By Proposition 2.5.3, any traceless ideal in g induces a particular g-invariant element. The following
theorem gives more details on the relation between traceless ideals and elements of (Λg)g.
Theorem 2.5.4. The space spanned by a decomposable Ω∈(Λg)g is in one-to-one correspondence with
a traceless ideal h ⊂g such that ⟨Ω⟩= Λdim hh.
Proof. For any value of m ∈N, a decomposable element Ω∈(Λmg)g takes the form Ω= v1 ∧. . . ∧vm
for some linearly independent v1, . . . , vm ∈g. This defines a subspace h := ⟨v1, . . . , vm⟩⊂g that is
independent of the chosen v1, . . . , vm, and ⟨Ω⟩= Λdim hh. Obviously, all elements of ⟨Ω⟩give rise to the
same h.
Let us prove that h is a traceless ideal. If bΩ: β ∈g∗7→ιβΩ∈Λm−1g, then ker bΩ= h◦. For v ∈g and
θ ∈h◦, we obtain

Thus, ad∗
vθ ∈h◦for every θ ∈h◦. Consequently, advh ⊂h for every v ∈g, which means that h is an ideal
of g. Since [v, Ω]S = (Tr adv|h)Ω= 0, one gets that adv|h is traceless.

Chapter 2. Algebraic methods in the classification of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
47
Conversely, let h be a non-zero traceless ideal. Then, Λdim hh is one-dimensional and it admits a basis,
Ω, given by the exterior product of the elements of a basis of h. Since every adv, with v ∈g, acts on h
tracelessly by assumption, [v, Ω] = (Tr adv|h)Ω= 0 and thus, Ω∈(Λdim hg)g. Obviously, all elements in
⟨Ω⟩belong to (Λdim hg)g.
If a Lie algebra g admits a root gradation, one can restrict the search of g-invariant elements to the
specific homogeneous space of the Grassmann algebra decomposition.
Proposition 2.5.5. If g admits a root gradation, then (Λmg)g ⊂(Λmg)(0).
Proof. Let w ∈(Λmg)g. By Proposition 2.4.4, any w can be written as w = P
α∈G w(α), where each
w(α) ∈(Λmg)(α) ∩(Λmg)g for α ∈G. For every e ∈g(0), one has that [e, w(α)] = eα(e)w(α) = 0 and
therefore, α = 0. Since the mapping Ξ : α ∈G 7→eα ∈(g(0))⋆of the root gradation is injective, α = 0.
Thus, w ⊂(Λmg)(0).
In Section 2.3, we described several methods that simplify the determination of g-invariant forms. When
g admits a root gradation, we obtain new results, detailed below, that help in further analysis of these
forms.
Theorem 2.5.6. Let a Lie algebra g admit a root G-gradation. If b : g⊗2 →R is a g-invariant bilinear
symmetric map on g, then its extension bΛmg : (Λmg)⊗2 →R satisfies bΛmg(v(α), v(β)) = 0 for every
v(α) ∈Λmg(α) and v(β) ∈Λmg(β) with α + β ̸= 0 ∈G.
Proof. Since bΛmg is g-invariant by Theorem 2.1.3, we have bΛmg([h, v(α)]S, v(β)) = −bΛmg(v(α), [h, v(β)]S),
for every h ∈g(0), v(α) ∈Λpg(α), and v(β) ∈Λpg(β). Hence, (eα+ eβ)(h)bΛmg(v(α), v(β)) = 0. Since α+β ̸= 0
by assumption, the injectivity of the map Ξ defining the root gradation implies that (eα + eβ)(h) ̸= 0 for
some h ∈g(0). In consequence, bΛmg(v(α), v(β)) = 0.
Example 27. Let us illustrate Theorem 2.5.6 for Λ2so(2, 2). Using the basis {e−, e0, e+, f−, f0, f+} of
so(2, 2) introduced in Example 22, we obtain that the only non-zero values for the Killing form κso(2,2)
in the chosen basis read
κso(2,2)(e0, e0) = κso(2,2)(f0, f0) = 2,
κso(2,2)(e−, e+) = κso(2,2)(f−, f+) = 2.
Consider the homogeneous spaces (Λ2so(2, 2))(1,1) = ⟨e+ ∧f+⟩, (Λ2so(2, 2))(0,1) = ⟨e0 ∧e+, f0 ∧e+⟩and
(Λ2so(2, 2))(−1,−1) = ⟨e−∧f−⟩. By direct computation one can verify that, for instance,
κΛ2so(2,2)(e+ ∧f+, e0 ∧e+) = κso(2,2)(e+, e0)κso(2,2)(f+, e+) −κso(2,2)(e+, e+)κso(2,2)(f+, e0)
−κso(2,2)(f+, e0)κso(2,2)(e+, e+) + κso(2,2)(f+, e+)κso(2,2)(e+, e0) = 0
and
κΛ2so(2,2)(e+ ∧f+, e−∧f−) = 1
2[κso(2,2)(e+, e−)κso(2,2)(f+, f−) −κso(2,2)(e+, f−)κso(2,2)(f+, e−)
−κso(2,2)(f+, e−)κso(2,2)(e+, f−) + κso(2,2)(f+, f−)κso(2,2)(e+, e−)] = 4.
Corollary 2.5.7. Let g admit a root gradation. If b is a non-degenerate bilinear symmetric form on g,
the restrictions of bΛmg to (Λmg)(0) and Λmg(α) ⊕Λmg(−α) are non-degenerate.

Proof. We prove both results by contradiction.
If the restriction of bΛmg to (Λmg)(0) is degenerate,
there exists a w ∈(Λmg)(0) perpendicular, relative to bΛmg, to every element of (Λmg)(0). Additionally,
Theorem 2.5.6 yields that w is perpendicular to (Λmg)(α) for α ̸= 0 ∈G. Thus, w is perpendicular to
the whole Λmg and in consequence, bΛmg is degenerate. However, Theorem 2.1.6 asserts that the induced
form bΛmg is non-degenerate, hence the contradiction. Therefore, bΛmg is non-degenerate on (Λmg)(0).
Similarly, if w ∈Λmg(α) ⊕Λmg(−α) is orthogonal to Λmg(α) ⊕Λmg(−α), then it stems from Theorem 2.5.6
that w is orthogonal to Λmg, which again contradicts the non-degeneracy of the induced bΛmg.
Example 28. Let {e−∧e0∧e+, e−∧e+∧f0, e0∧f−∧f+, f−∧f0∧f+} denote the basis of (Λ3so(2, 2))(0).

Finally, let us discuss invariant elements for nilpotent Lie algebras. Recall that every nilpotent Lie algebra
g possesses a flag of ideals, called the lower central series of g, defined recurrently as gs) := [g, gs−1)] for
s ∈N with g0) := g. Then, g ⊃g1) ⊃. . . ⊃gp−1) ⊃gp) = {0} for a certain p (see [88] for details). Let
us use this fact to study (Λg)g. Similarly as in Theorem 2.5.4, certain decomposable elements of (Λmg)g
can be characterised by particular Lie subalgebras of g, as shown in the following proposition.
Proposition 2.5.8. Let g be a nilpotent Lie algebra. Then, the space spanned by any decomposable
element Ω∈(Λg)g is in one-to-one correspondece with a non-zero ideal h of g such that Λdimhh = ⟨Ω⟩.
Proof. Since nilpotent Lie algebras are unimodular [97], this result follows from Theorem 2.5.4.
As shown by the next proposition, the knowledge of the lower central series of g allows to obtain some
invariant elements quite immediately. Recall that z(g) denotes the center of Lie algebra g, i.e. a Lie
subalgebra z(g) ⊂g such that [v, w] = 0 for any v ∈g and w ∈z(g).
Proposition 2.5.9. Let g be a nilpotent Lie algebra with gp) = 0 and gp−1) ̸= 0. Then:
• If dim z(g) = 1, then z(g) ∧gp−2) ⊂(Λ2g)g
• If dim z(g) = 2, then Λ2z(g) ∧gp−2) ⊂(Λ3g)g;
• If dim z(g) = 1 and dim gp−2) > 1, then z(g) ∧Λ2gp−2) ⊂(Λ3g)g;
• If dim z(g) = 1 and dim gp−2) = 1, then z(g) ∧gp−2) ∧gp−3) ⊂(Λ3g)g.
Proof. Consider a decomposable element r = a ∧b ∈z(g) ∧gp−2) and assume dim z(g) = 1. Then for any
v ∈g, [v, a ∧b]S = [v, a] ∧b + a ∧[v, b]. Since a ∈z(g), it follows that [v, a] = 0. Moreover, [v, b] ∈z(g)
and as dim z(g) = 1, we obtain a ∧[v, b] = 0. Thus, [v, r]S = 0 and r ∈(Λ2g)g.
Let dim z(g) = 2 and consider an element r = a ∧b ∧c ∈Λ2z(g) ∧gp−2), where a, b ∈z(g) are linearly
independent and c ∈gp−2). Then for any v ∈g, we get [v, r]S = [v, a] ∧b ∧c + a ∧[v, b] ∧c + a ∧b ∧[v, c].
Since a ∈z(g), we have [v, a] = 0 and [v, b] = 0. By the properties of the lower central series, [v, c] ∈z(g).
As dim z(g) = 2, it follows that [v, c] = α1a + α2b for certain α1, α2 ∈R. Thus, a ∧b ∧[v, c] = 0 and
r ∈(Λ3g)g.

Chapter 2. Algebraic methods in the classification of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
49
Let dim z(g) = 1 and consider an element r = a ∧b ∧c ∈Λ2gp−2) ∧z(g), where a, b ∈gp−2) are linearly
independent and c ∈z(g). Then for any v ∈g, we get [v, r]S = [v, a] ∧b ∧c + a ∧[v, b] ∧c + a ∧b ∧[v, c].
Since [v, a] ∈z(g) and [v, b] ∈z(g), we get [v, a] ∧b ∧c = 0 and a ∧[v, b] ∧c = 0. Obviously, [v, c] = 0 as
c ∈z(g). Thus, [v, r]S = 0 and r ∈(Λ3g)g.
Let dim z(g) = 1 and dim gp−2) = 1. Consider an element r = a ∧b ∧c ∈z(g) ∧gp−2) ∧gp−3). Then for
any v ∈g, we get [v, r]S = [v, a] ∧b ∧c + a ∧[v, b] ∧c + a ∧b ∧[v, c]. Obviously, [v, a] = 0 as a ∈z(g).
Since [v, b] ∈z(g) and dim z(g) = 1, it follows that a ∧[v, b] ∧c = 0. Similarly, since [v, c] ∈gp−2) and
dim gp−2) = 1, we get a ∧b ∧[v, c] = 0. Thus, [v, r]S = 0 and r ∈(Λ3g)g.
2.6
Reduced mCYBE
For low-dimensional Lie algebras g, the analysis of mCYBE can be performed by hand, as the dimension
of spaces Λ2g and (Λ3g)g is small. However, if dim g ≥4, mCYBE is frequently very complicated to solve.
In this section, we show that it is sometimes possible to simplify the study of mCYBE by considering its
equivalent version on the quotient space Λm
R g := Λmg/(Λmg)g.
Definition 2.6.1. The elements of Λm
R g, for m ∈Z, are called reduced m-vectors. An equivalence class in
Λm
R g associated with an element w ∈Λm
R g will be denoted by [w]. If r is an r-matrix, then the equivalence
class of r in Λ2
Rg is called a reduced r-matrix.
Many structures discussed in previous sections can be defined on the reduced space Λm
R g. In particular,
the Schouten braket will be of special interest to us.
Proposition 2.6.2. Let πp : wp ∈Λpg 7→[wp] ∈Λp
Rg, with p ∈Z. The algebraic Schouten bracket
induces a new bracket, called the reduced Schouten bracket, on ΛRg := L
p∈Z Λp
Rg, of the form
[[wp], [wq]]R := [[wp, wq]S],
∀wp ∈Λpg,
∀wq ∈Λqg,
(2.6.1)
such that (ΛRg, [·, ·]R) admits a Gerstenhaber algebra structure. In particular, the decomposition ΛRg =
L
p∈Z Λp
Rg is compatible with the reduced Schouten bracket, that is [Λp

Rg]R ⊂Λp+q−1

the projection π := L

r ∈Λ2g is an r-matrix if and only if [π(r), π(r)]R = 0.
Proof. Let us show that (2.6.1) is well defined. If [wp] = [ ¯wp] and [wq] = [ ¯wq] for wp, ¯wp ∈Λpg and
wq, ¯wq ∈Λqg, then wp−¯wp, wq −¯wq ∈(Λg)g. Since [(Λg)g, Λg] = 0, we obtain [[wp], [wq]]R := [[wp, wq]] =
[[wp −¯wp + ¯wp, wq −¯wq + ¯wq]] = [[ ¯wp], [ ¯wq]]R.

any [v] ∈Λp

and

In other words, (ΛRg, [·, ·]R) admits a Gerstenhaber algebra structure.
For any a, b ∈Λg, the definition of π and the reduced bracket give immediately [π(a), π(b)]R = [[a], [b]]R =
[[a, b]S] = π([a, b]S).
If r is an r-matrix, then [r, r]S ∈(Λ3g)g. Thus, [π(r), π(r)]R = π([r, r]) ∈π((Λ3g)g) = 0. Conversely,
since [0] = [π(r), π(r)]R = π([r, r]), it follows that [r, r]S ∈(Λ3g)g.

for any u ∈Λpg, v ∈Λqg, w ∈Λsg. If p = q = 1, the above identity can be written as

2.7
On the automorphisms of a Lie algebra
Further studies of equivalent r-matrices and Lie bialgebra structures require the knowledge of the group
Aut(g) of automorphisms of a Lie algebra g. In this section, we briefly investigate Aut(g) and comment
on relevant features of Aut(g)-invariant metrics on Λg. These remarks will be useful in the remaining
part of this chapter, which focuses on the classification of r-matrices for three-dimensional Lie algebras.
Let us begin our analysis with the study of the Lie algebra aut(g) of the group Aut(g). For that purpose,
recall a relevant property of Lie algebra automorphisms [155].
Proposition 2.7.1. If der(g) is the Lie algebra of derivations of g, then der(g) ≃aut(g).
Derivations of g are elements D ∈gl(g) such that D([v1, v2])=[D(v1), v2] + [v1, D(v2)] for any v1, v2 ∈g
(see [88]). This condition can be solved via computer programs even for relatively high-dimensional Lie
algebras and thus, one can easily determine aut(g).
In consequence, it is also possible to determine
Autc(g), the connected part of the neutral element of Aut(g), by using the exponential map.
However, the determination of the other connected parts of Aut(g) might not be straightforward, espe-
cially when g is not complex and semi-simple [97, 104]. Let us consider the real Lie algebra sl2 to illustrate
the possible difficulties. The Killing form κsl2, given by (2.1.3) in the basis {e1, e2, e3} indicated in Table
2.1, is indefinite with signature (2, 1). The κsl2 induces a quadratic function f(xe1+ye2+ze3) = 2x2+4yz
on sl2. Its level set Sk consists of points (x, y, z), where 2x2 + 4yz = k. If k < 0, then Sk is a two-
sheeted hyperboloid contained in the region of sl2 with z > 0 or in the region of sl2 with z < 0. The
elements of Aut(sl2) are isometries of κsl2 [88]. Thus, the elements of Autc(sl2) leave invariant each
component of each two-sheeted hyperboloid. However, notice that T ∈Aut(sl2) given by T(e1) = −e1,
T(e2) = −e3, and T(e3) = −e2 does not preserve the sign of z. Consequently, it swaps connected parts
of each two-sheeted hyperbolid and T /∈Autc(sl2). Hence, Aut(sl2) is not connected.
The above example shows that the analysis of Aut(g) is a complex task. In consequence, its effectiveness
in the classification problem of Lie bialgebras is relatively restricted (e.g. in [84], the real sl2 case is
studied only up to inner automorphisms). On the other hand, the Aut(g)-invariant bilinear symmetric
maps are easier to obtain. For instance, Proposition 2.1.2 assures such maps must be der(g)-invariant.
This in turn is equivalent to the Autc(g)-invariance of these maps. In practical applications, the Killing
forms and Killing-type forms are mostly employed. These forms are known to be invariant relative to the
whole group Aut(g). Consequently, the induced forms on Λg inherit the invariance property. This fact
follows from the next result, which is a straightforward generalisation of Proposition 2.2.1.
Theorem 2.7.2. If b is a k-linear map on g invariant under Aut(g), bΛmg is invariant under Aut(g).
The Aut(g)-invariance of the induced bΛmg plays a crucial role in the study of equivalent r-matrices.
Consider the level sets, Sk := {v ∈Λmg : bΛmg(v, . . . , v) = k ∈R}. Since bΛmg is Aut(g)-invariant,
bΛmg(ΛmTv, . . . , ΛmTv) = bΛmg(v, . . . , v) for any T ∈Aut(g) and v ∈Sk. Thus, a given orbit of Aut(g)
on Λmg is contained in a single level set Sk. This observation greatly simplifies our analysis of equivalent
elements of λmg. Although these orbits do not need to be connected, it is ensured that only the orbits
within the same level set Sk could contain equivalent elements.
In consequence, we can restrict our
analysis of Aut(g)-action to each Sk.
Given a level set Sk, we first investigate the action of the group Inn(g) of inner automorphisms of g. Inner
automorphisms consist a subgroup of Aut(g) that is relatively easy to compute. Moreover, it informs
about the connected components of Aut(g). Then, it remains to find such automorphism T ∈Aut(g) that
identifies different orbits of Inn(g) within the same Sk. Since each Sk usually contains a small number of
such orbits, this task can be achieved by hand.

52
Chapter 2. Algebraic methods in the classification of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
In order to perform the last step of the procedure described above, let us now provide a few hints to
characterise automorphisms of Lie algebras. If g is a complex simple or semi-simple Lie algebra, then
the quotient group Aut(g)/Inn(g) is isomorphic to the automorphisms of Dynkin diagrams of g [97, 104].
Meanwhile, automorphisms of general Lie algebras cannot be determined so easily. Let us end this section
with few remarks on automorphisms of solvable and nilpotent Lie algebras.
Recall that the derived series of a Lie algebra g are the sequence of ideals defined recurrently by gp) :=
[gp−1), gp−1)] for all p ∈N and g0) = g (see [97]). It follows by induction that Tgp) = gp) for every
p ∈N ∪{0} and T ∈Aut(g). A similar result applies to lower central series. If g is solvable, the elements
of Aut(g) leave invariant the elementary sequence spq := gp) ∧gq), p ≤q, p, q ∈N ∪{0}. If spq ̸= 0, then
spq ⊃slm if and only if p ≤l and q ≤m. Similar results apply to spq := gp) ∧gq), p ≤q, for p, q ∈N∪{0}.
2.8
Study of real three-dimensional coboundary Lie bialgebras
In this section, we use previously devised methods to analyse and to classify, up to Lie algebra automor-
phisms, coboundary Lie bialgebra structures on real three-dimensional Lie algebras. In each case, the
following general scheme is used. First, we employ gradations to obtain g-invariant elements. If possible,
we perform further analysis on the reduced space Λ2
Rg. After computing the g-invariant symmetric forms
bΛ2g, the orbits of Inn(g)-action on level sets Sk of bΛ2g are obtained. Finally, we find automorphisms that
connect separate connected orbits within each Sk. In the last step, we avoid using the full automorphism
group in the classification of Lie bialgebras (cf. [84]). Our results are gathered in Table 2.1.
For each Lie algebra g, its basis will be hereafter denoted by {e1, e2, e3} with commutation relations given
in Table 2.1. We also choose the induced bases {e12, e13, e23} and {e123} in Λ2sl2 and Λ3sl2, respectively.
2.8.1
General properties
In order to facilitate further analysis of coboundary Lie bialgebra structures on three-dimensional Lie
algebras, let us prove a few additional results on the characterisation of (Λmg)g and Aut(g). For the
remaining part of this section, we assume that dim g = 3.
Proposition 2.8.1. Let g be such that κg ̸= 0 and g1) ⊂ker κg is a two-dimensional abelian Lie
subalgebra. If v /∈g1), then every T ∈Aut(g) leaves invariant the set of eigenvectors of adv|g1).
Proof. Let us prove that if T ∈Aut(g), then Tv ∈v + g1) or Tv ∈−v + g1) for every v /∈g1). Since
T ∈Aut(g), one has that κg(Tv, Tv) = κg(v, v). Since v and g1) generate g and T is injective, Tv = λv+h
for an h ∈g1) and λ ∈R\{0}. As g1) ⊂ker κg, then κg(Tv, Tv) = λ2κg(v, v) for every v ∈g. Since κg ̸= 0
, one has that λ ∈{±1}. Hence, Tv ∈v + g1) or Tv ∈−v + g1). Since dim g1) = 2 and g1) is an abelian
ideal of g invariant under Aut(g), one obtains that adv|g1) = ±adT v|g1) = ±T|g1) ◦adv|g1) ◦T −1|g1).
Consequently, if e is an eigenvector of adv|g1), then Te is a new eigenvector of adv|g1).
Proposition 2.8.1 can be helpful to study T|g(1) in various scenarios. For instance, if adv|g1) has two
eigenvectors e1, e2 with different eigenvalues λ1, λ2 satisfying that λ1 + λ2 = 0 and Tv ∈−v + g1), then
T|g1) is an anti-diagonal matrix in the basis {e1, e2}. If λ1 + λ2 ̸= 0 and λ1 ̸= λ2, then Tv ∈v + g1) and
T|g1) is diagonal. Several variations of this reasoning can be applied, e.g. when adv|g1) is triangular.
Proposition 2.8.2. Let Ω∈(Λ3g∗)\{0} and assume that Υ : Λ2g ∋r 7→Ω([r, r]) ∈R is a semi-definite
function (i.e. a non-negative or non-positive function different than zero). Then, every automorphism of
g has positive determinant.

Chapter 2. Algebraic methods in the classification of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
53
Proof. Since dim g = 3, the Ωis a basis of Λ3g∗and there exists a dual basis θ ∈Λ3g. As Ω([r, r]) = Υ(r),
then [r, r] = Υ(r)θ. Since Υ is not identically zero, there exists an r ∈Λ2g such that [r, r] = Υ(r)θ ̸= 0.
If T ∈Aut(g), then Υ(r) det(T) θ = det(T) [r, r] = Λ3T[r, r]. By the properties of the Schouten bracket,
Λ3T[r, r] = [Λ2Tr, Λ2Tr] = Υ(Λ2Tr)θ.
Hence, Υ(r) det(T) = Υ(Λ2Tr). As Υ is semi-definite and
Υ(r) ̸= 0, then det(T) = Υ(Λ2Tr)/Υ(r) > 0.
Finally, the derivation of (Λ3g)g is simplified by the following proposition.
Proposition 2.8.3. Each G-gradation on g has a unique homogeneous subspace (Λ3g)(α) ̸= 0 and
[g(β), (Λ3g)(α)] = 0 for β ̸= 0. If g has a root gradation, then Λ3g = (Λ3g)g if and only if α = 0.
Proof. Since dim(g) = 3, the subspace Λ3g is one-dimensional and thus, it yields a unique nonzero
homogeneous subspace of a certain degree, say α. By Theorem 2.4.3, [g(β), (Λ3g)(α)]S ⊂(Λ3g)(α⋆β). For
β ̸= 0, we get α ⋆β ̸= α. As the only nonzero homogeneous subspace of Λ3g is of degree α, it follows that
[g(β), (Λ3g)(α)]S = 0. Let g admit a root gradation. If Λ3g = (Λ3g)g, then for any v ∈g(0) and w ∈Λ3g,
we get [v, w]S = [Ξ(β1)(v)+Ξ(β2)(v)+Ξ(β3)(v)]w = Ξ(β1+β2+β3)(v) = 0, where βi denote the (possibly
different) degrees of basis elements of g such that β1 + β2 + β3 = α and Ξ is the group homomorphism
introduced in Definition 2.4.2. Then, Ξ(α) = 0. Since Ξ is an injective map, we obtain α = 0. Conversely,
let us take α = 0. Then, [v, w]S = [Ξ(β1)(v) + Ξ(β2)(v) + Ξ(β3)(v)]w = Ξ(β1 + β2 + β3)(v) = Ξ(0)(v) for
any v ∈g(0). Since Ξ is a homomorphism, Ξ(0) = 0 and [v, w]S = 0 for all v ∈g(0).
2.8.2
Lie algebra sl2
The Lie algebra sl2 admits a root decomposition which gives rise to a root Z-gradation such that the
whole Λ3sl2 is a homogeneous space of zero degree. Thus, Proposition 2.8.3 yields that Λ3sl2 = (Λ3sl2)sl2
and every element of Λ2sl2 satisfies the mCYBE. The root gradation of sl2 also implies, by virtue of
Proposition 2.5.5, that (Λ2sl2)sl2 ⊂(Λ2sl2)(0). It is then immediate to verify that (Λ2sl2)sl2 = 0. Thus,
every r ∈Λ2sl2 induces a different cocommutator δr(·) := [·, r]S.
Let us analyse the level sets Sk of fΛ2sl2 : r ∈Λ2sl2 7→κΛ2sl2(r, r) ∈R. If r = xe12 + ye13 + ze23, then
fΛ2sl2(r) := 8xy −4z2 and fΛ2sl2 admits three types of Sk according to the sign of k:
• if k < 0, then Sk is a one-sheeted hyperboloid;
• the level set S0 consists of two cones (without their common vertex), one opposite to the other, and
the origin of Λ2sl2;
• if k > 0, then Sk is a two-sheeted hyperboloid with two parts contained within the region x >
0, y > 0 and x < 0, y < 0, respectively.
The representative level sets of each type are presented in Figure 2.3.
As explained in the previous section, each Sk is the union of different orbits Ow, w ∈Λ2sl2, of the
Inn(sl2)-action on Λ2sl2. Using Proposition 1.3.7, one gets that the dimension of Ow is dim Θ2
w = 2
for w ∈Λ2sl2\{0} and dim Θ2
0 = 0. Since Inn(sl2) is connected, the Ow are two- or zero-dimensional
connected immersed submanifolds. Then:
• for k < 0, each Sk is a single orbit;
• each Sk with k > 0 consists of two connected orbits;
• the S0 has three orbits given by two cones for points with z > 0 or z < 0, and the origin (0, 0, 0).

54
Chapter 2. Algebraic methods in the classification of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
Consequently, there are six families of inequivalent classes of r-matrices on sl2 relative to the action of
Inn(sl2) (cf. [84]). The representatives of each class are r0 = 0, r = ae23, with a > 0 (one-sheeted
hyperboloids), r = a(e12 + e13), with a ̸= 0, (two-sheeted hyperboloids), and r = ±e12 (cones).
Finally, let us search for the automorphisms that would identify the disconnected orbits within each Sk.
The T ∈Aut(sl2) such that T(e1) := e1, T(e2) := −e2, T(e3) := −e3 can be extended to Λ2T giving
rise to a map satisfying that Λ2T(e12) = −e12, Λ2T(e13) = −e13, Λ2T(e23) = e23, This extended map
indetifies two connected parts of the two-sheeted hyperboloids in Sk for each fixed k > 0. It also maps
the two cones contained in S0. This leads to four separate classes of inequivalent r-matrices: r = 0,
r = |k|e23 (for Sk with k < 0), r = k(e12 + e13) (for Sk with k > 0) and r = e12 (for S0).
Our result agrees with [95], but they do not match [84, p. 56]), since the authors performed the classifi-
cation only up to Inn(sl2). However, as shown in the previous section, Inn(sl2) ̸= Aut(sl2).
2.8.3
Lie algebra su2
The Z2-gradations of su2 and the associated decomposition of Λ3su2, both presented in Table 2.1, allow
to show that the space Λ3su2 is su2-invariant. Thus, every element of Λ2su2 is a solution to the mCYBE.
Although the Z2-gradation of su2 implies the whole space Λ3su2 is a single homogeneous space of degree
zero, Proposition 2.8.3 cannot be applied to analyse (Λ2su2)su2, as the considered gradation is not a root
gradation. Since (Λ2su2)su2 = {0}, every r ∈Λ2su2 induces a different cocomutator and the classification
of coboundary cocomutators of su2 up to Aut(su2) amounts to classifying r-matrices.
Let us consider the Killing and Killing-type forms on su2, Λ2su2, and Λ3su2. In the bases given in Table
2.1, these forms read [κsu2] = −2 I3×3, [κΛ2su2] = 4 I3×3, [κΛ3su2] = −8 I1×1, where Ik×k denotes the
identity matrix of size k. Then, the level sets Sk are given κΛ2su2(r, r) = 4(x2 + y2 + z2) = k. Since
Inn(su2) is connected, Proposition 1.3.7 implies that the dimension of the orbits Ow of the Inn(su2)-action
on Λ2su2 satisfies dim Ow = 2 for w ∈Λ2su2\{0} and dim O0 = 0.
As the orbits of the Inn(su2)-action on Λ2su2 are connected immersed submanifolds contained in the level
sets Sk and must be open relative to the topology of each Sk (with k ≥0), which are connected, each
orbit of Inn(su2) must be the whole Sk for each k ≥0.
Hence, non-equivalent r ∈Λ2su2, with respect to the action of Inn(su2), are given by elements ra = ae12,
with a ≥0. Since the orbits of the action of Aut(su2) on Λ2su2 are given by the sum of orbits of Inn(su2)
and they are contained in the surfaces Sk, the orbits of the action of Aut(su2) in Λ2su2 are the spheres
Sk with k > 0 and the point k = 0 (see Figure 2.4). Therefore, we conclude there are two classes of
inequivalent r-matrices: r = 0 and ra = ae12 for a > 0. This agrees with the results given in [84, 95].

2.8.7
Lie algebra r3,1
Since r3,1 admits a root gradation and the unique homogeneous space in Λ3r3,1 is not related to the zero
element of the group (see Table 2.1), Proposition 2.8.3 shows that (Λ3r3,1)r3,1 = {0}. Moreover, the root
gradation of r3,1 yields (Λ2r3,1)r3,1 ⊂Λ2(r3,1)(0). It is then immediate to verify that (Λ2r3,1)r3,1 = 0.
Therefore, one cannot use the reduced space to simplify the analysis and additionally, one has to solve
the mCYBE directly.
In the coordinates {x, y, z} corresponding to the basis {e12, e13, e23} of Λ2r3,1, any r ∈Λ2r3,1 can be
written as r = xe12 + ye13 + ze23. Simple computation shows that [r, r] = 0. Hence, every element of
Λ2r3,1 is an r-matrix.
As we have shown in Example 18, the Lie algebra r3,1 admits no r3,1-invariant forms. Therefore, previously
employed methods does not allow to analyse this case. Nevertheless, it is possible to overcome that
difficulty by studying directly the action of Inn(g), as it is relatively easy to determine this group.
The fundamental vector fields of the action of Inn(r3,1) on Λ2r3,1 are spanned by X1 := x∂x + y∂y +
2z∂z, X2 := −y∂z, X3 := x∂z. Proposition 1.3.7 implies that the distribution D spanned by these vector
fields is singular, as its dimension varies depending on the value of (x, y, z) ∈R3 (e.g. for x = y = 0

58
Chapter 2. Algebraic methods in the classification of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
and z ̸= 0, it equals one, whereas apart from the z-axis it equals two). By Theorem 1.2.3, this singular
distribution is integrable. Let us determine its integral manifolds. Notice that in cylindrical coordinates
(r, ϕ, z), the vector field X1 reads X1 = r∂r + 2z∂z. This observation helps to conclude that off the line
x = y = 0, the integral manifolds of D are two-dimensional semi-planes Iϕ := {(r sin(ϕ), r cos(ϕ), z) ∈
R3 : r ∈R+, z ∈R} for ϕ ∈[0, 2π[, presented in Figure 2.11.
When restricted to the line x = y = 0, the distribution reads X1 = 2z∂z, X2 = 0, X3 = 0.
Then,
one immediately obtains three orbits of the Inn(r3,1)-action: {(x, y, z) ∈R3 : x = 0, y = 0, z > 0},
{(x, y, z) ∈R3 : x = 0, y = 0, z < 0} and the origin {(0, 0, 0)} (see Figure 2.11).
Therefore, we obtain three classes of inequivalent r-matrices relative to Inn(r3,1), namely r = 0, r(±) =
±e23 and rϕ = cos(ϕ)e12 + sin(ϕ)e13 with ϕ ∈[0, 2π[.
Let us study now the equivalence of r-matrices up to the action of Aut(r3,1). Obviously, the r = 0 is an
orbit of the action of Aut(r3,1) on Λ2r3,1. As follows from the final observations in Section 2.7, elements of
Aut(r3,1) leave the first derived ideal [r3,1, r3,1] = ⟨e2, e3⟩invariant. Then, the induced action of Aut(r3,1)
on Λ2r3,1 must leave the subspace ⟨e23⟩invariant and every point within it must be contained in an orbit
within ⟨e23⟩.
Finally, consider the Lie algebra automorphisms Tα,β,γ,δ given by
Tα,β,γ,δ(e1) := e1,
Tα,β,γ,δ(e2) := αe2 + βe3,
Tα,β,γ,δ(e3) = γe2 + δe3,
αδ −βγ ̸= 0,
which gives rise to the induced map Λ2Tα,β,γ,δ of the form
Λ2Tα,β,γ,δ(e12) = αe12 + βe13,
Λ2Tα,β,γ,δ(e13) = γe12 + δe13,
Λ2Tα,β,γ,δ(e23) = (αδ −βγ)e23
Take Tα,β,γ,δ with α = cos(ψ), δ = cos(ψ), γ = sin(ψ) and β = −sin(ψ) for any ψ ∈[0, 2π[. Then,
Tα,β,γ,δ(rϕ) = cos(ϕ)[cos(ψ)e12 −sin(ψ)e13] + sin(ϕ)[sin(ϕ)e12 + cos(ϕ)e13]
= [cos(ϕ cos(ψ) + sin(ϕ) sin(ψ))]e12 + [sin(ϕ) cos(ψ) −cos(ϕ) sin(ψ)]e13
= cos(ϕ −ψ)e12 + sin(ϕ −ψ)e13 = rϕ−ψ.
Since ψ can be chosen arbitrarily, it follows that such Tα,β,γ,δ identifies all semi-planes Iϕ. Moreover,
Tα,β,γ,δ also connects the parts z > 0 and z < 0 of the line x = y = 0.
Hence, there exist three non-equivalent r-matrices for r3,1, namely r = 0, r1 = e13 and r2 = e23.

2.8.8
Lie algebra r3
Since Z-gradation of r3 shown in Table 2.1 is not a root gradation and one cannot use Proposition 2.8.3
to analyse (Λ3r3)r3, it is necessary to directly verify that (Λ3r3)r3 = {0}. Thus, the determination of
r-matrices requires solving the mCYBE.
Let us determine (Λ2r3)r3 to know whether different r-matrices induce different coboundary cocommuta-
tors. Since r3 admits a Z-gradation, (Λ2r3)r3 is the sum of the r3-invariant elements on each homogeneous
subspace of Λ2r3 by Proposition 2.4.4. Using this gradation of r3, one also sees that [v(α), w(β)] = 0, for
v(α) ⊂r(α)
3 , w(β) ∈(Λ2r3)(β) when α + β ̸= 2. Inspecting remaining Schouten brackets, one obtains
(Λ2r3)r3 = {0} and every r-matrix induces a different coboundary cocommutator.
Let {x, y, z} be the coordinates on Λ2r3 induced by the basis {e12, e13, e23}. The mCYBE, where r =
xe12 + ye13 + ze23, reads [r, r] = −2z2e123. Hence, Y B = ⟨e12, e13⟩is the space of solutions to the
mCYBE, presented in Figure 2.13.
A long but simple calculation shows that Λ2r3 does not admit a non-zero r3-invariant metrics. As in the
previous case, we need to carry on the analysis of the orbits of Aut(r3)-action directly. The fundamental
vector fields of the action of Inn(r3) on Λ2r3 are given by X1 := z∂x, X2 := (−y+z)∂x, X3 := 2x∂x+(y+
z)∂y +z∂z. Since automorphisms preserve the set of mCYBE solutions, the above vector fields are tangent
to Y B and their restrictions to Y B read X1|Y B = 0, X2|Y B = −y∂x, X3|Y B = 2x∂x +y∂y. By Theorem
1.2.3, the distribution spanned by X1, X2, X3 is integrable. For y ̸= 0, the integral manifolds are semi-
planes P+ := {(x, y, z) ∈R3 : x ∈R, y > 0, z = 0} and P−:= {(x, y, z) ∈R3 : x ∈R, y < 0, z = 0}. For
y = 0, we get L+ := {(x, y, z) ∈R3 : x > 0, y = 0, z = 0} and L−:= {(x, y, z) ∈R3 : x < 0, y = 0, z = 0}.
Finally for z = y = x = 0, we obtain the single-point orbit {(0, 0, 0)}. All mentioned orbits of Inn(r3) are
depicted in Figure 2.13. Representative r-matrices for these orbits read r = 0, r±
1 = ±e12 and r±
2 = ±e13.
Let us classify coboundary cocommutators up to the action of elements of Aut(r3) on Y B. By the remarks
in Section 2.7, it follows that the derived ideal [r3, r3] = ⟨e1, e2⟩is invariant under the action of Aut(r3).
Thus, ⟨e12⟩is also invariant under the action of Aut(r3) on Λ2r3. In consequence, the orbits L± will not
be identified with any of the semi-planes P±.
Consider T ∈Aut(r3) given by Tα(e1) = αe1, Tα(e2) = αe2 and Tα(e3) = e3. The induced action Λ2T
on Λ2r3 reads Λ2T(e12) = α2e12, Λ2T(e13) = αe13 and Λ2T(e23) = αe23. Consequently, Λ2T identifies
both r±
2 for α = (−1).
Finally, one needs to verify whether there exists an automorphism T of r3 that identifies both r±
1 .
As none of our methods can address this issue, we will try to construct this map explicitly. Denote
T(ei) = P3
j=1 aijej for i ∈{1, 2, 3} and assume Λ2T(e12) = −e12. This implies the following conditions
must hold:
a11a22 −a21a12 = (−1),
a11a23 = a21a13,
a12a23 = a22a13
If both a13, a23 ̸= 0, then it follows that a11a22 = a21a12, contradicting the first equation. If a13 = 0
and a23 ̸= 0, then a11 = a12 = 0, which again contradicts the first equation.
Similarly, one gets a
contradiction for a23 = 0 and a13 ̸= 0. Thus, a13 = a23 = 0.
Since the commutator [e1, e3] = −e1 yields [T(e1), T(e3)] = −T(e1), we get
a11a33 + a12a33 = a11,
a12a33 = a12
If a12 ̸= 0, then a33 = 1 and the first equation gives a12 = 0, contrary to the assumption. Thus, a12 = 0
and we are left with a11a33 = a11. Since a11a22 −a21a12 = (−1) = a11a22, we conclude that a11 ̸= 0 and
a33 = 1.

60
Chapter 2. Algebraic methods in the classification of three-dimensional Lie bialgebras
By similar reasoning, the commutator [e3, e2] = e1 + e2 yields the condition a11 = a22.
But then,
a11a22 = (−1) = a2
11. As we work over the reals, this equation has no solution. In consequence, there
exists no automorphism of r3 such that r±
1 can be identified.
Hence, we have the r-matrices inequivalent relative to Aut(r3) read r0 = 0, r± = ±e12, r = e13, as
depicted in Figure 2.14.

2.8.9
Lie algebra r3,λ (λ ∈(−1, 1))
In view of Proposition 2.8.3 and the fact that r3,λ admits a root gradation and the unique non-zero
homogeneous space in Λ3r3,λ has degree three, one has that (Λ3r3)r3,λ = {0}. The root gradation of r3,λ
also implies that (Λ2r3,λ)r3,λ ⊂(Λ2r3,λ)(0) = {0}.
If we write an element r ∈Λ2r3,λ as r = xe12+ye13+ze23, then the mCYBE reads [r, r] = 2(λ−1)yz e123.
Hence, the space of r-matrices, denoted by Y B, consists of tow intersecting planes, one with y = 0 and
the other with z = 0.
Similarly to the previous case, r3,λ does not admit r3,λ-invariant forms. Thus, we will directly inspect
the orbits of Aut(r3,λ)-action on Y B ⊂Λ2r3,λ.
Let us first classify r-matrices up to the action of
Inn(r3,λ). The fundamental vector fields of the action of Inn(r3,λ) on Λ2r3,λ are given by Z1 := z∂x, Z2 :=
−λy∂x, Z3 := (1 + λ)x∂x + y∂y + λz∂z. We will analyse the distribution D spanned by Z1, Z2, Z3 for
three separate subsets of Y B, namely Y B1 = {(x, y, z) ∈R3 : x ∈R, y = 0, z ̸= 0} , Y B2 = {(x, y, z) ∈
R3 : x ∈R, y ̸= 0, z = 0} and Y B3 = {(x, y, z) ∈R3 : x ∈R, y = 0, z = 0}.
Assume λ ̸= 0. On Y B3, one immediately notices that dim D|Y B3 = 1 for x ̸= 0 and dim D|Y B3 = 0
otherwise. Hence, Y B3 is divided into three orbits of Inn(g), namely {(x, 0, 0) ∈R3 : x > 0}, {(x, 0, 0) ∈
R3 : x < 0} and {(0, 0, 0)}. On Y B1, the distribution D is spanned by Z1 = z∂x, Z3 = (1 + λ)x∂x + λz∂z.
By Proposition 1.3.7, one gets that the orbits of Inn(r3,λ)-action are two-dimensional for z ̸= 0. Thus,
we obtain two separate orbits {(x, 0, z) ∈R3 : x ∈R, z > 0} and {(x, 0, z) ∈R3 : x ∈R, z < 0}. On Y B2,
the distribution D is spanned by Z2 = −λy∂x, Z3 := (1 + λ)x∂x + y∂y. By Proposition 1.3.7, the orbits
of Inn(r3,λ)-action are two-dimensional. Thus, we also obtain two orbits {(x, y, 0) ∈R3 : x ∈R, y > 0}
and {(x, y, 0) ∈R3 : x ∈R, y < 0}.
Therefore, there are seven classes of r-matrices inequivalent relative to Inn(r3,λ), that is r = 0, r±
1 = ±e23,
r±
2 = ±e13, and r±
3 = ±e12.
Let us now classify these r-matrices up to the action of Aut(r3,λ). Since [r3,λ, r3,λ] = ⟨e1, e2⟩is invariant
under Aut(r3,λ), the space ⟨e12⟩is also invariant relative to the action of Aut(r3,λ). Consider the auto-
morphisms of the form Tα,β(e1) = βe1, Tα,β(e2) = αe2, Tα,β(e3) = e3, for all α ∈R\{0}. The induced
map Λ2Tα,β reads Λ2Tα,β(e12) = αβe12, Λ2Tα,β(e13) = βe13, Λ2Tα,β(e23) = αe23. Thus, we conclude

that T1,−1 identifies both r±
3 . Similarly, T1,−1 identifies r±
2 and T−1,1 connects r±
1 . Hence, we obtain four
classes of r-matrices inequivalent relative to Aut(r3,λ), namely r = 0, r1 = e23, r2 = e13 and r3 = e12.
This is depicted in Figure 2.16.
Let us now tackle the case λ = 0, namely r3,0. The analysis of solutions to the mCYBE goes as in the
previous case. The fundamental vector fields of the action of Inn(r3,0) read Z1 := z∂x, Z2 := 0, Z3 :=
x∂x + y∂y. On Y B3, the distribution spanned by Z1, Z2, Z3 has rank one for x ̸= 0 and zero for x = 0.
Therefore, we obtain three orbits: {(x, 0, 0) ∈R3 : x > 0}, {(x, 0, 0) ∈R3 : x < 0}, and {(0, 0, 0)}. In
consequence, we get three classes of r-matrices: r = 0 and r±
1 = ±e12.
Restricting the fundamental fields to Y B1, we get Z1|Y B1 = z∂x, Z2|Y B1 = 0, Z3|Y B1 = x∂x, which span
⟨∂x⟩. Thus, the orbits of the action of Inn(r3,0) on this space are the lines Lz0 = {(x, 0, z0) ∈R3 : x ∈R}
with a constant z0 ̸= 0. It gives the family of r-matrices of the form ra = ae23 with a ∈R\{0}.
Finally, the restriction of Z1, Z2, Z3 to Y B2 gives Z2|Y B2 = y∂x, Z3|Y B2 = x∂x + y∂y. Since y ̸= 0,
Proposition 1.3.7 yields that the orbits of Inn(r3,0)-action are two-dimensional. Thus, there are two such
orbits, i.e.
{(x, y, 0) ∈R3 : x ∈R, y > 0} and {(x, y, 0) ∈R3 : xinR, y < 0}.
The representative
r-matrices of these orbits are r±
2 = ±e13.
Consider the maps Tα,β,γ ∈Aut(r3,0) of the form Tα,β,γ(e1) := αe1 + γe2, Tα,β,γ(e2) := βe2, Tα,β,γ(e3) :=
e3, with α, β ∈R\{0} and γ ∈R. The induced map Λ2Tα,β,γ read Λ2Tα,β,γ(e12) = αβe12, Λ2Tα,β,γ(e13) =
αe13 and Λ2Tα,β,γ(e23) = βe23. It is easy to verify that T−1,1,1 identifies both elements in r±
1 and r±
2 .
Moreover, the maps T1,β,1 for β ∈R\{0} identify all the elements in the family ra. Hence, we get the
following classes of inequivalent r-matrices: r = 0, r1 = e12, r2 = e13 and rz = e23, as shown in Figure
2.16.

By direct calculation one obtains that there are no r′
3,λ-

3,λ.
Then, we resort to the di-
rect analysis of the orbits of Aut(r′
3,λ). The fundamen-
tal vector fields of the Inn(r′
3,λ)-action on Λ2r′
3,λ are
spanned by Z1 = (y + λz)∂x, Z2 = (z −λy)∂x and
Z3 = 2λx∂x + (λy + z)∂y + (λz −y)∂z. On Y B, these
vector fields reduce to a single nonzero Z3|Y B = 2λx∂x.
Thus, we immediately conclude that there are three orbits
of Inn(r′
3,λ)-action on Y B, namely {(x, 0, 0) ∈R3 : x > 0},
{(x, 0, 0) ∈R3 : x < 0} and {(0, 0, 0)}. Thus, we obtain
two classes of r-matrices inequivalent relative to Inn(r′
3,λ),
namely r = 0 and r± = ±e12. Finally, let us attempt to
construct the automorphism T ∈Aut(r′
3,λ) that would
connect r±. Let us assume the induced map Λ2T satisfies
Λ2T(e12) = −e12.
Denote T(ei) = P3
j=1 aijej for i ∈{1, 2, 3}. Then, the condition Λ2T(e12) = −e12 yields a13 = a23 = 0.
One can show by a long computation that [T(e1), T(e3)] = T([e1, e3]) and [T(e3), T(e2)] = T([e3, e2])
result in the following set of equations:
(a33 −1)(a11 + a22) = 0,
(a33 −1)(a21 −a12) = 0,
(1 + λ2)a11a33 = λ2a11 −λa12 −λa21 + a22,
(1 + λ2)a12a33 = λa11 −a12 −λ2a21 −λa22.
If a33 ̸= 1, then a11 = −a22, a12 = a21 and we get
(1 + λ2)a11a33 = a11(λ1 −1) −2λa12,
(1 + λ2)a12a33 = 2λa11 + a12(λ2 −1).
It follows that a12[(1+λ2)a33 −(λ2 −1)]2 = −4λ2a12. Since we work over reals, [(1+λ2)a33 −(λ2 −1)]2 =
−4λ2 has no solutions for a33 and a12 = 0. In consequence, the third equation gives a11 = 0, contradicting
that a11a22 −a12a21 = (−1).
Similarly, assume a33 = 1. Then, we have
(1 + λ2)a11 = a11(λ1 −1) −2λa12,
(1 + λ2)a12 = 2λa11 + a12(λ2 −1).
These equations can be rewritten as a11 = −λa12 and a12 = λa11. Then, a11 = −λ2a11 and a11 = 0 as
λ > 0. Consequently, a12 = 0 and this again contradicts the condition a11a22 −a12a21 = (−1).
Therefore, there are no automorphisms of r′
3,λ such that r± can be identified. Hence, we get the following
classes of r-matrices inequivalent relative to Aut(r′
3,λ): r = 0 and r± = ±e12, illustrated in Figure 2.17.

2.9
A four-dimensional case
In the previous section, we used the methods introduced at the beginning of this chapter to classify
coboundary Lie bialgebra structure on three-dimensional Lie algebras. As it is the simplest nontrivial case
that has been thoroughly studied [84, 95], it mostly served as a benchmark for the introduced formalism.
In order to illustrate that our techniques facilitate the analysis of higher-dimensional examples, let us
classify coboundary Lie bialgebra structures on gl2.

For p = (x, y, z, ˙x, ˙y, ˙z) ∈TR, an easy but tedious calculation gives that Dp = ⟨(X1)p, (X2)p, (X3)p⟩
has dimension three if and only if the subspace of R3 spanned by the vectors (−x, −y, z) and ( ˙x, ˙y, ˙z)
is two-dimensional. Moreover, dim D0 = 0 and dim Dp = 2 elsewhere. The connected components of

Chapter 3
Darboux families and the classification
of Lie bialgebras
This chapter introduces a new concept, the so-called Darboux family, which is employed to determine,
to analyse geometrically, and to classify up to Lie algebra automorphisms, in a relatively easy man-
ner, coboundary Lie bialgebras on real four-dimensional indecomposable Lie algebras.
The Darboux
family notion can be considered as a generalisation of the Darboux polynomial for a vector field. The
classification of r-matrices and solutions to classical Yang-Baxter equations for real four-dimensional in-
decomposable Lie algebras is also given in detail. Our methods can further be applied to general, even
higher-dimensional, Lie algebras. As a byproduct, a method to obtain matrix representations of certain
Lie algebras with a non-trivial center is developed.
3.1
On the notion of Darboux family
On a finite-dimensional vector space E, a polynomial function is a polynomial expression on a set of
linear coordinates on E. A Darboux polynomial, P, for a polynomial vector field X on E is a polynomial
function on E so that XP = fP for a certain polynomial f on E, the so-called cofactor of P relative to
X [59, 117]. It is worth noting that, geometrically, one cannot define intrinsically what a polynomial on
a general manifold is, as the explicit form of a function on a manifold depends on the chosen coordinate
system. However, one can introduce the following generalisation of a Darboux polynomial on manifolds.
Definition 3.1.1. A Darboux family for a Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra V on a manifold M is an
s-dimensional linear space A spanned by certain functions f1, . . . , fs ∈C∞(M) such that one can write
Xfj =
s
X
i=1
hi
jXfi,
∀j = 1, . . . , s
for every vector field X ∈V and certain smooth functions hi
jX ∈C∞(M). We call each hi
jX a co-factor
of fj relative to X and the basis {f1, . . . , fs}. The subset ℓA := {p ∈M : f(p) = 0, ∀f ∈A} is called the
locus of the Darboux family A.
If all the functions hi
jX are equal to zero for every X ∈V and i = 1, . . . , s, then fj becomes a constant
of motion for the vector fields of V on M. If the hi
jX are constants for every X ∈V and i, j = 1, . . . , s,
then we say that the Darboux family is linear. In this case, V gives rise to a Lie algebra representation
ρ : X ∈V 7→DX ∈End(A), where DX stands for the action of the vector field X on the space of
functions A.
69

70
Chapter 3. Darboux families and the classification of Lie bialgebras
The vector fields of V span a generalised distribution DV , which is integrable by Theorem 1.2.3. This leads
to a stratification of M by some disjoint immersed submanifolds which may have different dimensions.
Lemma 3.1.2. For a Darboux family A, its locus ℓA is the sum (as subsets of M) of a collection of
strata of the generalised distribution DV .
Proof. Let us first show that the integral curves of vector fields in V passing through the locus ℓA of the
Darboux family A remain within A. Indeed, take a point in the locus ℓA and consider a basis Y1, . . . , Yq
of the Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra V associated with A. Then, each function fi ∈A satisfy

From this fact and the decomposition (1.2.1), it follows that the functions f1, . . . , fs vanish on the strata
of the distribution DV containing a point within ℓA. In other words, the strata of DV containing some
point of ℓA are fully contained in ℓA. In consequence, the locus ℓA must be the sum (as subsets of M)
of a collection of strata of the generalised distribution DV .
In view of Lemma 3.1.2, one can separate the analysis of the strata in M of the generalised distribution
DV to two regions of M: the locus ℓA and its completion M\ℓA. This approach will be particularly
useful to obtain the strata of DV at points where the generalised distribution is not regular and it may
not exist a constant of motion common to all the vector fields in V that can be used to obtain the strata
of DV .

3.1.3, the space of functions (3.1.3) becomes a linear Darboux family for Vg on Λ2g.
The locus of (3.1.3) consists of elements r ∈Λ2g such that fv(r) = v([r, r]) = 0 for all v ∈((Λ3g)g)◦.
Hence, [r, r] ∈(Λ3g)g, which means that r is a solution of the mCYBE for g. Conversely, any solution
r ∈Λ2g to mCYBE satisfies [r, r] ∈(Λ3g)g. Then, it follows that v([r, r]) = 0 for any v ∈((Λ3g)g)◦.
Thus, the locus of (3.1.3) is the set of solutions to mCYBE.
Since every X(3) ∈V (3)
g
is linear vector field in a linear coordinate system on Λ3g, the space Λ3g∗is also a
Darboux family for V (3)
g
on Λ3g. By Theorem 3.1.3, one has that the family of functions on Λ2g induced
by (3.1.2) is a linear Darboux family relative to Vg on Λ2g. The Darboux family is indeed spanned by
the components of the CYBE of g, i.e. [r, r] = 0, and its locus is its set of solutions.
Finally, let us provide several results on the determination of Darboux families.
Proposition 3.1.5. If A1 and A2 are Darboux families for the same Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra V on
a manifold M, then the sum A1 + A2 is a Darboux family for V on M as well.

This fact will be used to

In next sections, an important role is played by the linear Darboux families for a certain Vg given by
a one-dimensional A ⊂(Λ2g)∗.
We will call such a linear one-dimensional Darboux family a brick.
From the practical standpoint, bricks are quite easy to analyse due to their dimension and moreover,
by Proposition 3.1.5, one can efficiently construct more complex and useful Darboux families with such
one-dimensional objects. Indeed, for every linear Darboux family for Vg, bricks are easy to obtain since,
in view of (1.3.2), they are given by the intersection of an eigenvector space for each endomorphism of
the form Λ2d ∈gl(Λ2g) with d ∈der(g).
Let us give another interesting proposition, which is an immediate consequence of the fact that the vector
fields of Vg are linear relative to a linear coordinate set on Λ2g.
Proposition 3.1.6. The space Λ2g∗is a linear Darboux family of functions on Λ2g relative to the Lie
algebra Vg.
Since Λ2g∗is a linear Darboux family of Vg, there exists a linear representation of aut(g) on Λ2g∗. Its
irreducible representations also give rise to Darboux families, which can be summed (as linear spaces)
to the Darboux family, or potentially elements thereof, associated with the mCYBE to determine new
Darboux families. The locus of such sums will be interested to as when they contain elements of Yg. This
will be employed to obtain the strata of Vg within Yg and, therefore, inequivalent r-matrices relative to
the action of Autc(g) on Λ2g. This in turn will be used to obtain families of inequivalent r-matrices and
coboundary cocommutators for real four-dimensional indecomposable Lie algebras [170] in Section 3.3.
3.2
Geometry of mCYBE solutions and Darboux families
As reminded throughout this thesis, the classification of equivalent r-matrices for the Lie algebra g
amounts to the study of the orbits of the action of Aut(g) on the space Yg ⊂Λ2g of solutions to mCYBE.
Since this task is not elementary and it cannot be accomplished efficiently by straightforward brute-force
approach, we developed in Chapter 2 several techniques based on g-invariant forms to facilitate the work
on this problem.
In this section, we attempt the analysis of the orbits of the Aut(g)-action on Yg within the geometric
framework. We detail some observations regarding the geometry of Yg and most importantly, we discuss
the role of Darboux families in the study of such orbits.

Chapter 3. Darboux families and the classification of Lie bialgebras
73
As previously, we hereafter write Vg for the Lie algebra of fundamental vector fields of the action of
Aut(g) on Λ2g and Eg stands for the distribution on Λ2g spanned by the vector fields of Vg. Recall that
Λ2
Rg := Λ2g/(Λ2g)g and πg : w ∈Λ2g 7→[w] ∈Λ2
Rg is the canonical projection onto the quotient space
Λ2
Rg. Finally, Yg denotes the space of r-matrices of g, i.e. solutions to mCYBE.
By Lemma 2.6.3, the action of Aut(g) on Λ2g induces an action of Aut(g) on Λ2
Rg of the form
Ψ : T ∈Aut(g) 7→[Λ2T] ∈GL(Λ2
Rg),
[Λ2T]([w]) := [Λ2T(w)],
∀w ∈Λ2g.
In consequence,
πg((Λ2T)(w)) = [Ψ(T)](πg(w)),
∀w ∈Λ2g,
∀T ∈Aut(g).
(3.2.1)
Let N1, N2 be manifolds and denote the action of a Lie group on N1 and N2 by Φ1 : G × N1 →N1
and Φ2 : G × N2 →N2, respectively. W say that Φ1 and Φ2 are equivariant relative to ϕ : N1 →N2 if
ϕ(Φ1(g, x)) = Φ2(g, ϕ(x)) for every g ∈G and x ∈N1. It can be proved that if Φ1 and Φ2 are equivariant,
the Lie algebra of fundamental vector fields of Φ1 projects via ϕ∗onto the Lie algebra of fundamental
vector fields of Φ2 and moreover, the orbits of Φ1 project via ϕ onto the orbits of Φ2 (see [3] for details).
Expression (3.2.1) shows that the actions of Aut(g) on Λ2g and Λ2
Rg are equivariant relative to πg : Λ2g →
Λ2
Rg. Then, the vector fields of Vg project via πg∗onto the Lie algebra of fundamental vector fields of
the action of Aut(g) on Λ2
Rg. We write E R
g for the generalised distribution spanned by the fundamental
vector fields of the action of Aut(g) on Λ2
Rg. This means that the strata of the generalised distribution
Eg project onto the strata of the generalised distribution E R
g .
As the elements of Aut(g) preserve the space Yg due to the properties of the Schouten bracket, the orbits
of Aut(g) containing a solution to the mCYBE consist of solutions to the mCYBE. Similarly, the orbits
of the action of Aut(g) on Λ2g containing a solution to the CYBE consist of solutions to the CYBE.
Thus, two r-matrices are equivalent up to Lie algebra automorphisms of g if and only if they belong to
the same orbit of Aut(g) within Yg.
Recall that Aut(g)/Autc(g), where Autc(g) is the connected component of the identity of the Lie group
Aut(g), is discrete and therefore countable [44]. In other words, the Lie group Aut(g) is a numerable
sum (as subsets) of disjoint and connected subsets of Aut(g) diffeomorphic to Autc(g). The strata of
Eg coincide with the orbits of Autc(g). Therefore, the orbits of Aut(g) are given by a numerable sum
of strata of Eg. Moreover, each particular orbit of Aut(g) is an immersed submanifold in Λ2g of a fixed
dimension. Hence, each one of the orbits of Autc(g), whose sum gives rise to an orbit of Aut(g), must
have the same dimension. Similarly, the orbits of Aut(g) on Λ2
Rg are given by a numerable sum (as
subsets) of strata of E R
g of the same dimension, which are orbits of the action of Autc(g) on Λ2
Rg.
By Proposition 1.5.8, two coboundary cocommutators δi : v ∈g 7→[ri, v] ∈g ∧g, with i = 1, 2 and
ri ∈Yg, are equivalent under a Lie algebra automorphism of g if and only if there exists T ∈Aut(g) such
that (Λ2T)(r1) and r2 belong to the same equivalence class in Λ2
Rg. Consequently, two r-matrices are
equivalent relative to the action of Aut(g) on Λ2g if and only if they belong to the same family of strata
of the distribution Eg giving rise to an orbit of Aut(g) in Yg.
The following proposition summarises previous considerations.
Proposition 3.2.1. The strata of Eg coincide with the orbits of the action of Autc(g) on Λ2g. Each set of
r-matrices for g that are equivalent relative to Autc(g) corresponds to a single stratum of the generalised
distribution Eg within Yg. Moreover, there exists a one-to-one correspondence between the families of
equivalent (relative to Aut(g)) coboundary cocommutators of g and the orbits of the action of Aut(g) on

74
Chapter 3. Darboux families and the classification of Lie bialgebras
πg(Yg) ⊂Λ2
Rg. Every such an orbit in πg(Yg) is the sum (as subsets) of a numerable collection of strata
of the same dimension of the generalised distribution E R
g .
In practice, we shall obtain each orbit of Aut(g) on Yg as a numerable family of strata of Eg in Yg. Such
orbits represent the families of r-matrices that are equivalent up to an element of Aut(g). Then, we will
derive all strata in Yg that map onto the same space in Λ2
Rg. This last task will give us, along with the
orbits of Aut(g) on Yg, the equivalent classes of coboundary cocommutators on g up to Aut(g).
Finally, let us show how Darboux families can be employed to obtain the orbits of Aut(g) on Λ2g and to
identify the strata of Eg.
Proposition 3.2.2. The locus ℓg of a Darboux family Ag relative to the Lie algebra Vg on Λ2g is a sum
(as subsets) of the orbits of the action of Autc(g) on Λ2g containing a point in ℓg. If ℓg is a connected
submanifold in Λ2g of dimension given by the rank of the generalised distribution Eg, then ℓg is a separate
stratum of the generalised distribution Eg.
Proof. Let E denote a single stratum of a generalised distribution Eg passing through a point p ∈ℓA.
Lemma 3.1.2 implies that all functions of AE
g are equal to zero on E. Thus, E is contained in ℓg. Since
the strata of Eg are the orbits of Autc(g) in Λ2g by Proposition 3.2.1, it follows that ℓg is a sum of strata
of Eg.
If the rank of Eg on ℓg is equal to dim ℓg, where ℓg is assumed to be a connected submanifold, then ℓg is
locally generated around any point p ∈ℓg by the action of Autc(g) on that point. Since ℓ1 is connected,
it is wholly generated by the action of Autc(g) and it becomes a strata of Eg.
Note that we are interested only in those loci of Darboux families of Vg contained in the space Yg of
solutions to the mCYBE of g.
3.3
Study of coboundary Lie bialgebras on four-dimensional in-
decomposable Lie algebras
A Lie algebra g is decomposable when it can be written as the direct sum of two of its proper ideals. Other-
wise, we say that g is indecomposable. Winternitz and Šnobl classified in [170, Part 4] all indecomposable
Lie algebras up to dimension six (see [170, p. 217] for comments on previous classifications) . The aim
of this section is to present the classification, up to Lie algebra automorphisms, of coboundary Lie bial-
gebras on real four-dimensional indecomposable Lie algebras (according to the Winternitz–Šnobl (WŠ)
classification) via Darboux families. Moreover, the equivalence of r-matrices and solutions to CYBEs for
these Lie algebras are also analysed.
We hereafter assume g to be a real four-dimensional and indecomposable Lie algebra. For the sake of
completeness, the structure constants for every g in a basis {e1, e2, e3, e4} are given in Table 3.1. We
hereafter endow Λ2g with a basis {e12, e13, e14, e23, e24, e34} with eij := e1 ∧ej, while its dual basis
is denoted by {x1, x2, x3, x4, x5, x6}. Additionally, Λ3g is endowed with a basis {e123, e124, e134, e234},
where eijk := ei ∧ej ∧ek. The classes of Lie algebras in Table 3.1 may contain several non-isomorphic
Lie algebras depending on several parameters, e.g. α, β. To simplify the notation, we will not detail
specific values of the parameters when we refer to properties of a whole class of Lie algebras or when the
particular case we are discussing is known from the context.
Finally, it is worth mentioning some physical and mathematical applications of the Lie bialgebras given in
our list and their relations to some previous works. For instance, the nilpotent Lie algebra n1 corresponds

Lie bialgebras defined on Lie algebras of the type n1 also occur in
[137, 141]. The coboundary Lie bialgebras on the Lie algebra s6, which corresponds to the so-called
harmonic oscillator algebra, are obtained in [18] without studying the equivalence up to Lie algebra
automorphisms. In that work, the quantisations and R-matrices of such a sort of Lie bialgebras are
also studied. In particular, equation (3.7) in [18] matches the mCYBE obtained in Section 3.3.7. The
work [19] proves that all Lie bialgebras on the harmonic oscillator algebra are coboundary ones. This
shows that our classification of Lie bialgebras on s6 finishes the classification of all Lie bialgebras on the
harmonic oscillator algebra. On the other hand, the Lie algebra s8 with α = −1/2 is isomorphic to the
Lie algebra A ¯G2(1) in [137]. The Lie algebra s7 provides a central extension of Caley-Klein Lie algebras
analysed in [25, eq. (3.13)]. Other cases of real four-dimensional indecomposable Lie bialgebras can be
found in [12, 142], where, for instance, two-dimensional central extended Galilei algebras are studied.
3.3.1
General classification scheme
Recall that by Proposition 3.2.2, the locus of a Darboux family for a Lie algebra Vs1 is the sum (as
subsets) of strata of the generalised distribution Es1 spanned by the vector fields of Vs1. Moreover, these
loci that form a connected submanifold of Λ2s1, whose dimension is equal to the rank of Es1, are the
orbits of Autc(s1) on Ys1, the space of r-matrices for s1.
For a given Darboux family A1 of Vg on Λ2g, let us consider its locus ℓ1.
As we are interested in
determining the strata of Eg within Yg, we look for an ℓ1 containing some solution to the mCYBE,
namely ℓ1 ∩Yg ̸= ∅. Recall that the strata of Eg are completely contained within the locus or completely
contained off the locus of every Darboux family for Vg. This divides the strata of Eg on Yg into those
within or outside ℓ1. If ℓ1 ∩Yg is a submanifold of dimension given by the rank of Eg on it, its connected
parts are the strata of Eg in ℓ1 ∩Yg. Otherwise, to obtain the strata within ℓ1 ∩Yg, we look for a new

76
Chapter 3. Darboux families and the classification of Lie bialgebras
Darboux family A2 for Vg containing the previous Darboux family A1 so that its new locus, ℓ2, will
satisfy ℓ2 ∩Yg ̸= ∅. This process is repeated until we obtain a Darboux family Ak, whose locus ℓk is a
submanifold of dimension equal to the rank of Eg on ℓk. Then, the connected components of the locus
ℓk are the orbits of Autc(g) in ℓk ∩Yg.
In the next step, we determine all the strata of Eg on Yg∩(ℓk)c, where (ℓk)c stands for the complementary
subset of ℓk in Λ2g, which is always open. For that purpose, one considers a subspace of the final Darboux
family Ak and the previous procedure is applied again iteratively to the obtained Darboux family on
Λ2g\ℓk. This leads to another family of strata of Eg and the procedure can be repeated to search for
remaining strata of Eg in Yg. In the end, the process described above allows us to obtain the orbits of
Autc(g) on Yg for all four-dimensional indecomposable Lie algebras g.
Our procedure can be represented in a diagram, which is hereafter called a Darboux tree. Every Darboux
family of the above-mentioned method is described by a collection of boxes going from an edge of the
diagram on the left to one of the edges on the right. The squared boxes of the type f = 0, for a certain
function f, give the generating functions of the Darboux family while the squared boxes of the type
f ̸= 0, give the conditions restricting the manifold in Λ2g where the Darboux family and Vg are restricted
to. The connected parts of the loci of all the Darboux families represented in a Darboux tree give rise to
a decomposition of Yg into orbits of Autc(g). We use sometimes oval boxes with additional information
that helps undertand the ongoing calculations. In many cases considered in this section, bricks were
employed to generate Darboux families.
3.3.2
Lie algebra s1
Using the commutation relations for s1 given in Table 3.1, one verifies directly that (Λ2s1)s1 = ⟨e12⟩and
(Λ3s1)s1 = 0.
By Remark 1.3.9, the Lie algebra Vs1 of fundamental vector fields of the natural Lie group action of
Aut(s1) on Λ2s1 is spanned by the basis (over the reals)
X1 = 2x1∂x1 + x2∂x2 + x3∂x3 + x4∂x4 + x5∂x5,
X2 = x4∂x2 + x5∂x3,
X3 = −x5∂x1 −x6∂x2,
X4 = x3∂x1 −x6∂x4,
X5 = x2∂x2 + x4∂x4 + x6∂x6,
X6 = x3∂x2 + x5∂x4.
(3.3.1)
As X1, . . . , X6 close on a finite-dimensional Lie algebra, Theorem 1.2.3 shows that they span an integrable
generalised distribution Es1 on Λ2s1.
We define M(p) to be a matrix whose entry (i, j) is the j-coefficient of Xi at p ∈Λ2s1 in the basis
∂x1|p, . . . , ∂x6|p, namely

It is easy to verify that the rank of M(p) for points p ∈ℓB is not constant. Thus, ℓB still consists of
orbits of Autc(s1)-action of different dimension and we need to search for more complex Darboux families
containing B in order to fully determine them.
Let B1 := ⟨x5, x6, x3, x4⟩⊂B. Then, B1 is a Darboux family and its locus ℓB1 ⊂Ys1. The inspection of
the matrix M(p) for p ∈ℓB1 shows that its rank is again not constant. We reach the same conclusion for
B2 := ⟨x5, x6, x3, x4, x2⟩⊂B1.
Finally, take B3 := ⟨x5, x6, x3, x4, x2, x1⟩⊂B2. Its locus ℓB3 ∈Ys1. One sees immediately that ℓB3 is a
submanifold of dimension zero and moreover, rkM(p) = 0 for p ∈ℓB3. Thus, ℓB3 gives a separate orbit of
the action of Autc(s1) on Λ2s1, which is described by the conditions in the first row of the Darboux tree.
Let us consider the complement ℓB2\ℓB3, given by the conditions x5 = x6 = x3 = x4 = x2 = 0 and x1 ̸= 0.
Obviously, it consists of two connected submanifolds of dimension one. Additionally, one checks directly
that rkM(p) = 1 for points in ℓB2\ℓB3. Then, it follows that both connected components of ℓB2\ℓB3 give
rise to new orbits of Autc(s1), denoted in the Darboux tree by the index I.
In the next step, we consider the complement ℓB1\ℓB2, given by the conditions x5 = x6 = x3 = x4 = 0
and x2 ̸= 0. By direct computation, one verifies that the rank of M(p) for p ∈ℓB1\ℓB2 is not constant.
This means we need to find a new Darboux family on ℓB1\ℓB2. This is satisfied by the subspace ⟨x1⟩.
One sees that the locus x1 = 0 consists of two connected parts in ℓB1\ℓB2 of dimension one. Meanwhile,
rkM(p) = 1 for p ∈ℓB1\ℓB2. Similarly, the complement x1 ̸= 0 in ℓB1\ℓB2 has four connected components
of dimension two, which equals the rank of M(p) on this subspace. Both cases yield separate orbits of
Autc(s1) (denoted by II and III in the Darboux tree).
Similar analysis is carried out for ℓB\ℓB1. As the matrix M(p) is not constant on ℓB\ℓB1, we introduce
the new Darboux family ⟨x1⟩on ℓB\ℓB1. One computes as in previous cases the dimension of its locus
on ℓB\ℓB1 and verifies that the rank of M(p) on this subset is the same. Analogously, one obtains that
rkM(p) = 3 for the complement x1 ̸= 0 in ℓB\ℓB1 and it equals the dimension of the four connected
components of this subset, which give rise to the orbits of Autc(s1) denoted by IV and V.
Consider the complement ℓA12\ℓB. On this subset, the subspace ⟨x4⟩defines a Darboux family such that
its locus belongs to Ys1. Meanwhile, its complement in ℓA12\ℓB does not, which is noted in the oval box.

78
Chapter 3. Darboux families and the classification of Lie bialgebras
Moreover, rkM(p) = 3 for the points in the locus and it is equal to its dimension. In consequence, only
the locus gives rise to orbits of Autc(s1), denoted by VI.
Finally, we are left with the last region of Λ2s1 that has not been considered, namely the complement
ℓA1\A12. We consider the Darboux family ⟨x3⟩on this subset. It is straightforward to see that only its
locus belongs to Ys1. Further analysis for the new Darboux family ⟨x3, x1⟩follows as in the previous
cases and leads to the last two strata containing orbits of dimension four and three (denoted by VII and
VIII, respectively).

3.3.4
Lie algebra s3
The structure constants of the Lie algebras of type s3 are given in Table 3.1. It was noted in [170, p.
228] that additional restrictions must be imposed on the parameters α, β given in Table 3.1 to avoid the
repetition of isomorphic Lie algebras within the Lie algebra class s3. Such restrictions were not explicitly
detailed in [170], but it is immediate that isomorphic cases within Lie algebras of the class s3 can be
classified by the adjoint action of e4 on ⟨e1, e2, e3⟩. In particular, if |α| = |β|, then the Lie algebras with
parameters (α, β) and (β, α) are isomorphic relative to the Lie algebra isomorphism that interchanges e2
with e3 and leaves e1 invariant. Due to the previous isomorphism, we restrict ourselves to the case α ≥β
when |α| = |β|.
Using the structure constants given in Table 3.1, one gets that (Λ2s3)s3 ⊂{e12, e13, e23}. More specifically,
e12 ∈(Λ2s3)s3 for α = −1, while e13 ∈(Λ2s3)s3 for β = −1. Finally, e23 ∈(Λ2s3)s3 for α + β = 0.
Moreover, (Λ3s3)s3 = ⟨e123⟩if α + β = −1. Otherwise, (Λ3s3)s3 = 0.

Chapter 3. Darboux families and the classification of Lie bialgebras
81
Since the Lie algebra class s3 contains so many subcases that relevant properties may change from one to
another subcase with given parameters (α, β), e.g. the structure of the Lie algebra automorphism group,
we develop here a modification of our classification scheme.
First, we apply the Darboux family method to the fundamental vector fields of the action of the Lie group
Autall(s3) of common Lie algebra automorphisms for all parameters α, β. In the next step, we analyse
its relation to the Lie algebra automorphisms for each case, sα,β
3
, to obtain our final classification. We
omit writing the parameters α, β in sα,β
3
whenever their values are irrelevant for the argument or if this
information was communicated differently.

values of α and β, read:

Let us finalise the analysis of the orbits of Autc(gl2) by considering the last region of R6 denoted in the
Darboux tree by the blue box. It can be easily verified that in this case, the functions f := 2x1x2 −x2

and g := 2x5x6 + x3 give a Darboux family. Moreover, the level sets f = k1 for all values k1 ∈R form a
foliation of R3 spanned by x1, x2, x4. Similar situation happens for g = k2 with any k2 ∈R. Thus, we
can restrict the analysis of the orbits to any pair of level sets f = k1, g = k2 for arbitrary k1, k2 ∈R. In
consequence, we get nine qualitatively different cases depending on the sign of k1 and k2.

In each case, we compute the rank of the distribution Dgl2 spanned by the vector fields Vgl2, which
informs us about the dimension of the orbits of the action of Autc(gl2) on the analysed subset of Λ2gl2.
For solutions 1)-6) we obtain rkDgl2 = 2, whereas we get rkDgl2 = 3 in the case 7). Thus, we conclude
that each presented solution is a single orbit of Autc(gl2).

Chapter 3. Darboux families and the classification of Lie bialgebras
By applying the elements of Aut(gl2), we can identify some of the representative elements listed before.
Moreover, it can be verified by direct computations that the level set f = k1 is invariant under the action
of Aut(gl2), whereas the level sets g = k2 with the same sign of k2 can be connected by an element of
Aut(gl2). Thus, we obtain the following families of inequivalent r-matrices:
rα<0 = e14 + |α|e23,
rα>0 = αe12 + αe13 + e24 −e34,
r0 = e12 + e24.
The remaining representative nonzero elements of classes of r-matrices relative to Aut(gl2) associated
with other Darboux families specified in the Darboux tree for gl2 read
rI,± = e12 + e13 ±
√
2e23,
rII,k>0,± = ±
r
k
2e12 ±
r
k
2e13,
rII,k<0 =
p
|k|e23,
rIII,± = ±
√
2e14 + e24 −e34,
rIV,k=1 = e14,
rIV,k=−1,± = ±e24 ∓e34.
Thus, we obtain the following classes of r-matrices relative to Aut(gl2):
r1,α>0,± = ±αe12 ± αe13,
r2,α>0 = αe23,
r3,± = e12 + e13 ±
√
2e23
r4,± = ±e24 ∓e34,
r5 = e14,
r6,± = e24 −e34 ±
√
2e14,
r7,α>0 = αe13 + e24,
r8,α>0 = αe12 + αe13 + e24 −e34,
r9 = e12 + e24.
Let us note that these classes agree with the results obtained in Section 2.9.

real four-dimensional indecomposable Lie algebras g. Each Lie algebra is divided into several subsets
enumerated by roman numbers, which classify the orbits of Aut(g) in Yg. The orbits of Autc(g) are

Chapter 4
Foliated Lie systems
Recall that a Lie system is a nonautonomous system of first-order ordinary differential equations (ODEs)
in normal form whose general solution can be written as a function, a so-called superposition rule, of a
family of particular solutions and some constants related to initial conditions [50, 51, 53, 116, 183]. The
Lie–Scheffers theorem [51, 116, 183, 120] states that a Lie system amounts to a t-dependent vector field
taking values in a finite-dimensional Lie algebra of vector fields, called a Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra of
the Lie system. Although most differential equations are not Lie systems [46, 53], geometric properties
of such systems were extensively studied and relevant applications were found both in mathematics and
physics [50, 53, 120, 122, 183], which motivated their further analysis. The theory of Lie systems has
been then extended in different manners to analyse much more general families of systems of differential
equations. PDE Lie systems [51, 140, 148] were applied to the study of conditional symmetries and
Bäcklund transformations [48, 119]. Quasi-Lie schemes and quasi-Lie systems were developed to investi-
gate integrability conditions for systems of ODES and PDEs, e.g. dissipative Milne-Piney equations and
nonlinear oscillators [46, 48, 52]. Superposition rules for discrete differential equations were considered
by Winternitz and his collaborators [145, 149, 154]. Super-superposition rules, which are aimed at the
analysis of general solutions to superdifferential equations, were analysed in [33, 34]. A detailed survey
on the previous and other generalisations of the theory of Lie systems can be found in [120].
In this Chapter, based on [54], we focus on the least studied generalisation of Lie systems, the foliated Lie
systems [50]. We prove that such systems admit an analogue of a superposition rule, the so-called foliated
superposition rule, and we discuss how to obtain such functions. We also discuss that the relation between
standard Lie systems and automorphic Lie systems (see [50, 53, 120, 176, 177] for details) extends to
the realm of foliated Lie systems. This in turn allows us to define a special class of such systems, the
so-called foliated automorphic Lie systems, which is studied in detail. Moreover, we show that foliated Lie
systems provide a framework to describe a new generalisation of Ermakov systems. Finally, we show that
a particular class of F-foliated Lie systems on Lie algebras, related to standard Lie–Hamilton systems,
can be studied Poisson structures induced by r-matrices.
4.1
On the definition of foliated Lie systems
Let us introduce our stratified Lie system notion and illustrate its usefulness with several examples of
physical and mathematical interest.
Definition 4.1.1. A t-dependent vector field X on N is called foliated Lie system on N, if it can be
107

Chapter 4. Foliated Lie systems
written as
X(t, x) =
r

∀t ∈R,

DVX associated with VX is regular and g1, . . . , gr are common t-dependent constants of motion of the
elements of V , i.e. Xαgβ = 0 on N for every α, β = 1, . . . , r. The expression (4.1.1) is referred to as a
decomposition of X and V is a Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra of the foliated Lie system X.
In view of the results by Stefan and Sussmann [108, 165, 166], the regular distribution DV associated
with a Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra V of a stratified Lie system is integrable. Thus, it gives rise to a
regular stratification F of N (also called a foliation, see Section 1.2) such that the tangent spaces to its
strata coincide with DV . For that reason, a foliated Lie system is sometimes called F-foliated Lie system,
if the form of the stratification F is relevant in our considerations.
As the vector fields of V are tangent to the strata of the stratification F, the system X can be restricted
to the strata of F. Since Xβgα = 0 for every α, β = 1, . . . , r, the restrictions of g1, . . . , gr to a stratum
Fλ of F give rise to r functions depending only on t and not on any value x ∈Fλ. Indeed, consider a
smooth curve γ : u ∈[0, 1] ⊂R 7→γ(u) ∈Fλ connecting two points of a stratum Fλ. Then, the tangent
vector to each point γ(u) of the curve γ, let us say ˙γ(u), can be written as a linear combination ˙γ(u) =

Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra VX gives rise to a Lie system XFλ on each stratum Fλ ∈F with a Vessiot–
Guldberg Lie algebra VXFλ defined by the restriction to Fλ of the vector fields V ∈VX. Consequently,
the dimensions of all the induced Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebras on the strata are bounded.
However, given a foliation F of a manifold N, it is not true in general that a family of Lie systems XFλ

t-dependent vector field on R3 of the form

4.2
Foliated superposition rules
Recall that a Lie system is usually defined as a system admitting a superposition rule. However, our
definition of a foliated Lie system does not refer to any such analogous notion and instead, it focuses
on the properties of the decomposition of the system. It is then natural to ask whether one can define
an analogue of a superposition rule that is suitable for foliated Lie systems. This section is devoted to
address that question.
Let us consider again the foliated Lie system (4.1.3) on T ∗Rn. This system was associated with a foliation
FHJ whose leaves FHJ
k
, with k ∈Rn, were given in (4.1.6). Particular solutions to (4.1.3) have the form
(Q(1)(t), P1 = P 0

One can see that the foliated Lie system related to the Lax pair (4.1.11) shares similar properties. This
motivates to introduce the following definition.
Definition 4.2.1. An F-foliated superposition rule depending on m particular solutions for a system X
on a manifold N relative to a foliation F on N is a superposition rule Ψ : N m × N →N for a certain
Lie system on N such that
1. Ψ(Fm+1
k
) ⊂Fk for every leaf Fk of F,
2. every particular solution, x(t), of X containing a point of a leaf Fk of F, namely there exists t0 ∈R
such that x(t0) ∈Fk, takes the form
x(t) = Ψ(x(1)(t), . . . , x(m)(t), λ)
in terms of m generic particular solutions x(1)(t), . . . , x(m)(t) of X contained in Fk and a unique
λ ∈Fk.
We will refer to an F-foliated superposition rule simply as a foliated superposition rule if F is known
from the context or its specific form is irrelevant to our considerations.
It follows from Definition 4.2.1 that if X admits an F-foliated superposition rule, then the particular
solutions of X are always contained in a leaf of F. Moreover, particular solutions of X taking values in
each leaf of F are always described in terms of the same number m of particular solutions within the
same leaf.
Recall that the system X can be restricted to any leaf Fk ⊂F as every vector field Xt := X(t, ·), for
t ∈R, is tangent to the leaves of F. Then, all the solutions of X within Fk can be written as a function
of a generic family of particular solutions of X within Fk. Additionally, the foliated superposition rule
becomes a standard superposition rule for the restriction of X to Fk, which becomes a Lie system.
In view of Definition 4.2.1, the foliated Lie system (4.1.3) admits an FHJ-foliated superposition rule
ΨHJ : T ∗Rn × T ∗Rn →T ∗Rn given by (4.2.2), whereas the leaves of FHJ take the form (4.1.6).

4.3
Foliated Lie–Scheffers theorem
In this section, we study the properties of first-order systems of ODEs admitting a foliated superposition
rule. Our results can be considered as a generalisation of the standard geometric Lie–Scheffers theorem
[51, 116, 183]. As a byproduct, our characterisation gives us an algorithm to obtain foliated superposition
rules.
Let us begin with a well-known fact in Lie systems theory (see [51, 53, 120]).
