As noted in the previous section, the definition of a foliated Lie system does not refer to the notion of
a foliated superposition rule. However, these two concepts are closely related as shown by the following
theorems.
Theorem 4.3.3. If X is an F-foliated Lie system, then it admits an F-foliated superposition rule
Ψ : N m × N →N such that ms ≥dim V, where s is the dimension of the leaves of F.
Proof. Assume that X is an F-foliated Lie system with an associated Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra V
and admitting a decomposition (4.1.1). Let us construct an F-foliated superposition rule for X.
As discussed in Section 4.1, any F-foliated Lie system X gives rise to a Lie system on each leaf Fk of F
by restricting X and its Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra v to Fk. Thus, we obtain a Lie system
Xk(t, x) =

same Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra V as X.
All Lie systems Y(k) admit a common superposition rule Ψ : N m × N →N.
However, it is not
assured in general that Ψ(Fm+1

Hence, the curve Ψ(x(1)(t), . . . , x(m)(t), λ), where the
x(1)(t), . . . , x(m)(t) are particular solutions to X and λ ∈Fk, might not be a solution of Xk and, in
consequence, a particular solution to X. Let us provide a method to obtain a superposition rule for
all the Y(k) such that Ψ(Fm+1

Ψ(x(1)(t), . . . , x(m)(t), λ) will be a particular solution to Y(k) within Fk and, therefore, a particular solu-
tion to X.
On a neighbourhood of a generic point of N, there exists a local coordinate system adapted to the
foliation F with s-dimensional leaves.

Observe that Theorem 4.3.3 gives a procedure to construct a foliated superposition rule. For reference,
let us detail the steps of this method below:
• Consider a Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra VF of an F-foliated Lie system X on an dT -dimensional
manifold T.
• Find the smallest m ∈N so that the diagonal prolongations of the vector fields of VF span a
distribution of rank dim VF at a generic point of T m. This states the number of particular solutions
of the foliated superposition rule, namely m.

the last dT −s coordinates are constant on the leaves of F. Define the same coordinate system

4.4
Foliated automorphic Lie systems
We showed in Section 1.6 that the study of a Lie system can be reduced to that of an automorphic Lie
system. In this section, we prove that the evolution of a stratified Lie system can be reduced to the
integration of a foliated Lie system on a principal bundle of a particular type.
Definition 4.4.1. Let π : G × M →M be a trivial principal bundle with structural r-dimensional Lie
group G acting on G × M by φπ : (g, (h, k)) ∈G × (G × M) 7→(gh, k) ∈G × M. A foliated automorphic
Lie system is a t-dependent vector field XR
π on G × M given by
XR
π =
r
X
α=1
fαXR
α ,
(4.4.1)
where XR
1 , . . . , XR
r form a basis of fundamental vector fields of the action of G on G×M, and f1, . . . , fr ∈
C∞(R × G × M) are t-dependent common constants of motion of XR
1 , . . . , XR
r .
Obviously, the system given by (4.4.1) is a foliated Lie system.
Note that every vector field on G
can be considered in a canonical way as a vector field on G × M via the vector bundle isomorphism
T(G × M) = TG × TM. Then, XR
1 , . . . , XR
r can be understood as right-invariant vector fields on G.
They also span a finite-dimensional Lie algebra of vector fields on G × M and a regular r-dimensional
generalised distribution. In fact, at each point of G×P the values of XR
1 , . . . , XR
r span the vertical space
at such point of the bundle π : G × M →M. Since f1, . . . , fr are common t-dependent constants of
motion for vertical vector fields, they are constant on the fibres of π : G × M →M and they can be
considered, in a unique manner, as t-dependent functions on M.
When M is a point, f1, . . . , fr become functions depending only on t and (4.4.1) turns into a standard
automorphic Lie system [53]. More generally, every trivial principal bundle π : P →M with a structural
group G and fundamental vector fields spanned by X1, . . . , Xr give rise to a t-dependent vector field X
on P of the form
X(t, x) =
r
X
α=1
fα(t, x)Xα(x),
∀x ∈P,
∀t ∈R,
where f1, . . . , fr ∈C∞(R × P) are t-dependent constants of motion of X1, . . . , Xr. The trivialisation of
the principal bundle π : P →M maps diffeomorphically X onto XR
π . Consequently, X is, up to a bundle
diffeomorphism, a foliated automorphic Lie system.
Let us prove that, in analogy with Lie systems, every F-foliated Lie system gives rise to a foliated
automorphic Lie system, whose solutions allow us to obtain the general solution of the foliated Lie
system. Recall that every t-dependent constant of motion for the vertical vector fields of a fibre bundle
can be considered as a t-dependent function on its base manifold in a canonical way.

systems are diffeomorphic, they share the same foliated automorphic Lie system. It is also immediate that
foliated Lie systems related to the same foliated automorphic Lie system do not need to be diffeomorphic
as they may be defined on manifolds of different dimension.
4.5
Applications
Let us use our results of previous sections to study several physical problems. First, we focus on an
extension of the generalised Ermakov system [109]. Then, we analyse the existence of related Hamiltonian
structures, which extends, in a geometric way, some of the results given in [6].
4.5.1
A new class of generalised Ermakov systems
There exists an extensive literature on the so-called Ermakov systems and their generalisations (see
[55, 94, 109, 150, 151, 152] and references therein). We propose here a class of generalised Ermakov
systems that cannot be described through Lie systems but they admit a description in terms of foliated
ones.
The so-called generalised Ermakov systems [151, 152] is a class of systems of differential equations on
R2
0 = {(x, y) : xy ̸= 0} of the form
d2x
dt2 = −ω2(t)x + g(y/x)
yx2
,
d2y
dt2 = −ω2(t)y + f(x/y)
xy2
,
t ∈R,
(4.5.1)

120
Chapter 4. Foliated Lie systems
where f, g : R →R are real functions. The equation introduced by Milne [134] was an example of (4.5.1)
given by setting g(u) = u in one of the equations and ignoring the second one [146]. Meanwhile, the so-
called Ermakov–Pinney system corresponds to the case g(u) = u and f(u) = 0. The latter generalisation,
along with each particular example of (4.5.1), admit a constant of motion, the so-called generalised Lewis
invariant, of the form
I(x, y, ˙x, ˙y) = 1
2 (x ˙y −y ˙x)2 +
Z x/y
[f(u) −u−2g(1/u)]du.
(4.5.2)
It was noted by Ray, Reid, and Goedert [94, 150, 152] that the term ω2(t) can be replaced by much more
general expressions (see [109] and references therein). For instance, there exist generalisations of (4.5.1)
where ω(t) depends on the time-derivatives of x and y [152].
As a new generalisation, we propose the second-order system of differential equations given by
d2x
dt2 = −ω2(t, I)x + g(I, y/x)
x2y
,
d2y
dt2 = −ω2(t, I)y + f(I, x/y)
xy2
,
(x, y) ∈R2
0,
t ∈R,
(4.5.3)
where f, g : R →R are arbitrary non-vanishing functions and I is given by (4.5.2). In the case where f
and g are constants, one recovers the generalised Ermakov system studied in [109].
Consider the system of first-order differential equations on TR2
0 of the form
dx
dt = vx,
dy
dt = vy,
dvx
dt = −ω2(t, I)x + g(I, y/x)

Previous examples justify the following definition.
Definition 4.5.1. A foliated Lie–Hamilton system is a foliated Lie system X admitting a Vessiot–
Guldberg Lie algebra of Hamiltonian vector fields relative to a Poisson structure.
As a final remark, let us briefly detail a new method to obtain Poisson structures on a Lie group G from a
KKS bracket on the dual to its Lie algebra g. This method does not require the use of an ad-equivariant
inner product on g, as needed to turn (4.5.4) into a (foliated) Hamiltonian Lie system via our previous
techniques.
Consider the two-dimensional Lie algebra b2 = ⟨e1, e2⟩such that [e1, e2] = e2. Let {e1, e2} be the dual
basis to {e1, e2}. Note that b2 is a Frobenius Lie algebra [58, 69]. Recall that a Frobenius Lie algebra
over a field k is a Lie algebra (g, [·, ·]g) equipped with a non-degenerate k-bilinear skew-symmetric map
β : g × g →k of the form β(v, w) := f([v, w]g), the so-called Kirillov form, for a certain linear map
f ∈g∗such that β([u, v], w) + β([w, u], v) + β([v, w], u) = 0 for any u, v, w ∈g. In case of b2, the Kirillov
form is given by the element f := e1 + e2. Note that the dual of r := e1 ∧e2 gives rise to the map
β. Such an element r is called a Jordan r-matrix [89]. Moreover, a short calculation shows that any
ad-invariant symmetric form on b2 is degenerate. Consequently, one cannot study the system (4.5.4) for
b2 as a foliated Lie–Hamilton system using the method discussed in this section.
Let B2 be the connected and simply connected Lie group associated with the Lie algebra b2. Then, B2
admits a Poisson bivector XL
1 ∧XL
2 , where XL
1 , XL
2 are left-invariant vector fields on B2 such that their
values at a neutral element of B2 read XL
1 (e) = e1 and XL
2 (e) = e2. In view of [89, Proposition 7] and
the fact that b2 is a Frobenius Lie algebra, there exists a local diffeomorphism ϕf : g ∈B2 7→Ad∗
gf ∈b∗
2
for a certain f ∈b∗
2. Indeed, the coadjoint orbits of B2 in b∗
2 are the integral submanifolds of the KKS
Poisson bracket on b∗
2, which have dimension 0 or 2. Since the KKS bracket on b∗
2 is different from zero,
there exists a two-dimensional coadjoint orbit and thus, such f must exist. Then, ϕf(B2) is an open
subset of b∗
2. By [89, Proposition 7], the map ϕf becomes a Poisson map relative to the KKS bracket on
b∗
2 and the Poisson bracket (with the negative sign) on B2 related to r.

5.1
Deformation of Lie–Hamilton systems
The section recalls a procedure devised in [13] to deform Lie–Hamilton systems via Poisson coalgebras
within the class of Hamiltonian systems. First, we will briefly survey the theory on such systems. Next,
we will detail a simple example.
5.1.1
General deformation procedure
Let us start by a considering the t-dependent system of first-order differential equations on M determined
by a t-dependent vector field on M given by
ds
dt = X(t, s) =
r
X
α=1
bα(t)Xα(s),
∀t ∈R,
∀s ∈M,
(5.1.1)
for certain functions b1(t), . . . , br(t) and vector fields X1, . . . , Xr spanning a Lie algebra V , a so-called
Vessiot–Guldberg Lie algebra, of Hamiltonian vector fields relative to a Poisson structure Λ on M. A sys-
tem of the form (5.1.1) is called a Lie–Hamilton system (see [120] and references therein). Hamiltonian
vector fields relative to a Poisson structure span its characteristic distribution, whose integral subman-
ifolds are symplectic manifolds relative to the restriction of the initial Poisson bracket to the functions
on each leaf [173]. Therefore, (5.1.1) can be restricted to the leaves of such a distribution. On each leaf,
the vector fields X1, . . . , Xr become Hamiltonian and (5.1.1) becomes a Lie–Hamiltonian system relative
to a symplectic form. Hence, the study of Lie–Hamilton systems reduces to the case of Lie–Hamilton
systems relative to symplectic forms. We hereafter assume that (5.1.1) is a Lie–Hamilton system relative
to a symplectic form ω on M.
Let {e1, . . . , er} be a basis of g satisfying the same commutation relations as {h1, . . . , hr}. By simplicity,
we assume V ≃g. By assumption, every Xα admits a Hamiltonian function hα, with α = 1, . . . , r.
Although M := ⟨h1, . . . , hr⟩need not be a Lie algebra relative to the Poisson bracket, it can always
be extended to a finite-dimensional one by adding the successive Poisson brackets of the elements of M
(cf. [56]). Let us assume that M has already been extended by new appropriate functions to close a
finite-dimensional Lie algebra. Let g be the abstract Lie algebra isomorphic to M.
If {e1, . . . , er} is the dual basis to {e1, . . . , er}, we can attach the Lie–Hamilton system (5.1.1) to a

morphism.
Our goal is to get a z-parametrised family of Hamiltonian systems determined by the z-parametrised
family of t-dependent vector fields, {Xz}z∈R, so that the system determined by Xz for z = 0, i.e. X0,
becomes the starting Lie–Hamilton system. We do not require every Xz to be a Lie–Hamilton system
as in (5.1.1). Instead, we will obtain a family of new Hamiltonian systems determined by {Xz}z∈R and
whose properties will be related to the ones of X0 in a manner to be explained next.

If the rank of the distribution is constant, then the distribution is integrable [143].
The functions h1,z, . . . , hr,z can be chosen in many manners. We derive them using a Lie bialgebra struc-

Poisson bracket related to ω. Note that D is the pullback on functions on g∗induced by µ, i.e. D = µ∗.
Let us construct a similar procedure for every z ∈R. Recall that every Poisson manifold is the union of
disjoint submanifolds (possibly of different dimension) given by the integral submanifolds of its charac-
teristic distribution, the so-called strata. Moreover, the initial Poisson manifold reduces to a symplectic
form on each such a stratum [173, 182]. Assume that θ ∈g∗is such that the stratification around it
can be considered as a foliation, i.e. all symplectic submanifolds intersecting an open neighbourhood
of θ have the same dimension. Almost every point of g∗admits this condition (cf. [174]). We also
assume that the Poisson bivectors {Λz}z∈R will additionally induce a foliation of the same codimension
on an open neighbourhood of θ for values of z within an interval I ∋0 in R. Then, one can introduce a
z-parametrised family of local coordinates {ξ1,z, . . . , ξ2n,z, c1,z, . . . , cs,z} on an open neighbourhood U of

5.1.2
On the construction of C∞

An important step in the presented procedure is to obtain a Poisson algebra C∞
z (g∗) for every z ∈I. Let
us describe in detail how to get C∞

Recall that a Poisson–Lie group is a Lie group G equipped with a Poisson structure whose group multi-
plication is a Poisson map. In other words, a Poisson–Lie group is determined by a Poisson bivector ΛG
on G such that [58]
ΛG

Lie bialgebras (g, δ) are diffeomorphic, i.e. if Λ1, Λ2 are the Poisson bivectors of two Poisson–Lie groups
on G related to isomorphic Lie bialgebras on g, then there exists a Lie group diffeomorphism ϕ : G →G
such that ϕ∗Λ1 = Λ2.

bivectors on g∗given by Λz in (5.1.7) are Poisson bivectors. It is immediate that Λ0 = ΛK. Note that
the Poisson bivectors {Λz}z∈R do not need to give rise to Poisson–Lie bivectors on the Lie group G∗.
5.2
On the deformation procedure
This section shows that the deformation procedure presented in the previous section do not lead to equiv-
alent families of z-parametric dynamical systems, i.e. there is no z-parametric family of diffeomorphisms
φz : M →M such that Xz = φz∗X0 for every z ∈R and Xz is the Hamiltonian vector field determined
by the t-dependent Hamiltonian function hz and Λz. In spite of that, the systems {Xz}z∈R are related
among themselves through a Poisson algebra structure in such a way that certain properties of their
spaces of solutions can be studied simultaneously by means of our techniques, which will allow us to
define a new class of Hamiltonian systems.
Definition 5.2.1. Two deformations Xz and X′
z on a manifold M of a Lie–Hamilton system X are called
equivalent if there exists a z-parametric family of diffeomorphisms {ϕz : Uz ⊂M →M}z∈R such that
X′
z = ϕz∗Xz for every z ∈R and a family of open, not-empty, subsets Uz ⊂M.
The process of obtaining a deformation of a Lie–Hamilton system described previously depends on the
point of a symplectic leaf in g∗.
Recall that the z-parametric family of Poisson structures {·, ·}z allows us to define a family of Hamiltonian
systems on M. This is done by defining Xt,z to be the Hamiltonian vector field related to ht,z for every
t, z ∈R.

5.3
Deformation procedure for Jacobi–Lie systems
The deformation procedure described in the previous sections can be accomplished when the initial non-
deformed system is Hamiltonian relative to a Poisson structure.
Nevertheless, there are t-dependent
systems of first-order ordinary differential equations that cannot be considered as t-dependent Hamilto-
nian relative to any Poisson structure [49]. Among t-dependent vector fields that cannot be considered
as Hamiltonian systems relative to a Poisson structure, we will here highlight certain subfamilies of the
so-called locally automorphic Lie systems [96], whose definition is given next.
Definition 5.3.1. A locally automorphic Lie system X on a manifold M is a Lie system whose minimal
Lie algebra V X satisfies that dim V X = dim M and whose associated distribution, DV X, is equal to TM.
Proposition 5.3.2 to be given next shows a class of locally automorphic Lie systems that are not Hamil-
tonian relative to any Poisson structure. It is worth noting that Proposition 5.3.2 can be considered as
an application of Proposition 5.2 in [49] to a relevant class of Lie systems.
Proposition 5.3.2. Every locally automorphic Lie system X on an odd-dimensional manifold M is not
Hamiltonian relative to any Poisson bivector Λ on M.

Chapter 5. Deformation of Lie–Hamilton systems via Jacobi structures
133
Proof. Let us proceed by reductio ad absurdum. The characteristic distribution of Λ on M is a generalised
distribution whose rank is even, but not necessarily constant, at every point of M [182]. Hence, all
Hamiltonian vector fields on M related to Λ take values in a generalised distribution that has even rank
at every point. Meanwhile, the vector fields of the minimal Lie algebra, V X, associated with X span
TM. If all the vector fields of V X are Hamiltonian, then the unique generalised distribution containing
all of them is TM, which is odd-dimensional. But then, the vector fields of V X cannot be contained
in a generalised distribution of even rank at every point of M. This is a contradiction and the locally
automorphic Lie system X is not Hamiltonian relative to any Poisson structure Λ on M.
Let us provide a Poisson algebra deformation of the Schwarz equation through a Jacobi structure. Since
the so-called Schwarz equation [37] cannot be considered as a Hamiltonian system relative to a Poisson
structure [49], the standard method in [13] cannot therefore be applied and an extension of that method
via Jacobi structures is fully justified. The procedure to be applied next will be clarified in the following
sections, and its generalisation to a quite general class of Jacobi-Lie systems will be then provided.
The Schwarz equation [37, 38, 47, 49] reads

to Schwarz derivative and its relation to other relevant differential equations appearing in physics [37,
110, 144].
It can also be considered as a particular kind of third-order Kummer-Schwarz equations.
As such, its generalisations or deformations can be of use in the study of generalisations of third-order
Kummer–Schwarz equations (see [120] and references therein).
The Schwarz equation can be rewritten by adding the new variables a = dv/dt, v = dx/dt as a t-dependent

which does not need to satisfy the Leibniz rule [178]. The Hamiltonian functions of the Ni are first-
integrals of RO, i.e.
ROhi = LS2(ιNiη2) = ιNiLS2η2 + ι[S2,Ni]η2 = ιNiιS2dη2 + ιNidιS2η2 = 0,
i = 1, 2, 3.
This makes h1, h2, h3 to have a series of nice properties, which justifies calling them good Hamiltonian
functions [100]. Further, {hi, hj}ΛO,RO = Λ(dhi, dhj), for i = 1, 2, 3, and, more specifically,
{h1, h2}ΛO,RO = h1,
{h2, h3}ΛO,RO = h3,
{h1, h3}ΛO,RO = 2h2.
(5.3.3)
Let us find the coordinates (ξ, u1, u2) in which the Reeb vector field RO = −x∂x −v∂v −a∂a takes the
form RO = ∂ξ. The equations ROu1 = 0, ROu2 = 0 have a particular solution u1 = a
v, u2 = x
v on O,
which allows us to write
h1 = 2u2,
h2 = −1 + u1u2,
h3 = −u1 + u2u2

2

Therefore, hi = hi(u1, u2) for i = 1, 2, 3.
Since {u1, u2}ΛO,RO = −1, the space OH = {f(u1, u2) :
f ∈C∞(R2)} becomes a Poisson algebra relative to the Poisson bracket {·, ·}ΛO,RO and u1, u2 are the
corresponding canonical coordinates. Indeed, RO|OH = 0 implies, by Lemma 4.4 in [100], that OH is
a Poisson algebra relative to the restriction of ΛΛO,RO to OH. This restriction allows us to deform the
Hamiltonian functions h1, h2, h3 via the procedure described in the previous section.

{v1, v2}z = −shc(2zv1)v1,
{v1, v3}z = −2v2,
{v2, v3}z = −ch(2zv1)v3
relative to a certain z ∈R.

Moreover, lim

Chapter 6
Conclusions and Outlook
Both methods devised in this thesis to address the classification problem for Lie bialgebras have proven
efficient in computations of inequivalent r-matrices for three-dimensional and four-dimensional indecom-
posable Lie algebras. In the former case, the results agreed with already known works on this problem
[84, 95]. From the practical standpoint, these methods allow for noticeably faster determination of equiv-
alent classes of r-matrices, especially by using the first approach. The main advantage in this aspect
comes from the fact that the analysis of invariant forms is a relatively simple linear problem. The second
method, although not as straightforward, offers additional geometric perspectives on the classification
issue.
Despite these advantages, there several difficulties related to both approaches.
As already hinted in
Section 2.9, the analysis of the orbits of the action of Inn(g) gets complicated when there are more than
one invariant form admissible for the considered Lie algebra g. In this case, an appropriate coordinate
system was necessary to proceed. Similarly, the study of the modified Yang–Baxter equation is practically
impossible unless suitable coordinates are employed. Additionally, the determination of Darboux families
is a nontrivial task that requires careful examination of Lie algebra derivations. Another difficulty is the
identification of the orbits (either of the action of Inn(g) in the first method, or of Autc(g) in the second
one) relative to the whole automorphism group. The structure of such group is already complicated for
dim(g) = 4 and direct computations, even though supported by software, might be more intricate when
dim(g) > 4. Such problems might be a significant hindrance to an efficient study of higher-dimensional
Lie algebras.
However, the considerations in Section 2.9 suggest a few directions to possibly overcome some of these
troubles. In this example, we dealt with the three-parameter family of invariant forms bΛ2gl2 on gl2 or
in other words, an invariant form that was a linear span of three separate ones. Its analysis was greatly
simplified by using the coordinates in which bΛ2gl2 gets an especially manageable form, as the first two
forms are diagonal. Thus, the problem splits into two subspaces of smaller dimension than the initial
one, given accordingly by the coordinates of each of these forms, that are additionally interconnected by
the quadratic function associated with the third form. In consequence, we could easily analyse the orbits
on these subspaces and in the last step, we imposed the condition given by the remaining form. It would
be interesting to verify whether an analogous procedure yields results efficiently in higher-dimensional
cases.
Apart from the concept of a brick, we have not established any techniques for the determination of
Darboux families that would significantly facilitate their search. Much work in this thesis related to
this problem has been solely based on an inspection of Lie algebra derivations.
Thus, an important
137

138
Chapter 6. Conclusions and Outlook
improvement of the method would rely on finding such techniques.
Let us also notice that, contrary to the first method, the structure of a Lie algebra seems to play no
role in the second approach. Similarly, the structure of Lie algebra of its derivations is not relevant in
subsequent computations. This issue opens another direction in the study of Darboux families and their
geometry.
One technical question that remains to be tackled concerns the generalisation of both methods to Lie
algebras over other fields than the reals. In particular, special attention should be paid to the complex
case. It would be interesting to investigate whether there exists any relation between the solutions for a
complex Lie algebra and the solutions for its real forms.
Finally, one might attempt to generalise both methods to classify general cocommutators.
In the second part of this thesis, we focused on applications of Lie bialgebras. In this regard, we have
discussed the notion of foliated Lie systems, which significantly extend the mostly theoretical examples
given in [50]. We have introduced foliated superposition rules and first-order systems of ODEs admitting
such a superposition rule have been studied. Moreover, we have defined foliated automorphic Lie systems
and their relations to foliated Lie systems have been investigated.
The theory of foliated Lie systems has been applied to study to a generalisation of Ermakov systems,
automorphic Lie systems related to Lax pairs and certain Hamiltonian systems. In particular, the theory
of Lie–Hamilton systems has been extended to foliated Lie-Hamilton systems.
We expect that our results can be extended to the so-called PDE Lie systems [51, 53, 140, 148]. This
should be accomplished by using the same fundamental ideas as presented in Chapter 5, but the devel-
opment is technically much more complicated due to the nature of PDEs.
Several foliated Lie systems studied in the applications of this work are concerned with Hamiltonian
systems admitting a maximal number of functionally independent autonomous constants of motion in
involution relative to the Poisson bracket induced by the associated symplectic structure. Such systems
can be understood as a t-dependent analogue of completely integrable Hamiltonian systems (see [92, 157,
158] for related notions). We aim to apply our methods as well as their possible generalisations to the
analysis of t-dependent integrable non-commutative systems (see [85, 118, 129, 167]).
Finally, we have sketched the generalisation of the deformation procedure for Lie–Hamilton systems
[13, 15] in terms of Jacobi structures. In particular, we have studied the Schwarz equation that cannot
be considered as a Hamiltonian system relative to a Poisson structure.
Following [15], the more geometric approach to this generalised method needs to be introduced. It is
also necessary to analyse further examples. As indicated in Section 5.3, locally automorphic Lie systems
might be of particular interest for that purpose.

ijek of the basis elements e[i] and e[j]

Com[i_, j_] := {
A = Table[a[l], {l, 1, Dim}];
temp = {e[i] . e[j] - e[j] . e[i] == Sum[e[k] a[k], {k, 1, Dim}]};
sol = Solve[Flatten[temp], Flatten[A]];

Deriv
This command gives the derivations of a Lie algebra g, i.e. linear maps D ∈End(g) such that
D([v, w]) = [D(v), w] + [v, D(w)]. The result is presented as a matrix with Dim rows and Dim columns,
where i-th row corresponds to D(ei).
Deriv := {
Der = Table[der[i, j], {i, 1, Dim}, {j, 1, Dim}];
temp = Table[Sum[Der[[l, k]] Str[[i, j, k]] - Der[[k, i]] Str[[k, j, l]]
- Str[[i, k, l]] Der[[k, j]], {k, 1, Dim}] == 0,
{i, 1, Dim - 1}, {j, i + 1, Dim}, {l, 1, Dim}];
sol = Flatten[Solve[Flatten[temp], Flatten[Der]]];
Der /. sol}
Id
This command constructs the identity matrix of dimension given by the parameter nMatrix.
Id := Table[KroneckerDelta[i, j], {i, 1, nMatrix}, {j, 1, nMatrix}]

+ KroneckerProduct[e[k], e[i], e[j]] + KroneckerProduct[e[j], e[k], e[i]]

AllAutN := {
autmatrix = Table[T[a, b], {a, 1, Dim}, {b, 1, Dim}];
temp = Table[Sum[T[p, i]*T[q, j]*Com[p, q][[k]], {p, 1, Dim}, {q, 1, Dim}]
== Sum[Com[i, j][[l]]*T[k, l], {l, 1, Dim}],
{i, 1, Dim - 1}, {j, i + 1, Dim}, {k, 1, Dim}];

k = 1;
For[i = 1, i < Length[solutions] + 1, i++,
autsol = autmatrix /. solutions[[i]];
If[Det[autsol] === 0, Null,
Print["Automorphism matrix no ", k];
Print[Simplify[MatrixForm[autsol]]];
Print["Determinant: "];

Print["Matrix of a automorphism no ", k, " lifted to bivector space:"];
tempmatrix = {};
For[p = 1, p <= Dim - 1, p++,
For[q = p + 1, q <= Dim, q++,
AppendTo[tempmatrix, GLiftMor2N[autsol, p, q]]]];
Print[MatrixForm[Simplify[tempmatrix]]];
k++]]}

Appendix A. Commands for Mathematica

Appendix B
Matrix representation for Lie algebras
with nontrival center
If a Lie algebra g has a nontrivial center, then the image of the map ad : g ∋v 7→[v, ·] ∈gl(g) is a
matrix Lie algebra that is not isomorphic to g. Hence, the image of ad does not give a matrix algebra
representation of g, which may give rise to a problem as matrix Lie algebra representation are quite
practical in computations [17]. The aim of this section is to provide a method to obtain a matrix Lie
algebra isomorphic to g when its center, Z(g), is not trivial, namely Z(g) ̸= 0, and g satisfy some
additional conditions. Our method is to be employed in the rest of our work during calculations.
Let {e1, . . . , es, es+1, . . . , en} be a basis of g such that {e1, . . . , es} form a basis for Z(g). Let ck
ij, with
i, j, k = 1, . . . , n, be the structure constants of g in the given basis, i.e. [ei, ej] = Pn
k=1 ck
ijek for i, j =
1, . . . , n. Define then ˜g := ⟨e1, . . . , en, e⟩. Let us determine the conditions ensuring that ˜g is a Lie algebra
relative to a Lie bracket, [·, ·]¯g, that is an extension of the Lie bracket on g, i.e. [e′, e′′]¯g = [e′, e′′]g for
every e′, e′′ ∈g, and [e, ei]¯g = αiei for i = 1, . . . , n so that
α1 · α2 · . . . · αs ̸= 0.
(B.0.1)
The meaning of the last condition will be clear in a while. If [·, ·]¯g is to be a Lie bracket, its Jacobi
identity leads to the following conditions
[e, [ei, ej]¯g]¯g = [[e, ei]¯g, ej]¯g + [ei, [e, ej]¯g]¯g ⇐⇒
n
X
k=1
αkck
ijek =
n
X
k=1
(αi + αj)ck
ijek,
∀i, j = 1, . . . , n,
[e, [e, ej]¯g]¯g = [[e, e]¯g, ej]¯g + [e, [e, ej]¯g]¯g = [e, [e, ej]¯g]¯g,
∀j = 1, . . . , n.
(B.0.2)
Thus, previous conditions can be reduced to requiring that, for those indices i, j, k satisfying ck
ij ̸= 0, one
gets
αi + αj = αk.
If the latter condition is satisfied, a new Lie algebra ˜g arises. Note that g is an ideal of ˜g. This leads to a
Lie algebra morphism R : v ∈g 7→Rv := [v, ·]˜g ∈gl(˜g). If Rv = 0, then [v, ei]˜g = 0 for i = 1, . . . , n, which
means that v ∈Z(g). Thus, v = Ps
i=1 λiei for certain constants λ1, . . . , λs and 0 = [e, v]˜g = Ps
i=1 λiαiei.
Then, condition (B.0.1) yields λ1 = . . . = λs = 0 and it turns out that v = 0. Hence, the elements Rv,
with v ∈g, span a matrix Lie algebra of endomorphisms on ˜g isomorphic to g.
It is clear that conditions (B.0.1) and (B.0.2) do not need to be satisfied for a general Lie algebra g with a
nontrivial center. Nevertheless, we used in this work that this method works for all indecomposable real

Appendix B. Matrix representation for Lie algebras with nontrival center
four-dimensional Lie algebras with non-trivial center (see [170] for a complete list of such Lie algebras).
This will be enough for our purposes.
Let us illustrate our method. Consider the Lie algebra s1 = ⟨e1, e2, e3, e4⟩with non-vanishing commuta-
tion relations [e4, e2] = e1, [e4, e3] = e3. Let us construct a new Lie algebra ˜g = ⟨e1, e2, e3, e4, e⟩following
(B.0.1) and (B.0.2). This gives rise to the following system of equations:
α4 + α2 = α1,
α4 + α3 = α3,
α1 ̸= 0.
We have that Z(s1) = ⟨e1⟩. The previous system, under the corresponding restriction (B.0.1), has a
solution α4 = 0, α3 ∈R, α2 = α1, α1 ∈R\{0}. In particular, set α2 = α1 = 1 and α3 = 0. Thus, the
endomorphisms Rei on ˜g read

As desired, the above matrices have the same non-vanishing commutation relations as s1, i.e.
[Re4, Re2] = Re1,
[Re4, Re3] = Re3.
Let us prove that not every Lie algebra g with non-trivial center admits an extended Lie algebra ˜g of the
form required by our method. Consider the Lie algebra s6,231 = ⟨e1, . . . , e6⟩with nonzero commutation
relations (see [170] for details):
[e2, e3] = e1,
[e5, e1] = e1,
[e5, e2] = e2,
[e6, e1] = e1,
[e6, e3] = e3,
[e6, e5] = e4.
Thus, Z(s4,231) = ⟨e4⟩. The corresponding system of equations (B.0.2) reads
α2 + α3 = α1,
α5 + α1 = α1,
α5 + α2 = α2,
α6 + α1 = α1,
α6 + α3 = α3,
α6 + α5 = α4.
Then, α5 = 0, α6 = 0 and α4 = α5 = 0, which contradicts the assumption of our method (B.0.2), namely
α4 ̸= 0.
Consider the 7-dimensional Lie algebra g := ⟨e1, . . . , e7⟩with Z(g) = ⟨e7⟩and nonzero commutation
relations (cf. [161, p. 492, case 7I])
[e1, e2] = e3,
[e1, e3] = e4,
[e1, e4] = e5,
[e1, e6] = e7,
[e2, e3] = e6,
[e2, e4] = e7,
[e2, e5] = e7,
[e2, e6] = e7,
[e3, e4] = −e7.
The corresponding system (B.0.2) reads
(i)
α1 + α2 = α3,
(ii)
α1 + α3 = α4,
(iii)
α1 + α4 = α5,
(iv)
α1 + α6 = α7,
(v)
α2 + α3 = α6,
(vi)
α2 + α4 = α7,
(vii)
α2 + α5 = α7,
(viii)
α2 + α6 = α7,
(ix)
α3 + α4 = α7.
From (vii) and (viii), we get α5 = α6; from (vi) and (ix), we get α2 = α3; and from (iv) and (viii), we get
α1 = α2. Thus, α1 = α2 = α3. From (i), it follows that α1 = 0. Thus, α2 = α3 = 0. From (ii), we get
α4 = 0 and (iii) yields α5 = 0. From (iv), one gets α6 = α7. From (ix), we get α7 = 0, and thus α6 = 0.
Therefore, α1 = . . . = α7 = 0 is the only possibility, but it does not satisfy (B.0.1).
